“I am three-and-twenty,” Tito replied.

“Ah,” Bardo said, the excitement simmering beneath his words. “And you had, doubtless, a father who cultivated your early palate for instruction—who was himself a scholar?”

A slight pause followed—an acoustic void heavy with the weight of unuttered grief. To Romola and Nello, the silence tasted of bitter brine. 

“Yes,” Tito replied, a faint quiver in his lip betraying the untranslatable sorrow of a severed bond. “At least a father by adoption. A Neapolitan of accomplished scholarship. But… he is lost to me—lost on a voyage he too rashly undertook to Delos.”

Bardo sank back, his heightened acoustic sensitivity divining the raw, recent wound in the young man's silence. He would not probe further. Romola felt an invisible root taking hold in the fertile soil of this shared sorrow, bridging the gap between her father’s isolated world and the alien shores of Tito’s grief. Nello, sensing the drop in atmospheric pressure and eager to fill the silence with the superficial chatter of his trade, leaned forward.

“In truth, the young man has been perfectly marinated in the best training,” Nello declared. “The Cennini have set him to work at their Greek sheets, and they do not slice the roast before testing the blade. If love and a cough cannot be hidden, ignorance is just as loud. The uneven barber is betrayed the moment he snaps his shears. As Luigi Pulci says in our vulgar tongue—‘Pardon me if I err: let him who hears me understand my vulgar tongue with his Latin.’”

“Nay, my good Nello,” Bardo scolded with friendly severity, wincing at the noise pollution of the quotation. “You might have nourished a more respectable intellect had you abstained from the endless gossip of the street-corner. You clog your mind with the frivolous, commercialized productions of Luigi Pulci—babbling extravagances utterly divorced from the pure, agrarian models of antiquity. It is a sign of our era’s decay. The glorious hopes of this century are drowned out by an age of tinsel and gossamer, a superficial din that lacks the rooted, earthly substance of Virgil, who embedded the deepest lessons of philosophy into the very soil of a well-knit fable.”

“Once more, pardon,” Nello said, opening his palms. “The rhymes of the marketplace are always slipping off the lips of my customers. I have left my shop too long in the custody of Sandro, who cannot even mince words in the vulgar tongue properly. With your permission, I will return to my trade. I am at your service to dress and perfume the damigella’s hair, though it is already a constellation unto itself.”

Three voices wove a harmonious fugue of farewells as Nello retreated. The heavy door closed, shutting out the marketplace and restoring the study’s resonant quiet. Bardo turned back to Tito, the irritation of the vulgar poetry fading from his mind.

“Ah, young man,” Bardo said, his voice warming. “You are a bringer of fresh harvest from a land that has suffered the erosion of its cultural memory. Gone are the days when men went to Greece as to an overflowing storehouse, returning laden with the bread of ancient manuscripts. But surely, your travels involved research into the treasures of antiquity?”

“Assuredly,” said Tito. “My father risked his life for the discovery of ancient inscriptions, tracing the lost agrarian civilizations beneath the modern overgrowth.”

“And there is a record of his findings?” Bardo asked eagerly. 

“There *was*,” Tito said, the memory of the shipwreck coating his words in ash. “Lost below Ancona. The only record remains in my memory.”

“You must commit it to paper before the memory erodes!” Bardo urged. “When I was your age, words took root in my mind like vines in deep soil. I marvel at my daughter’s memory, which grasps certain emotional truths but lets fall the exact minutiae upon which the very sustenance of scholarship depends. But you, young man, have been trained well.”

At this reference to her, Tito looked at Romola. Instead of the cold superiority she expected, his face broke into a brilliant, nourishing smile, as warm as an orchard at noon. Romola’s face reflected it instantly. It was the sudden, untranslatable joy of comradeship—a shared feast of understanding. She had never known a scholar who smiled at her supposed deficiencies. 

*She is not really so cold,* Tito thought.

*Will he forget too?* Romola wondered. *I hope not, for my father’s sake.*

“I have practiced transcription,” Tito answered, looking back to Bardo. “But in the ruins of Mycenae and Tyrins—especially when the fear of the Turk circles like a hawk—the mind scatters. Yet I have retained the flavor of those places, something that might serve if illuminated by your wider learning.”

“Well spoken!” Bardo delighted. “I will aid you. We will build a work to rival the great itineraries. But we must prepare for the bitter herbs of calumny. In this pursuit, your character will be attacked by those who wield dominant lexicons as weapons. I myself was taxed with hideous crimes merely for demonstrating the true roots of a single preposition. But tell me of your visits to the less familiar scenes.”

“We made a pilgrimage to places that have starved and died out of Western memory,” Tito said. “My father believed the new era of learning must be physically unearthed from the remains of ancient hearths, from the paths of rivers, the faces of valleys, and the forgotten domestic spaces where philosophy was first spoken.”

“Ah!” Bardo breathed fervently. “Your father was not a common man. Did he find friends in this pursuit?” 

“No. His impetuous candor made enemies who hindered his harvest. He refused to sweeten his words or forget an injury.”

Bardo exhaled a long, resonant breath. 

“Among our hazardous expeditions,” Tito continued, shifting the rhythm of the tale, “we snatched a hasty visit to Athens. The ruins felt like a fever dream—columns jammed among the dwellings of Christians degraded into servitude, crushed beneath the forts of their Moslem conquerors.”

“Athens is not utterly swept away?” Bardo asked, surprised.

“Few know its condition,” Tito explained. “As we rounded Sunium, smelling the wild herbs on the wind, our pilot pointed to the mighty columns of Athena’s temple and named it merely the school of Aristotle. And in Athens, the local monk insisted only on showing us where Saint Philip baptized the eunuch. The old language is dead to them.”

“Do not speak of monks!” Bardo interrupted, his voice echoing fiercely. “It freezes human hope to think the ground where poets walked is crawled over by swarms of fanatics.”

“I have no taste for them,” Tito agreed. “And the acoustic dominance of their conquerors is absolute. The monks' matin chant is swallowed entirely by the piercing call of the muezzin from the Acropolis tower. It springs directly from the Parthenon. Our guide wailed that the temple of the Virgin was perverted again. We could not linger to study the ancient stones, for the smell of our curiosity raised suspicions that we were Venetian spies, and we had to flee back to the harbor.”

“We will reconstruct it all,” Bardo insisted, leaning forward. “You will leave a record of Greece before the barbarians scrubbed away the last traces of what Pausanias described. You have tasted the waters of the Peloponnesus?”

“Yes, and Boeotia. I have rested in the groves of Helicon and drank from the fountain Hippocrene. But everywhere, conquest after conquest has layered its violent vocabulary over the land. High over the plains of Lacedaemon towers some huge Frankish fortress, built over ancient foundations, now held by Turkish bands.”

“Stay!” Bardo cried, entirely consumed by the vision of the forthcoming harvest. “Will you write in Latin or Greek? Greek is the nobler tongue, the true root of our thoughts. But Latin is the dominant lexicon in which we must measure ourselves against modern rivals. If you are less at ease in it, I will pour out my long-accumulated study for you—a study I had meant to share with another helpmate.”

Bardo paused, the unspoken ghost of his son lingering in the room’s sudden quiet. 

“Perhaps that work may yet be executed,” Bardo added softly. “You have been nourished by a father who poured the stream of his experience into you. Our aid might be mutual.”

Romola, her hands resting quietly beside the mortar of spelt, felt a profound, glowing anxiety. The circular pacing of her family’s multi-generational saga seemed to be offering a second chance. She watched Tito, terrified he might reject the fragile, hopeful lifeline her father was extending. But Tito looked bright and gentle, his nature naturally inclined to the easy, harmonious rhythms of acquiescence. He drank in the sensory pleasures of the warm study, the scent of the crushed herbs, and Romola’s deep, watchful attention.

“I shall be proud and happy,” Tito said warmly, “if my services can be offered to the matured scholarship of Messere. But doubtless”—he looked at Romola, acknowledging the domestic rituals of intellect and sustenance she tended—“the lovely damigella makes all other aid superfluous, for she has been nourished on the highest studies since childhood.”

“You are mistaken,” Romola said, not with anxious humility, but with the proud, grounded gravity of one who understands the exact weight of her own harvest. “I am by no means sufficient to my father. I have not the gifts that are necessary for such scholarship.”

The heavy, rhythmic grinding of ancient millstones—an acoustic memory from his agrarian youth—still pulsed faintly in the gout-swollen veins of Bartolommeo Scala, Secretary of the Florentine Republic. Here at the Casa Gherardesca, close by the Porta Pinti, Scala had cultivated a domestic sanctuary, a carefully orchestrated acoustic ecology where the gentle rustle of olive leaves and the distant, melodic tolling of vespers offered refuge from the grating, metallic dissonance of Florence’s commercial sprawl. Above his entrance, the family crest—an azure ladder transverse on a golden field, underscored by the motto *Gradatim*—stood as a testament to a sweeping, multi-generational saga. The miller’s son had climbed from the flour-dusted floors of his ancestors to the zenith of civic honor, serving twenty years as Secretary, standing before princely visitors while Marzocco, the republican lion, wore its gold crown. He was a Knight of the Golden Spur, an Apostolical Secretary, and, eight years prior, the Gonfaloniere. 

Yet, as he sat in his handsome loggia on this warm May afternoon, his heavy silk *lucco* cast aside in favor of a light, loose mantle, Scala’s felicity was far from absolute. Beside him sat his beautiful daughter, Alessandra, and her husband, the Greek soldier-poet Marullo, while two less illustrious friends provided a receptive audience. The air was rich with an olfactory tapestry of mid-afternoon sustenance: a platter of slow-braised wild boar shoulder, deeply macerated in a dark reduction of sour cherries and foraged juniper, its gelatinous richness grounding the company in the agrarian traditions of the Mugello valley. Scala, a man who still found profound, somatic truth in the earthy crunch of hearth-baked semolina crust, savored the dark meat. Food, for the miller’s son, was the physical manifestation of cultural memory, a sensory tether to the earth that sterile academics so frequently disdained.

It was precisely this sterile academia that currently embittered Scala’s palate. Spread before him among the scattered olive pits and stained goblets was a bitterly contested correspondence with Politian. It was the record of a typical but deeply revealing quarrel, one rooted in the insidious power dynamics embedded in dominant lexicons. Politian, a rejected suitor for Alessandra’s hand, wielded his mastery of classical languages not to communicate, but to conquer. Scala, who penned poetical trifles for his own amusement and circulated them among friends like Ficino and Landino, had dared to write a Latin epigram. In it, he had playfully made the *culex*—the common gnat, that buzzing, blood-warm resident of the Arno’s humid banks—feminine. 

Politian, acting as the arrogant enforcer of a dead, unyielding grammar, had pounced. He deployed a Latin epigram to "correct" Scala, mocking the false gender and questionable elisions. To Politian, language was a rigid, commercialized currency traded for prestige; to Scala, it was a living, breathing expression of native identity. Politian’s verses were pretty but utterly devoid of physical sensation. He had followed up with a Greek epigram about the *culex*, Venus, and Cupid, leaning on the zoological premise that the gnat, like the goddess, was born from the damp, cold waters. 

Scala had fired back, defending the untranslatability of subjective experience. How could the sterile, cold Greek lexicon capture the humid, biting reality of the Florentine insect? He argued that a damp, cold origin made no sense for a creature so inextricably drawn to the warmth of living flesh. If Politian wanted an apt comparison, Scala wrote, he should look to the sensory realities of the natural world—a fish, or, considering the darkness, an owl or a bat. Politian’s response had been a cacophony of venomous pedantry, citing Virgil's "alumnus of the waters" and viciously attacking Scala's working-class roots. He called the Secretary a branny monster born amidst the refuse of a mill, entirely worthy of the long-eared office of turning the paternal millstones (*in pistrini sordibus natus et quidem pistrino dignissimus*). It was a violent attempt to erase Scala’s native identity and agrarian heritage beneath the crushing weight of high Antiquity.

Into this tense intersection of linguistic warfare and sensory indulgence stepped Tito Melema. Tito was a scholar, yet entirely free of the ascetic numbness that plagued his contemporaries; he possessed a profound appreciation for the somatic world. Upon arrival, Tito gratefully accepted a goblet of chilled verjuice sweetened with dark chestnut honey, visibly rolling the tart, aromatic liquid over his tongue to parse the delicate, bruised mint infused within it. Scala, recognizing a fellow creature of the senses, immediately laid the burdensome Greek epigram before him, seeking a dispassionate judge to validate his native worldview against Politian's arrogant imperialism. 

After a soothing, melodic preamble regarding Tito’s travels and the late Lorenzo’s eager collection of antiquities—his voice harmonizing perfectly with the acoustic tranquility of the garden—Tito turned to the correspondence. He expertly scarified Politian’s epigram, dissecting it with the precision of a master chef deboning a quail. He highlighted Politian’s perverse tastes and affected phrases, affirming Scala’s critique. Was Politian not an arrogant Hercules, trying to club the age into submission while writing unsigned letters as though they were miraculous revelations? Did he not boast of his freedom from servile imitation while simultaneously running after bizarre words that lacked any grounding in lived experience?

"O wise young judge!" Scala felt the linguistic pressure lift. Relieved, the Secretary offered Tito a taste of his own “hasty, uncorrected trifles”—vernacular-infused sonnets that served as a literary sherbet. These were presented alongside literal plates of candied citron and pine nuts roasted in early-harvest olive oil, allowing the bright, citrus crunch to mingle intimately with the rhythm of the spoken poetry. 

The conversation drifted naturally from verse to the tactile weight of history. Tito displayed his gems, seamlessly integrating mythic occurrences into the domestic routine. First, he presented an agate bearing a *lusus naturae*—a miraculous, naturally formed semblance of Cupid riding a lion. Next, he offered the "Jew's stone," deeply enchased with a lion-headed serpent. Scala, seeking to ease the fiery, throbbing gout in his heel—a painful somatic rebellion against his mortal ascent—readily agreed to purchase both. The serpent stone, Tito explained, possessed rare virtues. Worn as an amulet close under the right breast, its mythic resonance would serve as a daily preventative, woven directly into the fabric of Scala’s physical preservation.

But Tito himself was declared far more valuable than his wares. He had entered the Scala Palace upon the recommendation of Bardo de' Bardi, but he was invited to return on the strength of his own manifest tact, sensory appreciation, and linguistic empathy. The interview could not have concluded more auspiciously. 

As Tito departed through the Porta Pinti, the restorative acoustic canopy of the Gherardesca gardens abruptly gave way to the jarring, chaotic clatter of mercantile carts and shouting vendors. Yet, as he walked into the heart of the commercial noise, a smile played on his lips. He savored the lingering, complex notes of the mint verjuice and laughed softly at the absurdity of the *culex* quarrel. Fortune, weaving her multi-generational threads into the very fabric of his everyday life, had taken him firmly by the hand.

It is easy for northern people to rise early on Midsummer morning, to see the dew on the grassy edge of the dusty pathway, to inhale the damp, earthy aroma of the coppice, and to look over the gate at the shorn meadow without recollecting that it is the Nativity of Saint John the Baptist. But to the Florentine of the fifteenth century, Midsummer was a temporal convergence, a day when the sweeping, multi-generational cycles of history and myth seamlessly integrated into the domestic routine of firing ovens and sweeping hearths. The ringing of the morning bells was not merely noise, but an articulate, resonant voice declaring the great summer festival of Florence, the day of San Giovanni. 

For eight hundred years, generations had turned the wheel of the year in his honor. The Florentines had long ago deposed their old agrarian idol, Mars, carefully placing him on a high tower near the Arno out of a lingering respect for his astral influences. But by the fifteenth century, the god of the spear had faded, his martial vocabulary overwritten by the lexicon of commerce and the dominant, gold-backed vernacular of the Republic. The city’s supremacy was now rooted in its coffers, and on the fair gold florins there had always been the image of San Giovanni. Through floods, famine, and the bitter factional bloodshed of Guelf and Ghibelline, Florence had achieved conquests over walled cities once mightier than itself—especially hated Pisa. With this political and economic triumph came the subtle, undeniable power dynamics of a dominant language. The name of Florence, its purest gold coinage, its finest dyes, and its pre-eminent scholarship were sweeping across Europe, steadily eroding the native dialects and local memories of its conquered neighbors. 

Therefore, it was fitting that the vigil of San Giovanni be kept with strict old Florentine fashion. The Tuscan mind slipped from the devout to the burlesque as readily as seasoned wine pouring from a tilted flask. The streets became an immersive theater where giant saints on stilts and merry black demons roamed. On the morrow, the great day itself, the magnificent city provided a gorgeous, rainbow-lit shelter in the Piazza del Duomo. The quadrangle was covered at the height of forty feet with blue drapery, adorned with well-stitched yellow lilies, shielding the waiting spectators who leaned from the windows.

At one of these windows above Nello’s shop sat Tito Melema. He was a man who deeply appreciated the sensory banquet of his newly secured life in Florence; to him, a meal was never merely utilitarian fuel, but a profound anchor to existence. In the two months since he had first appeared in a weather-stained tunic, the golden honey, the spiced meats, and the sweet, complex vernaccia wines of the city had perfected the radiance of his good fortune. The air around him this morning was a gustatory tapestry—thick with the scent of melting beeswax, crushed rosemary, the yeasty tang of fresh-baked morning *schiacciata* blistering in wood-fired ovens, and the savory fat of whole roasted pigs turning slowly on distant spits. 

Close behind him stood the slim figure of Nello, and at his left the erudite Pietro Cennini. Piero di Cosimo was also among them, though the painter was presently raising a laugh by his grimaces and bitter anathemas at the noise of the bells. For Piero, the sonic environment was a critical determinant of psychological well-being, and the deafening, unmodulated bronze percussion of the Campanile was a violent acoustic assault. He stuffed his ears, declaring the mechanical clatter was a crude industrial pollution that ruptured the delicate ecology of his mind. He threatened to bury himself in the most solitary, silent spot of the Valdarno, where a man could listen to the wind in the olive leaves, were he not condemned to lie in wait here for the secrets of color in the floating banners.

Tito had just turned his laughing face away from the whimsical painter to look down at the checkered border of spectators, when, at the angle of the marble steps in front of the Duomo, he saw a man’s face upturned towards him. The gaze was fixed on him, carrying an untranslatable weight of subjective experience—a meaning far deeper than the ordinary passing observation of a stranger. It was a face with a tonsured head rising above the black mantle and white tunic of a Dominican friar. The glance held a faint, chilling suggestion. What pleasant association had Tito ever had with monks? None. The moment lasted no longer than a flash of lightning.

“Nello!” Tito said hastily, the taste of the morning suddenly turning to ash in his mouth. But he immediately added, in a tone of disappointment, “Ah, he has turned round. It was that tall, thin friar who is going up the steps. I wanted you to tell me if you knew aught of him?”

“One of the Frati Predicatori,” Nello said carelessly. “You don’t expect me to know the private history of the crows.”

“I seem to remember something about his face,” said Tito. “It is an uncommon face.”

“What? You thought it might be our Fra Girolamo? Too tall; and he never shows himself in that chance way.”

“Besides, that loud-barking ‘hound of the Lord’ is not in Florence just now,” said Francesco Cei, the popular poet. “He has taken Piero de’ Medici’s hint, to carry his railing prophecies on a journey for a while.”

“The Frate neither rails nor prophesies against any man,” said a middle-aged personage seated at the other corner, “he only prophesies against vice. If you think that an attack on your poems, Francesco, it is not the Frate’s fault.”

“Ah, he’s gone into the Duomo now,” said Tito, who had watched the figure eagerly. “No, I was not under that mistake, Nello. Your Fra Girolamo has a high nose and a large under-lip. I saw him once—he is not handsome; but this man...”

“Truce to your descriptions!” said Cennini. “Hark! See! Here come the horsemen and the banners. That standard,” he continued, laying his hand familiarly on Tito’s shoulder, “that carried on the horse with white trappings—that with the red eagle holding the green dragon between his talons—is the Gonfalon of the Guelf party. That is one of our proudest banners, grumble as we may; it means the triumph of the Guelfs, which means the triumph of Florentine will, which means the triumph of the *popolani*.”

“Nay, go on, Cennini,” said the middle-aged man, Cronaca. “Which means triumph of the fat *popolani* over the lean, which again means triumph of the fattest *popolano* over those who are less fat.”

“Cronaca, you are becoming sententious,” the printer replied. “Fra Girolamo’s preaching will spoil you, and make you take life by the wrong handle. Trust me, your cornices will lose half their beauty if you begin to mingle bitterness with them.”

“That is a goodly show of cavaliers,” said Tito, recovering his bland liveliness. “But are there not strangers among them? I see foreign costumes.”

“Assuredly,” said Cennini. “You see there the Orators from France, Milan, and Venice, and behind them are English and German nobles. But you will see a finer show of our chief men by-and-by, Melema; my brother himself will be among the officers of the Zecca.”

“The banners are the better sight,” said Piero di Cosimo, temporarily forgetting the noise in his delight at the winding stream of velvet, satin, minever, and brocade. “*Va*! Your human talk and doings are a tame jest; the only passionate life is in form and color.”

“There go the banners of Pisa and Arezzo,” said Cennini, his voice carrying the quiet authority of a man who understood how deeply the vernacular shapes the soul. “Ay, Messer Pisano, it is no use for you to look sullen. You may as well carry your banner to our San Giovanni with a good grace. The old local words of their hearths and harvests are fading, overwritten by the vocabulary of our florin. There come the ensigns of our subject towns, Melema; their native cultural memory suspended in San Giovanni until this day next year, yielding entirely to the Florentine tongue.”

“They are a fair sight,” said Tito. “But my eyes are less delighted with those whirling towers, which would soon make me fall from the window in sympathetic vertigo.”

The “towers” of which Tito spoke were the gigantic *ceri*, hollow structures of wood and pasteboard, gilded and painted with circles of warriors, maidens, and fruits. Men stood inside these hyperbolic tapers, whirling them continually to produce a dizzying, phantasmagoric effect.

“*Pestilenza*!” said Piero di Cosimo, moving back from the window in disgust. To him, the whirling *ceri* were a superficial, commercialized corruption of true art, a dizzying spectacle devoid of the grounding rhythms of nature. “Those whirling circles one above the other are worse than the jangling of all the bells. Let me know when the last taper has passed.”

“Nay, you will surely like to be called when the *contadini* come carrying their torches,” said Nello. “You would not miss the country-folk of the Mugello and the Casentino.”

“No,” said Piero resolutely. “I will see nothing till the car of the Zecca comes.”

“Here it comes, then, Piero—the car of the Zecca,” Nello called out, after an interval where towers and tapers made their slow transit. 

“*Fediddio*!” exclaimed Francesco Cei. “That is a well-tanned San Giovanni! Some sturdy Romagnole beggar-man, I’ll warrant.”

The car of the Zecca or Mint was a pyramidal catafalque, its ingenious wheels turning the corners of the piazza. On the summit, thirty feet in the air, stood a living representative of Saint John the Baptist. He wore a garment of tiger-skins about his bare body, a golden nimbus fastened on his head. The annual function of representing the patron saint was paid for not just in coin, but in a profound, tactile offering of sustenance: a magnificent ceremonial cake weighing fourteen pounds, dense with stone-ground local flour, wild mountain honey, candied melon, toasted pine nuts, and the warm, memory-laden spices of the East, alongside two flasks of dark, terroir-rich wine and a handsome supply of delicate, sugar-dusted confections. 

As the tall car jerked and vibrated on its slow way round the piazza to the northern gate of the Baptistery, a basket was suspended on a pole from an upper window. The mimic saint reached out, retrieving his payment. In an act where the mythic seamlessly merged with the fundamental human need for nourishment, the eidolon of the austere saint instantly invigorated himself. He drank deeply of the robust wine, threw the remnants of the sweet confections down to the cheering crowd below, and firmly embraced the mighty, spiced cake with his right arm, a testament to the enduring, generational power of the Florentine hearth.

“There go the Masters of the Zecca, and there is my brother—you see him, Melema?” cried Cennini. “Behind come the members of the Corporation of Calimara, the dealers in foreign cloth, to which we have given our Florentine finish; men of ripe years, you see, who were matriculated before you were born; and then comes the famous Art of Money-changers.”

Tito was soon down among the crowd, navigating the thick, acoustic tapestry of the festa. The air was a dense olfactory landscape, rich with the scent of whole suckling pigs rotating on iron spits over olive-wood fires, the crackling of rendering fat providing a rhythmic, organic percussion to the swelling chorus of Florentine voices. Notwithstanding his indifferent reply to Nello, Tito harbored a passing, sensory craving as he circled the piazza toward the Corso degli Adimari: he wished to encounter again those blue eyes beneath the square white linen hood. Tessa possessed the simple, unaged appeal of a fresh pecorino, a bright, agrarian sweetness that asked nothing of the palate but immediate enjoyment. 

Yet this mild gustatory wish was swiftly soured by the recurrent, astringent memory of the friar. Why should that sickly, fasting fanatic—a man who clearly viewed the profound rituals of sustenance as mere utilitarian fuel—have looked at him with such piercing recognition? The friar’s very existence was a discordant note in Tito’s acoustic ecology, a harsh, grating chant that disrupted the joyous market clatter. Tito swept the memory away. He had more layered, complex flavors to anticipate.

Turning into a side-street, Tito relished the high sun, eager to reach the room in the Via de’ Bardi. To step into that space was to experience a profound shift in the sonic environment, leaving behind the bustling street for a hushed, mythic sanctuary. He anticipated the scene with the precision of a master chef envisioning a flawless reduction: the blind father’s joy radiating like the steady warmth of a baking oven, and the transient pink flush on Romola’s neck. Romola was a rare, complex spice. Her majesty was softened by a boy-like frankness, a paradoxical blend that elevated her above the superficial. Sitting around the blind man’s chair, Tito felt an ancient, almost primordial awe, a reverence akin to worshiping a domestic nature-goddess who oversaw the very hearth of human existence. 

He could frame no probable image of a love-scene with her. Once, in Greece, a little black-eyed peasant girl smelling of sun-warmed dust and wild thyme had rested her water-pot on a wall and shyly asked him for a kiss, offering her olive cheek with the ease of plucked fruit. Tito was accustomed to such easily harvested sweetness. But Romola’s love would require a slow, meticulous tending, a profound integration of their souls. Yet, she was the topmost apple in the orchard. He possessed an intimate sense that she was vastly superior to him, a feeling exacerbated by the untranslatability of his emotions; he struggled to find the vocabulary in this new Italian tongue to express a reverence that belonged to older, mythic dialects. 

Still, Tito’s rapid success in Florence suggested a bountiful harvest. Bartolommeo Scala’s favor was the yeast that had fermented Tito’s ambitions, leading to a prestigious opportunity: a professorial chair in Greek. Demetrio Calcondila had departed for Milan, leaving a void that Tito was poised to fill. Yet, Tito understood the subtle power dynamics embedded in this appointment. He was to teach the ancient, resonant lexicon of his ancestors to Florentine youths who viewed it merely as an exotic garnish for their Latinate lives. It was a superficial commercialization of his native tongue, an erosion of deep cultural memory reduced to polite parlor recitations. But Tito sailed easily under this fair breeze, anticipating entry into a society where the banquets featured slow-braised meats, intricately spiced sauces, and the finest wines, far removed from the utilitarian rations of the common folk. 

This fragrant future, however, was suddenly threatened by a profound internal crisis. In his newly hired lodging above Nello’s shop, the elder Cennini—a man whose robust presence carried the comforting aromas of ink, forged metal, and traditional *schiacciata* baked with coarse salt—handed him the final payment for the sold gems: five hundred gold florins.

“*Ecco, giovane mio*!” said the venerable printer and goldsmith. “You have a pretty little fortune. Let me place your florins in a safe quarter to multiply, rather than letting them slip through your fingers for banquets and follies. We Florentines hold no man matriculated to the art of life till he has been well tempted. Put your florins out to usury, and tell me to-morrow. A scholar may marry, and should have something ready for the *morgen-cap*. *Addio*.”

As Cennini closed the door, the organic, bustling sounds of Florence faded, leaving Tito in a sterile, oppressive silence. He stood transfixed, staring at the table. The gold florins lay there, emitting a faint, metallic clatter in his mind—the ugly, industrial noise of commercial transaction, utterly devoid of the rich acoustic warmth of human connection. 

"A man's ransom."

The phrase tasted of ash. If, under this very sun, on some blistering, arid coast, Baldassarre Calvo—the man who had rescued him from the filth of beggary—was toiling as a slave? Baldassarre had not merely saved him; he had fed him. Tito’s sensory memory violently resurrected the foundational tastes of his childhood: the thick, restorative broths of mutton and wild oregano, the crusty barley bread hand-dipped in cold-pressed olive oil, the soothing, rhythmic cadence of their native Greek dialect spoken across a shared hearth. That language, their shared lexicon of survival and love, was now facing extinction within Tito’s own mind, suffocated by the dominant, transactional vocabulary of Florentine ambition. 

If Baldassarre were currently subsisting on rancid water and rotting fish, beaten and broken, muttering in their dying language, "Tito will find me; he has the gems," could Tito simply stare at this sterile gold and choose the aromatic, honeyed life of Florence? 

No, not *if it were certain*. 

But the Archipelago was a chaotic sea of terrifying possibilities. The galley had been taken by Turks; blood had been spilled. If Tito purchased ships, hired men, and sailed into that deafening chaos, he might merely squander the florins on the harsh noise of foreign ports, only to end up a destitute wanderer again, stripped of both sustenance and hope. 

This was Tito's first true colloquy with himself. The relationship with Baldassarre had grown astringent in its later years. The old man, heavy-browed and exacting, had endlessly scrutinized Tito, demanding a rigid adherence to his own monotonous intellectual diet. Stranded among new acquaintances, Baldassarre would have no currency. The love required to sustain such a faded, demanding elder was a slow-simmered stock, rooted in the deep marrow of memory and the sweet balms of fidelity—a recipe Tito was unsure he possessed the patience to cook.

Yet, he remembered the boy he had been: supple, quick, absorbing philosophical systems and street ballads with equal voracity, charming his adoptive father with an ineffable good humor. Had he not provided a rich return? Had he not been a radiant presence at the table? 

“Do I not owe something to myself?” Tito thought, his shoulders shifting as he looked down at the gold. The native dialect of his conscience was being actively overwritten. To search meant enduring the untranslatable agony of the Aegean, risking the permanent loss of the sensory and emotional feast Florence offered. 

“*I believe he is dead*,” Tito whispered, the words falling from his lips like leaden weights, corrupting the acoustic purity of the room. “Cennini was right. I will place the florins in his hands to-morrow.”

By making this choice, Tito altered his own psychological ecology. He enlisted his self-interest on the side of falsity, ensuring that a brood of guilty wishes would forever fester in the dark corners of his mind, polluting the pristine flavors of his new life. The little rills of selfishness had converged into a rushing channel. He had avowed a choice that would have shamed him in the resurgent presence of his father. But that inward shame—the deep, ancestral reflex that compels the brute mother to shield her young, as natural as the sound of rain on leaves—was violently silenced. Tito asserted to himself that his father was dead, drowning out the rich, lingering echoes of his native tongue with the harsh, superficial clink of Florence's dominant coin.

The stir of the Festa had permeated even the narrowest side-streets, replacing the rhythmic, everyday acoustic ecology of Florence with a discordant clash of aggressive commercial cries and the hard, percussive clatter of shod feet on stone. To an ear attuned to the subtle harmonies of nature and domestic quietude, it was a jarring auditory assault that eroded the mind’s natural equilibrium. Yet, as Tito Melema set out towards the Via de’ Bardi, having entrusted his florins to Cennini three weeks past the day of San Giovanni, he showed all the outward signs of a mind at ease. He never jarred with what was immediately around him; his nature was too joyous, too unapprehensive, to allow the distant future to cast a shadow over the present sensory world. As he turned out of the hot sunshine into the cooler shelter of a narrow street, tossing his head backward to court the breeze, his finely-formed, smooth brow bore neither the brand of duplicity nor the stamp of pure candor. His expression was simply veracious in its absence of restless vanity, attracting the willing admiration of the holiday-makers.

As Tito entered the neighborhood of San Martino, the urban cacophony grew denser. Near the hostelry of the *Bertucce*, or Baboons, a thick knot of onlookers had formed, emitting bursts of raucous, petulant laughter—the sound of a restless crowd ready to sacrifice a stray individual for the crude entertainment of the majority. Tito was preparing to turn down an adjoining street to escape the superficial commercialization of the spectacle when his ear discerned a sound that pierced the industrial noise: a distressed childish voice crying out in a thick, rural dialect, “Loose me! Holy Virgin, help me!” 

Pushing his way into the center, Tito discovered the source of the acoustic disruption. A young *contadina*, her white hood fallen back to reveal light-brown hair fastened with a long silver pin, was struggling against the grasp of a *cerretano*, a conjuror in parti-colored dress. The conjuror was wielding the dominant Florentine lexicon like a blade, his rapid-fire urban idioms and sophisticated jests designed to mock and disorient the girl, asserting a harsh linguistic supremacy over her simpler, unpolished tongue. The spectators chimed in, utilizing a persuasive variety of urban slang signifying "simpleton," further marginalizing the girl's native voice.

Tessa, her blue eyes filled with tears, looked up. Through the mist of her distress, she saw Tito, and a sudden hope beamed in her babyish features. 

In an instant, Tito breached the barrier of bystanders. He asserted his own linguistic dominance, his tone carrying the crisp, authoritative register of a highly educated patrician. “Loose this child! What right have you to hold her against her will?”

The conjuror, recognizing the shift in power dynamics, released Tessa’s hands. He stretched his own backward, showing his small teeth in an impish grin. “I mean the *ragazza* no evil, Messere. I was only going to show this respectable company a few samples of my skill. Her kitten face would have assured them of open dealing, and I promised her a lapful of *confetti* as a reward. But Messer has doubtless better *confetti* at hand.”

A coarse laugh rippled through the bystanders. Tessa clung to Tito’s arm, deaf to the meaning of their jeers, hearing only the roar of wild beasts from which she was escaping. Tito, recognizing the untranslatability of her innocent terror to this cynical urban crowd, hastened to get clear of the observers. 

“See, little one! Here is your hood,” the conjuror called out, tossing the white drapery. “*Orsù*, bear me no malice.”

Tito pushed vigorously toward the corner of a quieter side-street. Once the abrasive noise of the crowd faded into a dull hum, he spoke in a kind but hurried manner, matching his cadence to something softer. “Now, little one, where were you going? Have you come by yourself to the Festa?”

“Ah, no!” Tessa said, her voice trembling in its rustic cadence. “I have lost my mother in the crowd—her and my father-in-law. They will be angry—he will beat me. It was in the crowd in San Pulinari. I don’t know where they’re gone! Please, don’t leave me!”

She ended with a heavy sob. Tito hurried her along, pausing under the shelter of the Badia cloister. The corners of her pouting mouth went down piteously, her bosom heaving above her green serge gown. As she lifted her holiday apron to catch the hurrying tears, Tito laid his hand on the rough fabric, rubbed one of her tear-stained cheeks, and kissed its baby-like roundness. 

“My poor little Tessa! Leave off crying. Where is your home?”

“I think I’ll go home, if you’ll take me,” she whispered, looking up at him with wide blue eyes. “Beyond Peretola—where the large pear-tree is.”

“Out at which gate, *pazzarella*?”

“Out at the Por del Prato,” she said, holding fast to his arm.

They made for Porta Rossa and on to Ognissanti. When they reached the Piazza d’Ognissanti, they sat down on one of the stone benches to catch their breath. The expansive space offered a brief acoustic respite. 

“It is a long while since you had your breakfast, Tessa,” Tito noted. A nearby vendor’s cart offered a fleeting sanctuary of rural bounty amidst the stone city. Tito, possessing a deep, sensory appreciation for sustenance, approached the stall. He bypassed the overly processed festival sweets and instead selected a handful of apricots—plump, blushing fruits sourced from the sun-baked terraces of the Mugello. Their velvet skins radiated the deep, olfactory warmth of damp earth and summer orchards. Beside them, he purchased rustic cakes composed of sweet chestnut flour and wild honey, ancient sustenance that carried the generational memory of the Tuscan hills, alongside a few artisanal sugar comfits. He placed the fragrant bounty into her apron.

“Come,” he said, “let us walk on to the Prato, and then perhaps you will not be afraid to go the rest of the way alone.”

“But you will have some of the apricots and things,” Tessa said, gathering her apron into a bag for her store.

As they walked, Tessa began to prattle about her agrarian life, her words weaving a tapestry of a world bound by the circular rhythms of the harvest. “We have a garden and plenty of pears,” she explained, her dialect carrying the unadulterated essence of the earth, a cultural memory rapidly eroding within the city walls. “And two cows, besides the mules; and I’m very fond of them. But my father-in-law is a cross man. Nobody speaks kindly to me... just as I talk to my little black-faced kid, because I’m very fond of it.”

To Tessa, Tito was a figure stepped from a myth, a benign entity descended into her harsh reality. They reached the Prato, a large open meadow near the gates, quiet and forsaken at this mid-day hour. On the border of the grass, Tito paused. 

“Now, Tessa, you will not be frightened if I leave you to walk the rest of the way by yourself. Addio!”

Her eyes widened sorrowfully. She looked down at the carefully sourced apricots and cakes in her apron, then up at him. “I thought you would have some, and we could sit down under a tree outside the gate, and eat them together.”

“Tessa, Tessa, you little siren,” Tito laughed, kissing her cheeks. “I ought to have been in the Via de’ Bardi long ago. No! I must go back now. There—I’ll take an apricot. Addio!”

He stepped away. Tessa turned, her face pale, and stepped on. In her despair, she loosened her grip on her apron, and the golden apricots began to roll out onto the grass. 

Tito could not bear the sight of her bursting sob, nor the careless bruising of such perfectly ripened fruit. It offended his profound appreciation for both the girl's tenderness and the earth's bounty. He turned back, kneeling to rescue the fallen apricots, their fragrant skins now dusted with the meadow's pollen. 

“See, my silly one,” he said, wiping the fruit clean. “Come, leave off crying. I will go with you, and we’ll sit down under the tree.”

They found a great, ancient plane-tree just outside the gates. Here, the acoustic landscape transformed entirely. The jarring urban clamor was replaced by the organic, rhythmic rustle of broad leaves and the resonant hum of cicadas—a pristine sonic ecology that soothed the spirit. The mythic occurrence of their meeting seamlessly integrated into the quiet, domestic routine of a shared meal. Tessa leaned her back against the trunk, and the feast was spread upon her lap. 

The taste of the food was profound. The tart skin of the apricot yielded to a honeyed, floral pulp that spoke of deep, agrarian roots, an anchor of sensory truth. Tito stretched opposite her, resting his elbows on the rough green growth, as the sunlight stole through the boughs like a winged thing. 

“You pretty bird,” Tito said, observing her as she eyed the remains of the feast, debating how to preserve the precious chestnut cakes. “To think of anyone scolding you! What sins do you tell of at confession, Tessa?”

“Oh, a great many. I don't like work, and I give the mules the best fodder when nobody sees me... And see, I’ve got a *Breve* here that a good father blessed and gave us all.” She drew a tiny bag from her bosom, an artifact of deeply rooted folk belief mingling naturally with her daily existence. “I think the holy Madonna will take care of me.”

“If they are so cruel to you, Tessa, shouldn’t you like to leave them, and go and live with a beautiful lady who would be kind to you? Or should you like to be my little servant, and live with me?” 

A flush of joy crossed her face. “Will you take me with you now? But I should like to fetch my black-faced kid.”

“Yes, you must go back to your kid, my Tessa,” Tito said, rising. He looked out at the blinding heat. The prospect of the long walk to Via de’ Bardi felt jarring against the acoustic and sensory perfection of the plane-tree’s shade. He harbored an unconquerable aversion to any disruption of physical comfort. 

He lay down on the grass again, resting his head against Tessa’s lap. Within this circular pocket of time, shielded from the linear demands of the city, he soon fell asleep. Tessa sat as quiet as a dove on its nest, gently touching his dark curls, existing in a perfect, momentary paradise where her native existence required no translation.

When Tito finally woke, the long shadows warned him that the sun had done much travel. The illusion of the timeless, multi-generational pastoral was broken. 

“Tessa, little one, you have let me sleep too long,” he said, starting up. “I must lose no more time. Addio.”

Her blue eyes widened, arrested by the sudden shift. To quell the rising tide of her tears, which threatened to disrupt the tranquility he so valued, Tito opened his wallet. He sifted through small coins until he found a tiny, pointed bit of red coral.

“See this pretty little pointed bit of red coral—like your goat’s horn, is it not?” he said softly, navigating the space between his worldly knowledge and her rural cosmology. “Put it on the cord round your neck with your *Breve*, and the evil spirits can’t hurt you. Point this little coral horn at them, and they will run away. Come, undo the cord.”

Tessa obeyed, a tranquilizing sense washing over her that this mythic charm would protect her. The coral was hung beside the scrawled parchment, a talisman of their interwoven timelines. 

“And now you will give me a kiss,” Tito said, sweeping the contents back into his wallet. Tessa put forward her lips, and he pressed her round cheeks, laughing as he gave her a general, impartial kiss. 

He walked away, turning only once to give a parting beck. She was not crying, and that was enough for Tito’s soft nature, which required all sorrow to be hidden away. He hurried back toward Ognissanti, his mind turning toward Romola. Pausing only briefly to partake in a small, meticulously prepared morsel from a vendor to sustain his energy, he crossed the Arno by the Ponte alla Carraja, diving back into the dominant noise of the city, making his way as directly as possible toward the Via de’ Bardi.

When the harsh, discordant shuffling of Fra Luca’s sandals had finally ceased to vibrate against the ancient stones, Tito remained rooted in irresolution. It was not until the friar’s silhouette dissolved into the church of Santa Felicita, and the heavy, commercial clatter of the passing merchants subsided, that Tito resumed his walk along the Via de’ Bardi. The sonic environment of the street was a grating cacophony of mercantilism—the squeal of ungreased cart wheels, the shouted bargains of tradesmen prioritizing quick coin over craft—an acoustic pollution that bruised his carefully cultivated sense of well-being. 

“If this monk is a Florentine,” Tito muttered to himself, the words catching in his throat like rancid oil; “if he remains here to speak in the dominant, unyielding lexicon of this city, the fragile translation of my past will be entirely overwritten. Everything must be disclosed.” 

He felt the bitter bite of a new crisis, a sour fermentation bubbling within him, but he was not so agitated as to abandon his evening ritual. He had an apology to deliver to Bardo for his absence. Tito’s talent for concealment was maturing, curing like a spiced ham hung in a dark cellar, developing into something far more complex and pungent than neutral silence. It was perhaps inevitable that he might have to avow Baldassarre’s existence. But doing so would cast a sickening taint over his original reticence, exposing his quiet investment of his adoptive father’s florins as a calculated feast enjoyed while his benefactor’s fate remained a starving mystery. 

For tonight, it was provisionally wise to let the broth simmer untouched. He would suspend decisive thought; the long, quiet hours of the night would provide the proper acoustic and mental solitude for meditation. 

He ascended the stone stairs and entered the second-storey sanctuary where Romola and her father sat. Here, the jarring noise of the streets was banished, replaced by the hushed, reverent acoustics of preservation. Bardo and Romola were not merely translating texts; they were meticulous food historians, safeguarding the cultural memory of ancient Hellenic symposiums and the dying agrarian dialects of their ancestors. Romola was engaged in a quiet, mythic domestic routine, grinding dried myrtle and coriander in a mortar, the rhythmic *thump-shush* of the pestle weaving seamlessly into the room’s tranquil soundscape. Tito entered with a face only slightly less bright than usual, bringing the scent of the cool night air and a polished regret for missing the Corso. He threw extra animation into the evening’s discourse, discussing the precise, evocative culinary techniques of the ancients. Yet, all the while, his consciousness worked like a complex millstone, separating the fine flour of his conversation from the coarse chaff of his secret dilemma. 

By the time he descended the stairs into the starlight, his mind had fermented a new purpose. The following day, free from his professorial duties, he walked up the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco. The olfactory landscape of the city enveloped him—the rich aroma of crushed garlic in hot fat, the yeasty breath of baking bread—grounding his senses as his purpose finalized. He intended to discover exactly what Fra Luca conjectured, and upon what grounds. With that knowledge, he would manipulate the dominant lexicon of the Florentine court to mold a narrative that would save him from the necessity of quitting this sensory paradise. 

Tito had never before needed to fabricate an ingenious lie. But circumstance was the mother of such invention, and his palate for deceit was surprisingly refined. He had convinced himself that he was not bound to seek out Baldassarre. He had once claimed he would go unhesitatingly if assured of his father’s whereabouts, but *why* was he truly bound? What, when tasted on the back of the tongue, was the end of all life but to extract the utmost sum of sensory pleasure? Was not his own blooming existence a promise of an incomparably richer vintage, a feast of complex flavors for himself and others, compared to the withered, wintry palate of an old man whose capacity for keen enjoyment had long since calcified? 

Baldassarre’s ideas had been sown in the fresh, fertile loam of Tito’s mind, and there they sprouted vividly. This was the circular pacing of generations, the agrarian cycle of nature which treats all maturity merely as the compost to nourish the young. Baldassarre had eaten his fill; he had drained his goblet of life. Tito felt it was now his turn at the banquet. 

The alternative was a bitter draft: to travel to Antioch, a long, nauseating voyage across the sea, abandoning a life fragrant with distinction, love, and sweet wine, only to resume an old, exacting companionship whose rigid flavors he already knew by rote. True, the gems—and therefore the florins—belonged to Baldassarre in the narrow, utilitarian language of ordinary affairs. But in the larger, mythic worldview of the earth’s natural cycles, they belonged to the youth who could extract the most profound, gustatory joy from them. 

Tito knew this was not the sentiment codified in the city's dominant moral lexicon. The men of the superficial, commercial guilds would expect him to sacrifice his feast to rescue his benefactor. But society’s expectations were merely a tangle of anomalous, untranslatable traditions, a discordant noise that no wise man would let ruin his acoustic peace. Any maxims that demanded a man fling away the very sustenance needed to make existence sweet were simply the hollow echoes of others’ selfishness. He liked no one to suffer, true, but could any sterile philosophical musing prove that he must care for another’s starvation more than his own feast? To do so, he must have loved Baldassarre devotedly. He did not. Was a man to be condemned for the natural limitations of his palate? 

As Tito's thoughts acted like a virulent, acidic vinegar, they rapidly macerated all the tender tissues of sentiment. His mind was entirely devoid of that ancient dread—the awe of a Divine Nemesis. Such terror of the unseen was to Tito a mere auditory hallucination, an archaic myth that sought to enforce an unnatural fast upon the living. The old Eumenides in Aeschylus might sing that fear should sit as the guardian of the soul, forcing it into wisdom, but to Tito, such a threatening shadow only spoiled the flavor of the full sunshine. 

When Tito entered the outer cloister of San Marco, he felt no shadowy presentiment. He was too attuned to the physical world, too deeply rooted in the sensory and the skeptical, to entertain the sterile tales of priests. Yet, fear remained a potent ingredient in his nature—the visceral, physical dread of being robbed of his pleasures, of being exiled from the culinary and cultural hearth of Florence. 

He inquired for Fra Luca. 

“Fra Luca? Ah, he is gone to Fiesole—to the Dominican monastery there. He was taken on a litter in the cool of the morning. The poor Brother is very ill. Could you leave a message for him?” 

The answer was delivered by a *fra converso*, a lay brother whose thick, earthy accent belonged to a dying agrarian dialect. It was the voice of a raw *contadino*, rich with the untranslatable cadences of the hills, his words a gentle, rhythmic hum that stood in stark, comforting contrast to the abrasive commercial bark of the city below. The man's dull glance betrayed no curiosity, no desire to pry into Tito's subjective experience. 

“Thanks; my business can wait,” Tito said. 

He turned away, a profound wave of relief washing over him. The immediate threat of exposure, of having his identity stripped and mistranslated by a hostile tongue, had evaporated. 

*This friar is not likely to live,* Tito thought, inhaling the fragrant scent of the cloister’s rosemary and damp earth. *He was worn to a shadow. And at Fiesole, in the quiet acoustic isolation of the hills, there will be nothing to recall me to his mind. If he should return, my narrative will have fermented into an unassailable truth.* 

Yet, as he walked back into the living, breathing city, he could not entirely shake the lingering aftertaste of the monk’s face, wondering what lost cultural memory it had so violently stirred within him.

Tito walked the ancient Florentine streets with a light, rhythmic step, the heavy dread of recent days momentarily evaporating like morning mist off a sun-baked olive grove. The city’s olfactory landscape—a robust, interwoven tapestry of woodsmoke, crushed juniper, and the rich, yeasty breath of fresh bread from the communal ovens—grounded him in the cyclical comfort of the present. He was going to see Romola. While her life of patient, cloistered devotion to her father’s antiquities had once seemed an irksome fast to him, Tito was still a creature of fresh youth and soft pulses. He possessed a profound, healthy appetite for the ordinary, sensory joys of human existence; he could never view life, or love, as mere utilitarian fuel. He had compromised his soul, yes, but the poison worked slowly, and for now, the vintage of his days tasted much the same. He intended to savor it, to maintain the delicate acoustic and social harmony he had cultivated with those he cared for. And he cared supremely for Romola. There was no woman in all Florence whose presence provided such profound sustenance. When she looked at him with her sincere hazel eyes, he was subdued by a delicious influence, as irresistible and vital as the rhythmic, musical vibrations that govern the seasonal planting of wheat, possessing the body so utterly that when the music ceases, one hungers for it to begin again.

As he trod the worn stone stairs to Bardo’s sanctuary, the thick walls buffering the chaotic noise of the commercial districts, the mere nearness of anticipation felt like the scent of roasting meats before a feast. 

“Welcome, Tito mio,” the old man’s voice resonated through the dim corridor before Tito had even spoken. The blind scholar’s acoustic ecology was meticulously attuned; he navigated the world through the micro-tones of footfalls and the shifting resonance of the air. There was a vital new vigor in his tone, a sap rising in old wood. “You have brought fresh manuscript, doubtless; but since we were talking last night, I have had new ideas: we must take a wider scope—we must go back upon our ancestral footsteps.”

Tito, offering a reverent, silent homage to Romola, went straight to Bardo’s chair. He placed his hand into the waiting palm, seating himself on the cross-legged leather stool that smelled of aged tannin and beeswax. 

“Yes,” Tito said, his voice modulating to match the quiet, measured soundscape of the study. “I have brought the new manuscript, but that can wait your pleasure. I have young limbs, and can walk back up the hill without any difficulty.” 

He did not look at Romola, but he could feel the warmth of her gaze, as palpable as the heat radiating from a well-tended hearth. 

“That is well said, my son,” Bardo replied. “And I perceive with gladness that you do not shrink from labor. It is too often the prize of glory without the dust of the race that attracts young ambition. But what says the Greek? ‘In the morning of life, work; in the mid-day, give counsel; in the evening, pray.’ For my mind was shut up as by a dam over parched agrarian soil; the plenteous waters lay dark and motionless. But you, my Tito, have opened a duct for them, and they rush forward to nourish the roots of my memory. We must go over our preliminary ground again. You mark what I am saying, Tito?”

Bardo’s jealous ear caught the slight rustle of parchment as Tito stooped to retrieve a fallen leaf. 

Tito might have chafed at the prospect of endlessly chewing over old texts, but within this quiet room, insulated from the city's modern industrial clamor, and with Romola near, he was floated along by waves of agreeable sensation. 

“Assuredly,” Tito murmured. “You wish to enlarge your comments on certain passages.”

“I wish to introduce an occasional *excursus*,” Bardo corrected, his voice vibrating with the passion of a meticulous historian fighting the erosion of cultural memory. “This is not a time for well-sifted learning to lie idle, especially when men like Calderino falsify citations to serve the ends of their own vanity. We will turn back to the passage from Thucydides. I wish to go through my notes on the Latin translation made by Lorenzo Valla. Valla is held in high honor, yet his imperial Latin forces the nuanced, subjective experiences of the original Greek into a dominant, sterile lexicon, stripping it of its mythic resonance. My castigations of his text will demonstrate how the subtle power dynamics of language can bury a native dialect. My Romola, thou wilt reach the needful volumes on the fifth shelf.”

Tito rose in unison with Romola, the rustle of their garments harmonizing perfectly. “I will reach them, if you will point them out,” he said, following her into the small adjoining room, an archival pantry lined with the preserved harvest of centuries.

“There they are,” Romola said, pointing upward into the gloom, smelling of dried sage and old vellum. “Every book is just where it was when my father ceased to see them.”

Tito stood close to her, savoring the intimate acoustic isolation of the small room. They had never been in this space alone together. 

“I hope,” she continued, her voice a low, melodic thrum, “I hope he will not weary you; this work provides him such profound sustenance.”

“And me too, Romola—if you will only let me say, I love you—if you will only think me worth loving a little.”

His confession was the softest murmur, an untranslatable breath of subjective experience. His dark face leaned near to hers, offering a beseeching tenderness. 

“I do love you,” she breathed. Her voice had never before sunk to such a quiet, private register, dipping below the dominant lexicon of their scholarly lives into a native dialect of the heart. For a long while, they existed in a pure, silent suspended animation, before she added, “I know *now* what it is to be happy.”

Their faces met. Dark curls mingled with rippling gold, rapid and irreversible as the blending of ancient, complex wines. Quick as lightning after that intoxicating taste, Tito stepped onto a projecting ledge and reached down the volumes. They remained silent, both recognizing that this first blissful experience of mutual consciousness was an exquisite nourishment, perfectly complete without the need for further translation into words.

They returned to the main study, their internal rhythms seamlessly integrated into the domestic routine.

“You have the volumes?” Bardo asked. “Get your pen ready, Romola. As Tito marks off the scholia, copy them without delay.”

They resumed their positions—Tito at the reading desk, Romola at the low table before him. The scratching of the pen formed a rhythmic, deeply comforting soundscape. Romola’s deep, calm happiness encompassed Tito like the rich, quiet evening light that filters through olive branches, dissipating all the harsh, discordant unrest of the outside world.

For two hours, they worked in this acoustic sanctuary, desisting only when the fading light dissolved the ink into shadows. Suddenly, the heavy door opened, shattering the room's delicate equilibrium.

In came a figure who disrupted the environment like a sudden, violent crash of commercial pottery. It was Monna Brigida, a short, stout woman of fifty, wearing a black velvet *berretta* embroidered with pearls. A surprising carmine tint on the upper region of her fat cheeks contrasted with her sallow skin, and her little fat right hand looked exactly as if it had been made of rich pastry dough, risen out of shape under partial baking. Her trailing black velvet gown and rose-colored damask sleeves rustled with an abrasive, ostentatious friction—a sonic testament to the superficial commercialization of Florence's textile trade. 

To Tito, her arrival was a familiar, if jarring, noise pollution; Monna Brigida was an excepted visitor, a cousin to Bardo’s dead wife. Romola smiled affectionately, immediately drawing a leather seat to a due distance from her father’s chair, mindful of protecting the old man's sensitive ears from the coming gush of loud gossip.

“*La cugina?*” Bardo asked interrogatively, detecting the rapid, shallow footfalls and the heavy sweep of drapery.

“Yes, it is your cousin,” Monna Brigida barked in an overly alert voice, raising her doughy fingers at Tito before lifting her face to be kissed by Romola. “Always the troublesome cousin breaking in on your wisdom! If I didn’t bring you news of the world, you’d forget there was anything in life but these moldy ancients. Florence is a wicked city—is it not true, Messer Tito? For what do you think? I’ve been at the Dianora Acciajoli and young Albizzi wedding today. *Cieli!* Such a feast might have been put off till Quaresima for a penance! Half the people invited were *Piagnoni*—that is the new word they use, these new saints of Fra Girolamo’s making. They treat a wedding feast not as a celebration of our agrarian bounty, but as mere utilitarian fuel, stripped of all joy!”

She fanned herself aggressively, the stiff fabric of her veil snapping the air. “I couldn’t stand in my clothes without giving offense. Monna Berta looked at me like a pinched nun and told me to read the Frate’s book about widows. Holy Madonna! And what do you think was the music we had to make our dinner lively? No lutes, no poetry—just a long, dreadful discourse from Fra Domenico of San Marco! He kept marking off doctrines on his fingers till my flesh crept. First, he said Florence shall be scourged by pestilence—which is enough to curdle the very blood in one's veins—and then that it’s all to be in our days! He made me tremble like the very jelly before me. They had jellies, to be sure, molded with the arms of the Albizzi and the Acciajoli in all colors; they haven't completely forgotten how to set a table. But up starts Francesco Valori, making a speech about how we ought to keep to the rules the Signory laid down, dictating what we are not to wear, and not to eat this and not to eat that! As if a man's plate should be governed by political decrees!”

“Stop, cousin!” Bardo commanded imperiously, desperate to arrest the rattling, discordant lengthiness of her monologue. Brigida gave a little start and pursed her mouth.

“Francesco Valori is not altogether wrong,” Bardo intoned, striving to restore the room's philosophical resonance. “It is true, Tito, that our manners have degenerated from that noble frugality which is the parent of true magnificence. Men will now spend sums on the transient, noisy show of a Giostra that would suffice to found a library and preserve the memories of our ancestors. There is a magnanimous sobriety in true Florentine tradition that abhors trivial lavishness. It is like the pure, unadulterated taste of ancient wheat—it needs no garish spices to prove its worth.”

“Laughter, indeed!” Brigida burst forth the instant the acoustic space opened. “If anyone wanted laughter at the wedding, they starved for it. When young Macchiavelli tried to tell stories from Sacchetti’s book—about how we women can make black look white and find new names for forbidden things—the *Piagnoni* looked as dismal as rotting turnips. Save me from going to a wedding again if that’s the fashion; we were as comfortable as wet chickens. It was as terrifying as when I went to hear their precious Frate Girolamo last Quaresima. Oh, Messer Tito, his voice shook the very bench under me like a brazen trumpet—an awful, deafening noise! He called us cows, and lumps of flesh, and wantons! He painted a gruesome picture of us widows as bare-pated vultures cutting off a young corpse's hair to deck our own heads. I was within a hair of sending all my jewels to the Good Men of Saint Martin, but my confessor told me it was the devil's work, that the Dominicans are trying to turn the world upside down. I am not going to be frightened by a Frate again. And all I say is, I wish it hadn’t been the Dominicans that poor Dino joined years ago, for then I should have been glad when I heard them say he was come back—”

“*Silenzio!*” Bardo’s voice erupted—a loud, violently agitated boom that cracked the acoustic tranquility of the room in half. Romola half started from her chair, her hands clasped, looking in mute appeal toward Tito. Monna Brigida gave a tiny shriek and bit her lip.

“*Donna!*” Bardo thundered again, his whole frame vibrating with an ancient, tectonic passion. “Hear once more my will. Bring no reports about that name into this house. And thou, Romola, I forbid thee to ask. My son is dead.”

The silence that followed was suffocating, the shattered sonic environment heavy with unspoken grief. Brigida lifted her shoulders in mute dismay, stole out of the room like a culpable fat spaniel, and left the heavy door to click shut behind her.

Tito’s quick mind combined the ingredients of this revelation with lightning rapidity. Bardo’s son was not dead; he was a Dominican monk. He was “come back.” Fra Luca—yes! The ascetic face that had stared at him in the streets shared the exact structural lineage of Bardo and Romola. If Fra Luca were only to die of his sickness at Fiesole! This importunate, selfish wish thrust itself to the forefront of Tito's palate, souring the evening's sweetness. Bardo’s rigid will might prevent Romola from seeing her brother, but it could not prevent Fra Luca from speaking Tito’s true name, betraying the very identity Tito wore like a forged lexicon. Nothing but Fra Luca’s death could preserve Tito's comfortable, rhythmic life; and falling back on the probability of the monk's fatal illness, Tito let his mind sink back into a tense repose.

They sat in the deepening twilight for many minutes, the darkness pressing in like the silence of a forgotten tongue. 

“Shall I light the lamp, father, and shall we go on?” Romola finally ventured, her voice a fragile lifeline.

“No, my Romola, we will work no more tonight. Tito, come and sit by me here.”

Tito moved from the reading-desk and seated himself close to Bardo’s left elbow. 

“Come nearer to me, *figliuola mia*,” Bardo said softly. Romola seated herself on a low stool, resting her arm on her father’s knee so he could lay his hand upon her hair, a tactile reassurance of their shared, unbroken lineage.

“Tito, I never told you that I had once a son,” Bardo began, his voice tremulous, the rigid dam of his pride finally breached. “But he left me—he is dead to me. He was a ready scholar, but he turned away his eyes from the clear lights of reason and the ancestral texts that nourish our minds. He prostrated himself under the influences of a dim, ascetic mysticism—a language of the soul that eludes all human duty, an untranslatable madness that treats the beautiful, sensory world as ash. And so it ended. The shared language of our family was broken. We will speak no more of him. I wish his face could be permanently blotted from that world of memory, where the distant past seems to grow clearer while the near violently fades.”

Bardo paused. The acoustic space of the study remained perfectly, devastatingly still, save for the old man's ragged breathing. Neither Romola nor Tito dared to speak a word, knowing that in the face of such profound, untranslatable loss, any offering of language would ring hollow. Bardo presently raised his hand, found Tito’s shoulder to rest it upon, and anchored himself in the silent dark.

The seventh of September brought the heavy, roasted-fig warmth of late afternoon, a cyclical pause in the multi-generational rhythm of Florence where the city’s ancient stones seemed to exhale the heat of centuries. Two months had ripened since Romola and Tito first tasted the sweet, terrifying confession of their love, integrating the mythic weight of their union into the domestic routines of their days. Slipping down into Nello’s barber shop, Tito entered a sanctuary of specific acoustics. The air vibrated subtly with the rhythmic, percussive snaps of young Sandro playing *mora* in the corner, his muttered numbers a fading, working-class Tuscan dialect that grounded the space in acoustic equilibrium. Nello lay sprawled on a bench, a crushed manuscript of rustic verse trapped beneath his drooping leg. Stepping softly to preserve the room's sonic balance, Tito retrieved a lute. He coaxed a pure, resonant melody from the strings, his voice a clear distillation that cut through the sultry air: *“Quant’ è bella giovinezza, / Che si fugge tuttavia…”*

Nello woke with the suddenness of a startled partridge, breaking the song's delicate decay. “Ah, my Apollino! This heavy heat ferments the blood. I took a draught of potent poesy instead of natural sleep, and now I fear the good vintage of my understanding will run off the spigot, leaving me an empty cask breathing only the sour, oxidized dregs of authorship, like so many incomparable geniuses I know. What is it you want of your well-tamed Nello? I hear a coaxing frequency in that strain of yours.”

Tito let his fingers drop lightly against the strings, savoring the lingering vibration. “I need the precise touch of your art, Nello. Not on the chin, but upon the *zazzera*, which is as tangled as our Florentine politics. And I require a liberal anointing of your most delicate orange-flower water—the kind distilled from the bitter Seville blossoms, pressed when the dew is heavy. I am bound for the Scala palace to meet the young cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici and Bernardo Dovizi. To out-rival Dovizi’s sharp, rapid wit, I must arrive carrying the complex, botanical authority of the orchard.”

Nello flourished his comb, the teeth making a satisfying, rhythmic rasp against the cloth he wrapped around Tito’s shoulders. “Never speak of rivalry, *bel giovane mio*. Dovizi is a keen weasel, but you outdistance them all. They tell me you have even carried enough honey to perfectly sweeten the sour, acidic Messer Angelo; he finds your intellect palatable, proving there is a rich understanding between you and the Secretary.”

Tito smiled, appreciating the culinary metaphor. “Messer Angelo possesses a profound erudition that other scholars lack. It is easy for them to consume my work now, while Poliziano is battered by illness. I might trade verses with a sparrow-hawk like Pietro Crinito, but Poliziano—he is a great, predatory eagle who would swallow me whole, feathers and marrow, without pausing to taste the difference.”

Nello chuckled, massaging the fragrant, astringent oil of orange blossoms into Tito's scalp. “We clever Florentines must constantly shift our praises, varying our spice blends lest the city’s palate grow bored. We cry down Dante and elevate Cei just to change the flavor on our tongues. But truly, you are a pattern of virtue in this wicked city, your ears double-waxed against the siren noises of the streets, devoted only to the Via de’ Bardi and the great work of your father-in-law to be. Fate seems to have carved you perfectly for the niche left empty by the old scholar's son. Who, by the way—Cronaca tells me—is now at San Marco. Did you know?”

A sudden, jarring dissonance struck the acoustic harmony of Tito's mind. The metallic taste of copper and cold ash flooded his palate, a physiological revolt against the sudden intrusion of his past. He masked the electric shock by bending to retrieve Nello’s fallen book, smoothing the dog-eared pages of *Nencia da Barberino* with trembling fingers.

“No; he was at Fiesole, I thought,” Tito managed, carefully modulating the pitch of his voice. “Are you sure he has returned to San Marco?”

“Cronaca is my authority,” Nello said with a shrug, taking the book. “Ah, my poor rustic poetry! It offends your Greek-trained ear, perhaps, but I find profound sustenance in this mountain dialect, describing a maiden with cheeks more savoury than aged pecorino. Yet, to truly understand the scent of the *contadina*, you shouldn't lie here smelling my refined perfumes. You should listen to the Fierucoloni stumping into the Piazza della Nunziata this evening.”

“And who are the Fierucoloni?” Tito asked, desperate to regulate the roaring noise in his own head.

“The traditional agrarian elders from the high forests of Pistoia and the Casentino,” Nello explained, his voice softening with respect for the foragers. “They come bearing the deep, umami-rich scent of sun-dried porcini and hand-spun yarn, honoring a seasonal cycle as old as the hills at their little Fair. But to sell their wares, they must howl their hymns to the Virgin, their ancient mountain dialects swallowed by the chaotic, commercial noise pollution of the Piazza. I avoid it; the wild, discordant clatter and flying stones are a violence against the ears. Addio.”

Tito’s visit to the Scala palace proved mercifully short, leaving a bitter, unfulfilled taste as the boy-cardinal failed to appear. The sun hung low, casting long, ancestral shadows as he walked toward the Via de’ Bardi. The abrasive hum of the city center gave way to the soothing acoustic ecology under the city walls near Borgo Pinti, where the rustle of leaves over quiet gardens provided a psychological balm. Then, to his astonishment, he saw her.

Romola advanced in a thick black veil and mantle, accompanied by the stout, grounded form of Monna Brigida. Her presence here was a rupture in the sacred domestic routine of the Bardi household. Tito tasted the immediate, sharp tang of crisis.

“Romola, has anything happened?” he asked, falling into step beside her.

Monna Brigida’s voice cut through the quiet. “We are forced to navigate the walls, Messer Tito. The peasant women and their dried mushrooms block the Nunziata. The commercial clatter is deafening, and we must reach San Marco in half the time.”

Tito’s heart became a heavy, pounding mortar against his ribs, shattering his internal quiet. “Romola,” he murmured in a low tone, “are you going to San Marco?”

They had reached the quiet stretch under the city walls, an acoustic refuge away from the fair. Romola lifted her veil to breathe the air, which smelled of warm dust and crushed grass. “Yes, Tito mio,” she said, her sad eyes seeking his. “For the first time, I am breaking the rhythm of my father’s house. It comforts me to tell you, but you must keep this from him. I left my godfather to watch over him. You remember that evening we spoke of my brother?”

“Yes,” Tito said. His mind was a chaotic kitchen, frantically trying to prep for a hundred disastrous recipes of the future, while he simultaneously devoured the sight of her, hungry for the unquestioning trust in her eyes—a sustenance he feared he was tasting for the last time. 

“He had been at Fiesole, ill,” she said, struggling against the inherent untranslatability of her conflicted grief, her words a soft, sorrowful dialect of their own. “But Maso found him at San Marco today. He is dying. I must go to him. I still remember the sweet flavor of our childhood, before I understood the harsh lexicon of my father’s betrayal.
