It costs me deeply to defy my father’s strict, classical worldview of honor. You understand, Tito, because your palate for forgiveness is broader than mine. Yet even you would find it hard to forgive one who abandoned his father when the harvest of his life's labor was most needed.”

Tito could not stomach the hypocrisy of agreeing, nor did he have the courage to offer a counter-spice of dissuasion. “You are right, my Romola; you are always right, except in thinking too well of me.” His voice carried a genuine, raw vibration, his face pale, his lip quivering like a struck lute string.

Touched by his emotional resonance, Romola’s eyes shone with the sustenance of shared feeling. “And now, Tito, I wish you to leave me. The *cugina* and I shall be less noticed if we enter the piazza alone.”

“Yes, it were better,” Monna Brigida added, her practical tone grounding the moment. “Your presence draws eyes, Messer Tito, and the commercial clatter ahead is no place for a procession. We are dressed plainly to blend with the Piagnoni, for if the dead hold grudges, what use is living? Make them feel while they live, say I.”

Tito raised Romola’s hand to his lips, inhaling the faint, clean scent of her skin. “I understand, I obey you.” He lifted his cap—a respectful gesture of his Greek heritage that the dominant Florentine lexicon often misread as fawning, though to Romola it was a delicacy she savored. 

Dismissed, Tito was left adrift. He knew the devastating, interwoven timelines of fate were closing in. Fra Luca would demand to know Romola's future. The untranslatable secret of Tito’s past—the adoptive father he had left in slavery, the stolen gems—would be served up in plain, undeniable words. His carefully cultivated harvest in Florence would rot. Powerful elders like Bernardo del Nero would feast on his ruin, and his identity as a respectable scholar would be stripped away. 

Fevered, his acoustic equilibrium utterly shattered, Tito abandoned his visit to Bardo. He wandered aimlessly through the fading amber light, his steps drawn inevitably toward the Via San Sebastiano and the roaring, commercial cacophony of the Piazza dell’ Annunziata. Stripped of his culinary and social armor, overwhelmed by the sensory assault of a pathway suddenly closed, he surrendered to the chaotic noise, ready to consume whatever desperate, immediate purpose the night might offer.

The acoustic environment of the piazza burst upon Tito as a dissonant assault, a chaotic tapestry of sound that grated against the mind’s natural rhythms. He pushed his way into the Fierucoloni, finding a strange, numbing solace in the sheer volume of the hooting and elbowing. It was a sensory overload that temporarily drowned out his dreary calculations of the future, a future where he foresaw himself wandering away solitary in pursuit of some unknown fortune, perhaps even looping back in search of Baldassarre. 

At every inlet, the cyclical tide of humanity surged into the square beneath paper lanterns that waved like captive stars. A rude, monotonous chant from the country folk wove a distinctly traceable, ancient strand of noise, but it was violently overlaid by the screeching whistles, the harsh beating of drums, and the ringing of little bells—an abrasive acoustic ecology that threatened psychological equilibrium. Fitfully, a lantern would vanish with the smash of a hurled stone, followed by the jagged phonemes of boyish screams. Yet, on the outskirts of this swirling, commercialized tumult, deeper, multi-generational rhythms held fast to the vigil of the Nativity of the Virgin.

Here, the mythic seamlessly integrated into the domestic. Ragged men rattled their tongues in aggressive, dominant lexicons to lure country people into rigged games; masquerading figures on stilts swung lanterns in meteoric arcs. Most grounding was the dense, yeasty aroma billowing from a small covered stall doing brisk trade in hot *berlingozzi*. The olfactory landscape was heavy with the scent of this beloved farinaceous delicacy—sweet dough enriched with fresh egg yolks, twisted into rings, and baked to a golden, caramelized crust that radiated the warm, toasted notes of milled wheat and crushed aniseed. To eat such a pastry was to consume the agrarian history of the region.

Tito found himself swept against a trotting procession of barefooted, hard-heeled contadine. Their sun-dried, bronzed faces and obsolete, earth-dyed garments made them appear as a way-worn ancestry returning from a pilgrimage begun centuries ago. These were the scant-feeding peasant-women from the mountains of Pistoia. They bore on their backs moderate bundles of yarn—the tactile culmination of a year’s domestic labor—bringing the fruits of their agrarian routines to the Blessed Virgin. In their hearts rested a wide, dim fear of harm and a quiet hope, seeking the miraculous intercession of an angelic image whose protective potency was to stream forth on this Eve of her Nativity. As they spoke among themselves, Tito heard the fading inflections of their native mountain dialects, a linguistic heritage slowly eroding beneath the hegemonic weight of city speech, carrying subjective, earth-bound experiences that remained wholly untranslatable to the urban ear.

Forced toward the piazza's boundary, Tito encountered his acquaintance Bratti, who had judiciously placed himself away from the central din. Bratti stood against a pillar, his mouth pursed in disdainful silence, acting as a meticulous hoarder of material histories. His hand rested firmly on a serge cloth concealing his basket of wares. Two women approached with a look of curiosity, and Bratti, weaponizing his dominant urban lexicon to establish an immediate power dynamic, drew the cover tighter.

“Before I answer that, Monna, I must know whether you mean to buy,” Bratti said. “I can’t show such wares as mine in this fair for every fly to settle on and pay nothing. My goods are a little too choice for that. Besides, I’ve only two left, and with the chances of the pestilence that wise men talk of, there is likelihood of their being worth their weight in gold. No, no: *andate con Dio*.”

The two women exchanged a look, their native dialect silenced by his sharp Florentine commerce. “And what may be the price?” the second asked.

“Not within what you are likely to have in your purse, *buona donna*,” Bratti replied in a compassionately supercilious tone. “I recommend you to trust in Messer Domeneddio and the saints: poor people can do no better for themselves.”

“Not so poor!” the woman retorted indignantly, drawing out her money-bag. “Come, now! what do you say to a *grosso*?”

Through a calculated dance of mercantile coquetry, Bratti finally raised the cover. “Since you’ve set your mind on it, I should be loth to do you a mischief; for Maestro Gabbadeo used to say, when a woman sets her mind on a thing and doesn’t get it, she’s in worse danger of the pestilence than before. *Ecco!*” 

The precious objects were two clumsy iron rings, beaten into the fashion of old Roman artifacts, disinterred from the layered soil of antiquity. Their rust and hidden mythic potency were so deeply satisfactory to the agrarian women that the heavy coins were paid, and the remainder settled with a barter of hand-spun yarn. 

Covering his basket, Bratti turned and caught sight of Tito. “By the head of San Giovanni, now,” Bratti said, drawing him back to the pillar. “This is a piece of luck... talking of rings, it is your ring—that very ring you’ve got on your finger—that I could get you a purchaser for; ay, and a purchaser with a deep money-bag. A Genoese who is going straight away into Hungary. He came rummaging through all the shops to see if I had any old things I didn't know the price of. I told him I knew a fine young man who had a ring of that sort.”

Tito listened, the cyclical nature of his past pressing upon him. Why keep the ring? It was a talisman of an interwoven timeline, taken from Baldassarre’s finger and placed upon his own when his hand had grown to the needful size. He felt a sudden, profound desire to sever himself from this artifact of his personal history, to escape the recognition that was a relentless claim from the past.

“You speak with your usual wisdom, Bratti,” Tito said, suppressing the physical memory of the ring’s cool metal. “When and where shall I have speech of him?”

“To-morrow, at three hours after sunrise, at my shop,” Bratti replied, assuring Tito he should demand a heavy price, before complaining of the fair's dullness now that his goods were sold.

Tito moved away, carried by the zigzagging current of the crowd into a deep recess where the superficial commercialization of art and faith was in full display. A man in a white mitre adorned with yellow cabalistic figures stood elevated in stark light against the dim, waving lanterns. It was Maestro Vaiano, a mountebank Tito recognized. The man’s voice possessed a peculiarly sharp, penetrating ring—an acoustic pollution that shattered the acoustic ecology of the night.

“Behold, my children!” the conjuror shouted, peddling cheap matrimonies and fake indulgences from Pope Alexander the Sixth. “Spit once, my sons, and pay a white quattrino!” 

The scene was a grotesque degradation of the sacred, reduced to mere buffoonery and a transactional exchange of copper. A monkey, tied to the altar-like table, fidgeted with a taper while an impish boy in a surplice cuffed the animal. The linguistic power dynamic here was absolute: the conjuror’s rapid-fire, cynical patter overrode the simple awe of the crowd, offering them tiny bags of *Brevi* with secret charms. When a youth named Mariotto stepped up with his plump brunette bride, the tension snapped. The abused monkey, tired of the boy's over-active discipline, leaped with such violence that its slackened knot gave way. It bounded onto the head of a tall woman, shrieking and chattering.

Amidst the screaming and the chaotic disruption of the commercial spell, Tito slipped out of the circle. Craving auditory relief, he allowed himself to be pushed into the church of the Nunziata.

The transition across the threshold was a revelation in acoustic ecology. The hellish hubbub of the piazza dissolved into a resonant, uninterrupted Gregorian chant from the choir—a continuous sonic vibration that offered instant repose to the battered ear. Visually, the brilliant yellow splendor of silver lamps pressed upon his eyes, a heavenly illumination for peasant-women whose mountain skies were often obscured. The olfactory landscape was profoundly layered: the purifying, citrus-and-pine sharpness of frankincense and melting beeswax mingled intimately with the coarse, musky scent of rain-washed wool and the sweat of bronzed mountain dwellers. 

Above the worshippers, a multi-generational saga was physically suspended. The walls and ceilings were crowded with votive waxen images—effigies of Florentines in black silk *lucco*, popes, emperors, and famous *condottieri*. Centuries of history were wedged together, their faces blackened by time or fresh in new steel corselets. It was a resurrection-swarm of remote mortals, a frozen timeline watching over the cyclical rituals of the living.

Tito’s glance wandered the wild multitude until he found her. Tessa was kneeling just inside the doorway, her head hung wearily, blue eyes absently fixed on an altar-piece of the Archangel Michael. Her right hand listlessly gripped a bunch of silkworm cocoons arranged with fortuitous regularity on a stem. Her lips pouted; she was a large image of a sweet, sleepy child, entirely uncorrupted by the calculating lexicons of the city. To Tito, her loving, ignorant soul offered a refuge from his impending isolation, a world where subjective experience didn't require translation or justification.

He slipped quietly to her side and knelt. “Tessa!”

She turned, her face blooming into a happy, silent smile. “My little Tessa! you look very tired. How long have you been kneeling here?”

She collected her thoughts. “I’m very hungry.”

“Come, then; come with me.” He lifted her from the stone floor and led her to the open cloisters. 

“The Madre is ill,” Tessa explained, her voice carrying the musical, unhurried cadence of her people. “She sent me to bring these cocoons to the Santissima Nunziata, because they’re so wonderful. She thinks the Holy Madonna may take away her pains. But somebody took my bag with the bread and chestnuts in it...”

The loss of her food was not a mere utilitarian deprivation; she had been robbed of her daily sustenance, the dense, hearth-baked bread and the sweet, fire-roasted mountain chestnuts that rooted her to the earth and the seasons. 

“Yes, my little Tessa, you shall take them,” Tito promised, understanding the profound need for culinary comfort. “But first come and let me give you some *berlingozzi*. There are some to be had not far off.”

“Where did you come from?” she asked, bewildered. “I thought you would never come to the Mercato for milk any more. I set myself Aves to say, to see if they would bring you back...”

“You see I come when you want some one to take care of you, Tessa. But what shall you do if you are here all alone? Where shall you go?”

“Oh, I shall stay and sleep in the church,” she murmured, accepting her place in the ancient, circular rhythm of the harvest festival. “A great many of them do... and the *patrigno* is coming with the mules in the morning.”

When Romola stepped through the heavy oak doors of San Marco, the vibrant, nourishing soundscape of Florence—the rhythmic clatter of market carts, the rich, overlapping dialects of vendors hawking salted anchovies and fresh figs—was abruptly severed. In its place settled the oppressive, sterile acoustic ecology of the cloister, a suffocating quiet that seemed to vacuum the very marrow from the air. Monna Brigida was ushered into the adjoining church, leaving Romola to be guided alone toward the chapter-house. 

The corridor smelled faintly of boiled cabbage and damp, unyielding stone, a utilitarian scent devoid of the warmth that transforms mere sustenance into a shared communion. When the chapter-house door swung open, the dim light of two sputtering tapers illuminated a truckle-bed. There lay the emaciated face of Fra Luca. His golden hair was shorn into a tonsured crown, and his deep-sunken hazel eyes were fixed with desperate hunger upon a small crucifix. Another monk stood in the room’s acoustic shadow, his black cowl drawn, silent as a resting pestle. Behind them both, a frescoed crucifixion loomed, its painted faces of sorrow peering out from the plaster like ghosts trapped in a forgotten tongue.

Romola’s eyes met Fra Luca’s, and a visceral pang of recognition seized her. Beneath the ash-grey pallor and the rigid monkish asceticism lay the underlying architecture of her beautiful young brother. 

“Dino!” she cried. The name tore from her throat in a low, ragged pitch—a sound stripped of all polite artifice, vibrating with the raw, untranslatable dialect of childhood grief. 

Yet she held herself erect, anchoring her feet two yards from his bed. To her, his monkish robes were the coarse garments of a devastating betrayal. Dino had abandoned their blind father, starving Bardo of the son’s voice that should have been his daily bread. Their father had raised them on a profound, sensory appreciation for the ancients—a life where philosophy was not abstract, but possessed the tactile weight of cured parchment, the rich, slow-simmered complexity of Greek syntax, and the grounded, agrarian simplicity of a meal shared with honest hands. The Church, to Romola, was an institution that demanded the absolute starvation of the human senses, replacing the vibrant, native lexicons of family and earth with a dominant, flattened vocabulary of groveling superstition.

But Dino’s face remained a mask of hollow starvation. He looked at her not with the warmth of a brother who remembers the scent of roasting chestnuts in their winter hearth, but with the detached gaze of a spirit untethered from the flesh.

“My sister,” he said. His voice was a feeble, interrupted rasp, a dying language losing its final speaker. “It is well thou hast not longer delayed to come, for I have a message to deliver to thee, and my time is short.”

Romola took a step closer, her heart softening like butter in a warm pan. Surely, this message would be one of savory penitence. He would ask of their father’s blindness—how the dark days were measured by the tolling bells, what simple meals now sustained them in the home where he himself had been nourished on olive oil and ancient poetry. At the very end of his life, there would be a return to their shared vocabulary of love.

“Romola,” Fra Luca began, “I have had a vision concerning thee. Thrice I have had it in the last two months. I came from Fiesole, deeming it a message from heaven... and I gather a promise of mercy in this, that my breath is preserved in order to—”

A fit of ragged, difficult breathing severed his sentence. Romola’s heart chilled, the sudden frost killing the tender shoots of her pity. A vision. This was the same bitter, abstract root that had poisoned his mind years ago. The simmering indignation within her boiled over.

“Dino, I thought you had words to send to my father. You forsook him when his sight was failing; you stripped his life bare. Have you never repented? What is this religion of yours, that feasts on visions while starving natural duties?”

His sunken eyes shifted slowly to her. “No,” he answered, his tone flat and passionless, a chilling frequency that lacked any human resonance. “I have never repented fleeing from the stifling poison-breath of sin that threatened to steal over my senses like besotting wine. My father could not hear the voice that called me; he knew nothing of the demon-tempters. He lived like one busy picking shining stones in a mine, while a world died of plague above him. The studies he wished me to live for were dead toys, made warm only by worldly ambitions and fleshly lusts.”

“Has not my father led a pure and noble life?” Romola demanded, her voice rich with the vibrato of deep, bodily conviction. “He has sought no worldly honors. He has denied himself all luxuries, living like the ancient sages! He wished you to live according to the purest maxims of philosophy, sustained by the honest truths in which he brought you up.” 

“What were the maxims of philosophy to me?” Dino’s eyes remained strange, passionless hollows. “They told me to be strong when I was weak. The Divine love penetrated me, a seed demanding room to grow. I felt a life of perfect purity where there would be no uneasy hunger after pleasure. To attain that, I had to forsake the world. I had to turn away with loathing from the scent of the emptied cup.” He paused, his chest heaving, struggling to process the heavy air. “I saw that even among the servants of the Cross, my soul would be stifled with the fumes of hypocrisy. God called upon me to flee to lands where want and scorn bore me to repose. My task has been short. I came back to Florence that—”

“Dino, you *did* want to know if my father was alive,” Romola pleaded, yearning to bridge the untranslatable gulf between their lived experiences.

“—That before I died I might urge others to study the Eastern tongues,” he pressed on, erasing her interruption with the steamroller of his fervor, “and I have felt I was sent partly to thee, not to renew earthly bonds, but to deliver a heavenly warning. In the painful linking of our waking thoughts, we mingle our own error with the light, but in visions, our souls are passive instruments. Listen, for the time is short.”

Romola recoiled. The acoustic distance between them was widening into an unbridgeable chasm. But as she pulled away, the second monk stepped to the opposite side of the bed.

“Kneel, my daughter, for the Angel of Death is present, and waits while the message of heaven is delivered: bend thy pride before it is bent for thee by a yoke of iron.”

The voice was a profound shock to the room’s ecology. It was a strong, rich baritone, vibrating with an undeniable psychological gravity. It possessed the quiet, resonant self-assurance of a man accustomed to defining the lexicon of an entire city. Romola looked at him. The cowl hid his face, but his hands, resting against his black mantle, were exquisitely sensitive, almost transparently delicate. Romola’s instinct was to rebel against the arrogant acoustics of monastic command, but those hands seemed to carry a tactile appeal against all hardness. 

The monk reached out with his right hand to relieve Dino’s fatigued grasp on the crucifix, while his left touched Dino’s cracked lips with a wet sponge—a fleeting, utilitarian gesture of hydration. As he bent, the cowl fell back. The tapers illuminated a high arched nose, a prominent under-lip, and blue-grey eyes shining beneath auburn lashes. It was Fra Girolamo Savonarola, the Prior of San Marco. Romola had previously dismissed him as an architect of spiritual noise pollution, a man whose commercialized sermons battered the ears of the Florentine public. Her rebellion flared at the sight of him.

“Kneel, my daughter,” the penetrating voice rolled out again, expertly calibrated to bypass her defenses. “The pride of the body is a barrier against the gifts that purify the soul.”

Beneath the sheer sonic pressure of his presence, Romola felt her proud erectness dissolve. Slowly, trembling, she sank to the stone floor. In her new state of passivity, her brother began to speak, his words carving out a landscape of profound starvation.

“Romola, in the deep night, I saw my father’s library. I saw you sitting before his chair. And at the leggio stood a man whose face I could not see—it was a blank, like an effaced painting. He took you by the hand, and you took my father, and together you went down the stone steps. You stood at the altar in Santa Croce, married by a priest with the face of death. The dead rose in their shrouds and followed you like a bridal train.”

Romola shuddered. The imagery tasted of ash.

“You passed into the valley,” Dino whispered, his breath catching. “And at last you came to a stony place. There was no water, no trees, no herbage to sustain you. Instead of water, written parchment unrolled everywhere. Instead of trees, men of bronze and marble crowded round. My father was faint for want of water and fell. The blank-faced man departed, and I saw his true face—the Great Tempter. You wrung your hands, Romola, seeking water, seeking life, but there was none. The bronze and marble figures mocked you, holding out cups of water, but when you put them to my father’s lips, the liquid turned to dry, choking parchment. The figures turned to demons and snatched his body. The parchments shriveled up, blood ran everywhere, and fire upon the blood, until the plain was bare and stony again. Thrice I have had that vision. It warns thee against marriage as a temptation of the enemy... it calls upon thee to dedicate thyself—”

A severe fit of gasping seized him. He stared at the crucifix, his lungs failing to draw nourishment from the damp air. Finally, a barely audible thread of sound emerged: “To renounce the vain philosophy and corrupt thoughts of the heathens... for in the hour of sorrow... the unclean gods will—”

The words died away. A strange awe settled over Romola. She possessed a vivid intellect and a healthy human passion that savored the tangible world, harboring no morbid cravings for the exceptional. Yet the vision’s cruel, anti-gustatory imagery—the transformation of life-giving water into dry, dusty parchment—jarred her like a physical blow. This brother, returning as a starved apparition, was dying before her eyes, and she had never before witnessed the ultimate failure of the human vessel.

“Frate,” the dying voice rattled.

Fra Girolamo leaned down. Silence expanded in the room, thick and untranslatable.

“Romola,” the breath whispered.

She leaned forward, desperate for the final word, for some crumb of reconciliation. But the words struggled in vain against his failing throat.

“Fra Girolamo, give her—”

“The crucifix,” Savonarola’s rich voice supplied, seamlessly inserting the dominant lexicon into the void of Dino’s failing dialect.

No other sound came from the dying lips.

“Dino!” Romola cried, a piercing wail of realization. The silence of misunderstanding was now locked in stone; the shared cultural memory of their childhood would never be spoken again.

“Take the crucifix, my daughter,” Savonarola said after a heavy pause. “His eyes behold it no more.”

Romola reached out and grasped the cold metal. The tactile reality of it broke the tension in her chest, and a great, ragged sob burst from her. She bowed her head against her dead brother’s bed and wept aloud, the sound cutting through the cloister's sterile quiet.

“Rise, my daughter, and be comforted,” Savonarola commanded gently, having already called for a lay Brother. “He has left you the crucifix, in remembrance of the heavenly warning—that it may be a beacon to you in the darkness.”

Trembling, she rose, hiding the crucifix beneath her mantle and folding her veil over her head. Savonarola led her out into the cloistered court, lit only by the cold stars and a servant's lantern. A group of dignified laity murmured near the entrance, their rustling garments and low voices a jarring intrusion of the outside world.

“Our brother is departed,” Savonarola murmured to them. 

The heavy doors opened, and Romola stepped out into the wide piazza of San Marco with Monna Brigida. The sudden rush of open space and the faint, lingering scent of woodsmoke and damp earth rushed into her lungs, reviving her. The horrific, starved landscape of Dino's vision clung to her palate like a bitter resin, making her yearn all the more fiercely for the warm, living voices of her father and Tito—voices that promised the rich, sensory sustenance of her waking life.

Tito, however, was not with Bardo. His destiny was already being kneaded by a guilty consciousness, shaped by the despairing belief that Romola now possessed the knowledge that would separate them forever. But the lips that could have poured out that bitter truth were sealed. The prophetic words Dino had offered came from an arid, shadowy region devoid of human sympathy, while the deep, nourishing revelation that might have flowed from simple filial affection had been swallowed into an irrevocable, untranslatable silence.

Early the next morning, Tito was returning from Bratti’s shop in the narrow, shadowed thoroughfare of the Ferravecchi. The Genoese stranger had carried away the onyx ring, and Tito carried away fifty florins in its stead. The heavy coins clinked against his thigh, a jarring, metallic intrusion upon the delicate morning acoustic ecology of Florence. It did cross his mind that if the cyclical wheel of Fortune somehow saved him from quitting the city, it would have been better not to have parted with a ring bound to the peculiar, untranslatable memories of his Greek childhood—a native lexicon of love and loss that was already eroding under the weight of his new Tuscan life. But in those moments of feverish regret, he had lost his confidence in Fortune. His deeds felt like heavy, indigestible seeds planted deep within his gut; they were children born to him that lived and acted apart from his will. The dreadful, enduring vitality of those deeds pressed hard upon him, a bitter harvest he had never meant to sow.

He was going back to his lodgings in the Piazza di San Giovanni, but he deliberately avoided the Mercato Vecchio. He could not bear the risk of seeing Tessa, nor did he possess the stomach for the abrasive clatter and harsh haggling of the meat stalls. He sought only the comforting, rhythmic patterns of the city’s heart to await the first sign of his altering lot. 

The piazza, bathed in the warm, golden light of autumn dew, functioned as a living tapestry of multi-generational memory. It was a festival morning, and the olfactory landscape was a profound invitation to linger: the deep, yeasty perfume of schiacciata baking in distant wood-fired ovens mingled with the sharp, verdant tang of crushed fennel and the sweet musk of ripe figs spread meticulously over the vendors’ stalls. The sounds were a harmonious, grounding symphony—laden mules rhythmically chewing their nose-bags of stone-ground oats, the soft, barefooted patter of a round-capped baby trying the warmed marble of the Duomo steps, the satisfying *shhh-shhh* of a cherry-stone being carved by a pale cripple, and the melodic, undulating cadence of ancient Florentine dialect passing between laughing men on the wide platform.

But the largest company of loungers was gathered toward Nello’s barbershop. In this warm season, the deft, percussive stropping of Nello’s razor under the red and white awning provided the rhythmic heartbeat of the piazza. It was the hour when yesterday’s crop of gossip was freshest, deeply rooted in the soil of the city’s collective memory. Tito foresaw he must be drawn into the circle; he must smile and retort in the dominant lexicon of local wit. It was but the ordeal of swallowing bread-and-cheese pills, though as a man who found profound sensory meaning in the crusty bite of artisan *pane sciocco* and the rich, aged funk of true pecorino, Tito resented the corruption of genuine sustenance into a mere metaphor for deceit. 

Just then, a hand laid heavily on his shoulder. The dull thud broke the plaza’s sonic harmony, jarring Tito’s nerves. His face betrayed his inward shock, but the owner of the hand broke into a light laugh. It was Niccolò Macchiavelli, a young man of Tito’s age, with keen features and a close-clipped head, his eyes bright with the pleasant predictions of mending his ancient family's fortunes.

“Why, Melema, what evil dream did you have last night, that you took my light grasp for that of a *sbirro* or something worse?”

“Ah, Messer Niccolò!” said Tito, recovering the smooth cadence of his voice. “It must have been an extra amount of dulness in my veins this morning that shuddered at the approach of your wit. But the fact is, I have had a bad night.”

“That is unlucky, because you will be expected to shine without any obstructing fog to-day in the Rucellai Gardens.”

“Messer Bernardo did me the honour to invite me,” said Tito, “but I shall be engaged elsewhere.”

“Ah! I remember, you are in love,” said Macchiavelli, with a shrug. “Why, we are to feast profoundly under the loggia among Bernardo Rucellai’s rare trees. We are to eat ortolans trapped in the vineyards and slow-roasted over fragrant olive-wood embers until their fat renders into a perfect, savory glaze, and peacock dressed in its own iridescent plumage, stuffed with sweet chestnuts and wild thyme. There are to be the choicest spirits and the most meticulously cellared wines. Only, as Piero de’ Medici is to be there, the sensory reverence of the feast may happen to be swamped in the capping of impromptu verses. I hate that game; it is a superficial commercialization of our language, a device for the triumph of small wits who reduce the profound poetry of our native tongue to a meaningless clatter.”

“What is that you are saying about Piero de’ Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?” said Nello, whose light figure predominated over the Herculean frame of Niccolò Caparra. 

The famous worker in iron, a traditional elder whose massive, muscular arms usually swung in time with the roaring hearths of his smithy, sat submissively while Nello lathered his chin. Caparra looked much like a lion preparing for society, though his soul belonged to the soot and the anvil. 

“A private secretary will never rise in the world if he couples great and small in that way,” continued Nello. “When great men are not allowed to marry their sons and daughters as they like, small men must not expect to marry their words as they like. Have you heard the news Domenico Cennini has been telling us? Pagolantonio Soderini has given Ser Piero da Bibbiena a box on the ear for interfering with the marriage between young Tommaso Soderini and Fiammetta Strozzi, and is to be sent ambassador to Venice as a punishment!”

“I don’t know which I envy him most,” said Macchiavelli. “The offence will make him the most popular man in Florence, and the punishment will take him among the only people in Italy who have known how to manage their own affairs.”

“Yes, if Soderini stays long enough at Venice,” said Cennini, “he may chance to learn the Venetian fashion. What has happened is likely to open Pagolantonio’s eyes to the good of our old Florentine trick of choosing a new harness when the old one galls us.”

“Not we,” said Niccolò Caparra, his deep, resonant voice vibrating with agrarian wisdom. “Eat eggs in Lent and the snow will melt. That’s what I say to our people when they get noisy over their cups at San Gallo, and talk of raising a *romor*. I say, never do you plan an insurrection; you cannot rush the seasons. You may as well try to fill Arno with buckets. When there’s water enough, the Arno will be full, and that will not be till the torrent is ready.”

“Caparra, that oracular speech of yours is due to my excellent shaving,” said Nello. “Ecco, Messer Domenico, I am ready for you now. By the way, my *bel erudito*,” continued Nello, noting Tito moving toward the door, “here has been old Maso seeking for you. The old man looked mournful. I hope there is nothing wrong in the Via de’ Bardi.”

“Doubtless Messer Tito knows that Bardo’s son is dead,” said Cronaca, who had just stepped into the fragrant shade of the awning.

Tito’s heart gave a leap—had the death happened before Romola saw him? 

“No, I had not heard it,” Tito said, maintaining a steady exterior while leaning against the doorpost. “I knew his sister had gone to see him. Did he die before she arrived?”

“No,” said Cronaca. “I was in San Marco, and saw her come out from the chapter-house with Fra Girolamo, who told us that the dying man’s breath had been preserved as by a miracle, that he might make a disclosure to his sister.”

Tito felt that his fate was decided. The intricate timeline of his deceptions was collapsing. He conceived a sudden plan: if he could retrieve his money from Cennini before the untranslatable grief of that disclosure became public, he could escape the scorching looks of the city. He stood motionless, anchoring himself in the scent of bruised herbs drifting from the stalls. 

“So you have got your Fra Girolamo back again, Cronaca?” Nello asked. “I suppose we shall have him preaching this next Advent.”

“And not before there is need,” said Cronaca gravely. “The ripeness of vice is turning to rottenness in the nostrils even of the vicious. The harvest of sin is ripe, and God will reap it with a sword.”

“I hope he has had a new vision,” sneered Francesco Cei. “The old ones are somewhat stale. Can’t your Frate get a poet to help out his imagination?”

“He has no lack of poets about him,” Cronaca countered, “but they do not think the truth stale which God has given him to utter, any more than they think the cycles of the moon are stale.” 

“Nay, Francesco,” Macchiavelli intervened gently. “You judge his preaching too narrowly. The secret of oratory lies in a certain power that moves the hearers. Fra Girolamo speaks a language that bypasses the sterile intellect and strikes at the bone.”

“Part of the secret lies in the prophetic visions,” said Cennini from the chair. “Our people will run after anything that prophesies tribulations.”

“Rather say,” answered Cronaca, “that the secret lies in the Frate’s pure life, which stamps him as a messenger of God.”

“He is satisfied with the pleasant lust of arrogance!” Cei burst out. “He hears the air filled with his own name!”

“Come, come, Francesco,” Nello conciliated, whipping up a fresh, soapy lather. “I must not have my friend Cronaca made angry. For my own part, when the Frate was preaching last Advent, I might have turned Piagnone too, if I had not been hindered by the liberal nature of my art, and the exhausting length of his sermons. But as Pulci says: ‘Dombruno’s scimitar had the fame of being enchanted; but I am of opinion that it cut sharp because it was of strongly-tempered steel.’”

“See, Nello!” Macchiavelli interrupted, pointing. “What doctor is this advancing on his Bucephalus? I thought your piazza was free from those furred and scarlet-robed lackeys of death.”

An arrhythmic, violent clatter of hooves shattered the square's acoustic harmony. A round-headed, round-bodied personage was approaching on a raw young horse, which fought the bit with a jarring obstinacy that grated against the tranquil morning soundscape. 

“A–ah!” Nello drew out the vowel low and long. “I have a few adventures in pickle for him. He’s a doctor from Padua. His great trick is making rounds among the *contadini*. Do you note those great saddle-bags? They are to hold the fat capons, the golden-yolked eggs, and the stone-ground meal he levies from the agrarian elders who truly know how to work the soil. He steals their hard-earned sustenance to vend his secret medicines, making my piazza a resort for asthmas and squalling *bambini*. It stirs my gall to see the toad-faced quack bagging a prized pigeon in exchange for his powders. I’ll put a few thorns in his saddle. Here, Sandro!”

Nello whispered into the ear of his apprentice, who nodded solemnly and darted away.

“How is it with you, Maestro Tacco?” Nello called out as the doctor wrestled his horse toward the shop.

“He is an accursed beast, the *vermocane* seize him!” Maestro Tacco spat in a crude, dominant jargon that lacked any of the poetic nuance of the local dialect, tying the frayed bridle to an iron staple. 

“Rather too hard riding for a man who carries your weight of learning, eh, Maestro?” said Nello. 

“Truly, I am hot,” the doctor grumbled, wiping his wide, lipless mouth. “Riding from Peretola when the sun is high is not like kicking your heels on a bench in the shade. I had not a little pulling to get through the carts to find the husband of a certain Monna Ghita, who had a fatal seizure before I was called in; and if I had not to demand my fees—”

“Monna Ghita!” said Nello. “Peace be with her angry soul!” 

Tito, roused from his internal calculations, felt a sudden rush of thought regarding Tessa: with Monna Ghita taken from the earthly harvest, Tessa was utterly untethered. 

“*Gnaffè*, Maestro,” Nello went on sympathizingly. “You think my shaving will cool you? One moment. It seems to me a thousand years till I wait upon a man who carries all the science of Arabia in his saddle-bags.”

Nello held up the shaving-cloth. Maestro Tacco, preoccupied with his own self-importance, sat down, deaf to the artisan’s irony. 

“It is fitting that a great *medicus* like you,” Nello said, “should be shaved by the razor that has shaved the illustrious Antonio Benevieni, the greatest master of the chirurgic art.”

“The chirurgic art!” Tacco interrupted in a burst of academic arrogance. “Is it your Florentine fashion to put the masters of medicine on a level with men who do carpentry on limbs and carve excrescences as a butcher trims meat? *Via!* A manual art, practiced by simple barbers, on a level with the noble science of Hippocrates, Galen, and Avicenna—a science locked up from the vulgar!” 

Here was the very erosion of cultural memory Tacco championed: dismissing the hands-on, sensory-rich wisdom of the common people for sterile, untranslatable academic dogma. 

“I never thought of placing them on a level,” Nello said, deliberately dragging the lathering process out. “But your high science is sealed from the profane, and so you become an object of slander. I grieve to say it, but there are low fellows here—*sgherri* who make it their business to sprinkle gall in a prospering man’s broth. There are fables buzzed about concerning you. I would not have a man of your metal stoned.”

“What fables?” stammered Maestro Tacco. “What do you mean?”

“The fact is, there is a company of evil youths possessed with a diabolical patience. It is commonly talked of that you have been seen making your secret specifics by night: pounding dried toads in a mortar, compounding a salve out of mashed worms, and making pills from the dried livers of rats—a vile corruption of the mortar and pestle that should only know basil, garlic, and coarse salt! And they say you mix it with saliva during a blasphemous incantation.”

“It is a pack of lies!” the doctor exclaimed, struggling against the approaching razor. 

“It is not to us you need say that,” Nello whispered ominously. “But what are reasonable men against a crowd with stones in their hands? They are persuaded you are a necromancer selling pestilence, and I am told your specifics have, in truth, an evil smell.”

“It is false!” Tacco burst out, highly defensive of his olfactory landscape. “I will show the pills and the salve to these honourable signori—it has an excellent odour!” 

He started up with the lather on his chin to search his saddle-bags for the belied medicines. In an instant, Nello adroitly shifted the shaving-chair into the close vicinity of the horse’s head, while Sandro, who had just returned, placed himself silently near the bridle, ready to unleash the perfectly timed mechanics of a Florentine joke. 

“Behold, Messeri!” said the doctor, bringing forth a small box of medicines and opening it before them.

The loggia at the pinnacle of Bardo’s house rose high above the suffocating, mercantile clatter of Florence, forming a quadrangular sanctuary where the cyclical rhythms of the seasons felt profoundly intimate. On the street side, classical columns supported the roof, while the rear wall, pierced by arched openings, offered Romola a sheltered promenade. Down below lay a dense sprawl of irregular dwellings, from which the sharp, metallic shrieks of industrial coinage and the grating grind of commercial mills rose like a caustic vapor. But up here, the acoustic ecology was softened by the gentle, rhythmic rustle of the wind sweeping toward the hill of Bogoli, carrying with it the grounding, everyday scents of the agrarian city: the yeasty breath of sourdough fermenting in communal ovens, the sharp tang of wild fennel, and the rich, roasting aroma of garlic bulbs blistering over open fires. 

Near one of the arched openings, bathed in a sickening slant of afternoon sunlight, Tito awaited her. He inhaled the ambient scent of sun-baked terracotta and crushed thyme, seeking an anchor for his fraying nerves. He had never relied on Romola’s passion to withstand the bitter tannins of his secret; he knew her love grew from the same deep, untarnished root as her belief in him. Yet, as he imagined her approaching in her radiant, tactile beauty—made so devastatingly mortal by the soft hazel of her eyes—he found himself wishing she were woven of coarser, simpler cloth. If she belonged to a lesser lineage, perhaps she would just let him clasp her, to consume the sweetness of her mouth before they parted. Their courtship had thus far been starved of sensory feast; he had known only the briefest appetizers—a lingering glance, the fleeting pressure of a hand, a chaste kiss. Now, they were to be alone, but he envisioned her standing inexorably aloof, a marble statue beyond the reach of human hunger.

His heart executed a heavy, percussive throb as the heavy oak door groaned on its iron hinges. Romola was there. It happened with the suddenness of a lightning strike: he felt, before he saw, the luminous grief surrounding her. He registered the untranslatable resonance of her cry—a raw, native dialect stripped of the dominant, polished Florentine lexicon—when she wept, “Tito!” 

In the next breath, she was in his arms, her sobs vibrating against his chest, a pure, unadulterated sound of sorrow that broke the quiet harmony of the loggia. How she had yearned through the dark, slow-simmering watches of the night to see his bright face. The mythic intrusion of death; the bewildering doubt infused into her by a brother whose life had become a forgotten language to her; the haunting vision that she had not only heard in Dino’s low, gasping breaths but had tasted, bitter as gall, as if it were her own. All of it made her acutely aware that it was Tito who had first brought the warm, nourishing broth of hope to her lips, Tito who had first blunted the keen, cutting edge of her familial grief. She needed to pour it all out to him; he was the only vessel left. For hours she had restrained her grief before her father, but now, the long-fermented sob finally burst forth. Proud and meticulously controlled before the world, Romola was as unguarded as a child in her hunger for Tito’s warmth. 

“My Romola! My goddess!” Tito murmured, his voice a rich, resonant hum against her ear. He clasped her gently, burying his face in the thick, golden ripples of her neck, breathing in the scent of sweet almond oil and warm wheat. He was in an earthly paradise. Disgrace, shame, the looming famine of parting—the fear of them evaporated. This sudden gustatory rush of happiness was too potent to be soured by the knowledge that Romola was consuming a grand deception; rather, he savored the sweetness of her delusion. The concealment had been the necessary yeast for this perfect loaf. 

When the sobbing finally spent itself, Romola raised her head. They stood in a suspended silence, enveloped in the sweet, sensory wonder of young love—her long, pale hands resting on his dark-brown curls, his olive fingers bathed in the streaming gold of her hair. The cold, heavy stone of sadness in Romola’s chest made her linger in that quiet acoustic space, drinking in the soothing proximity. Tito refrained from claiming her lips, fearing any sudden movement might shatter the delicate flavor of the moment.

“Tito,” she breathed at last, her voice a fragile timber, “it has been altogether painful, but I must tell you everything. Your vitality will help me digest these impressions that reason alone cannot metabolize.”

“I know, Romola—I know he is dead,” said Tito. His lustrous, dark eyes betrayed nothing of the secret wishes that would have hastened that death years ago, had mere desires possessed such potency. Romola only saw her own pure reflections in his gaze, reading his silence as one reads a beloved recipe in a happy dream.

“He was so changed, Tito! It pierced me to recognize Dino beneath that starved asceticism. And so strangely hard: not a word for my father, nothing but a vision he insisted on translating to me. Yet it was so piteous—the struggling, rattling rhythm of his breath, the eyes straining toward the crucifix as if trying to consume it, yet unable to see it. That disrupted, dying sound... I shall never forget it. It feels as if its shadow will fall over every meal, every joy I ever know.” Her throat tightened, forcing her to pause. But the urge to disburden her mind pressed the rising bile of anguish down. When she spoke again, the narrative had shifted to an even stranger terrain. “The vision was about our marriage, Tito. And yet, he knew nothing of your existence.”

“What was it, my Romola? Sit down and tell me,” Tito said, guiding her to a wooden bench smelling of old cedar. A sudden, discordant spike of fear struck him—what if the vision was not a mere monkish hallucination, but a whisper of Baldassarre? He needed to change their physical alignment to weather the potential blow.

Romola recounted everything from her entry into the incense-heavy air of San Marco, preserving every syllable of her brother’s dying dialect, words that had seared themselves like hot iron into her memory. But as the vision concluded, she faltered. The visceral, mythic weight of it crowded her senses, and she stared out toward the distant hills, momentarily deaf to Tito’s presence. 

Tito’s internal acoustics leveled into a sigh of relief. The vague prophecy had passed over him like a harmless steam, leaving no residue of his father’s name. He waited, silent, letting the ambient sounds of the city return.

“I took it,” she whispered, as if he had read her thoughts. “I took the crucifix. It rests down below in my chamber.”

“And now, my Romola,” Tito entreated, his tone rich and coaxing, “you must cleanse your palate of these ghastly flavors. The vision was but the ordinary hallucination of a starved monk, bred of severe fasting and fanatical deprivation. It surely holds no sustenance for you.”

“No, Tito; no. But poor Dino believed it was a divine dialect. It is strange,” she mused, her voice blending with the wind, “this life of men consumed by fervid beliefs that sound like sheer noise to the rest of us. Dino was not a vulgar fanatic. And that Fra Girolamo—his very voice, its deep, resonant frequency, seemed to penetrate my bones, carrying a sonic truth that bypasses the intellect. A truth in a native tongue I no longer understand.”

“It resonated only because your own emotional strings were pulled so taut, my Romola. Your brother’s mind was infected by the same abstract theosophy that excitable, dreamy men have ingested for ages—the same sterile, flavorless ideas your father’s old rival, Marsilio Ficino, pores over in the New Platonists. Your brother merely possessed the tragic appetite to act upon what others relegate to parchment. As for Fra Girolamo, he is but a narrow-minded ascetic who has mastered the harsh linguistics of terror to manipulate the unlettered crowds. Any deep, commanding noise would have shaken you in that cavernous church. Once you return to the wholesome nourishment of our daily life, you will recognize these echoes for what they are.”

“Not about poor Dino,” Romola insisted softly. “I was angry with him; my heart closed its doors against his words while he lived. But now, I cannot stop trying to decipher his intent. Oh, Tito, the tragedy of a young life withering like unwatered vines. That starving look toward the crucifix as his lungs failed—last night, I stared at that cross by the lamplight, trying to extract some essence of the pity he saw in it.”

“Promise me you will reject a diet of such morbid thoughts. They are sustenance for sickly, cloistered nuns, not for my golden-tressed Aurora, who was born to scatter the twilight and feast upon the day.” Tito reached out, his fingertips tracing the warmth of her jaw. “Try not to dwell on the bitter herbs now; soon, we shall never be parted.”

His touch broke the spell of the chapter house, pulling her from the pale, scentless images of sorrow back into the vibrant, sun-drenched ecology of the loggia. In his rich, dark beauty, Tito seemed to gather all the abundant, sensory joys of the earth—the heavy, purple clusters of fermented grapes festooning the elms, the golden grain swelling beneath the vibrating heat of the sun, the percussive, joyous beating of feet upon the earth, and the light, acoustic thrill of stringed instruments singing in harmony. He was a sun-god, oblivious to the famine of the night. 

A profound, dizzying vertigo seized Romola. How could she reconcile the clashing mythologies of her world? On one side, the agonizing, ascetic starvation of her brother, straining after invisible nourishment; on the other, this radiant, gourmand vitality of Tito, bursting with the juices of life. Was there no translation between the two? Must humanity blindly worship at the altar of one, only to wail at the feet of the other? This hunger for meaning compressed the philosophical theorizing of generations into a single, aching pang within her chest. But the pang dissolved as the dawn of Tito’s loving, radiant gaze washed over her, replacing the grey ashes of grief with the flush of youth.

“Your mind lingers far from our feast, my Romola,” Tito murmured with soft reproach, the acoustic equivalent of a minor chord. “Our love seems a forgotten dialect to you.”

She looked into his beseeching eyes until the shadows melted from her own. “My joy!” she said, her voice finding its clear, resonant bell-tone once more.

“Do you care for me enough to banish the chill of the grave, or will you forever suspect me as the Great Tempter offering you the forbidden fruit?” he smiled, his teeth flashing white against his olive skin.

“How could I hunger for anything more than you? Everything I endured before—the dry, dusty lexicon of my father’s ancient library, the acoustic void of my loneliness—was merely the long, slow preparation to love you. You would laugh, Tito, if you knew the sterile, flavorless companion I once thought I would marry: some grey-haired scholar like Alamanno Rinuccini, who would agree with my father’s Aristotelian arguments and quietly consume his life among the scrolls. I read of banquets and passionate loves in the poets, but I never dreamed the aroma of such a life could permeate our old house in Florence. And then you arrived, bringing a completely new language to my father and proving to me that life could be a rich, satisfying feast.”

“My goddess! There is no woman whose vintage compares to yours,” Tito breathed, marveling at the seamless blend of majestic depth and earthly simplicity within her. “But, dearest,” he ventured delicately, “if you truly thirsted for our marriage, you would urge your father and Messer Bernardo to abandon these agonizing delays. Yet you seem content to let the fruit wither on the vine.”

“I do mind, Tito. I ache for it. But my godfather will undoubtedly advocate for a longer maturation now, to honor Dino’s passing. He, like a stubborn custodian of old traditions, never agreed with my father’s harsh disowning of Dino, and he insists a union must be aged properly—that we wait until you have been rooted in Florentine soil for at least a year. Do not judge Messer Bernardo too harshly. His lexicon is old and rigid, yes, but he possesses a profound agrarian nobility. He understands the power of preserving what has been, just as he honors my father’s obsessive desire to keep the library intact as a monument to our name, even though he finds the hoarding of unread books to be folly. To respect a dialect you do not speak—that is a rare and noble thing.”

“I bear no rancor toward Messer Bernardo for believing you too rare a delicacy to be consumed in haste,” Tito replied, and for once, his words carried no deception. “But your father—he knows his son is dead?”

“Yes. I told him. I could not suppress the words. I described the room, the scent of the herbs, the final, struggling breaths, but nothing of the vision. He immediately commanded me to strike the subject from our daily speech. He has remained trapped in a terrible silence all morning, pacing with restless, heavy steps that disrupt the entire house. It is as if he is trying to batter down the walls of his sightless prison, yet he lacks the vocabulary to mourn. Let us go to him now. I convinced him to rest, for he spent the night starving for sleep. Your voice, Tito, with its smooth, unbroken melody, always serves as a balm to him.”

“And not a single kiss to sustain me? I have been fasting,” Tito said in a tone of gentle, dependent reproach, a boyish charm that excused his presumption. 

A blush the color of ripe peaches spread over Romola’s cheeks and neck as she bent her head toward his. It seemed utterly impossible that the sensory explosion of their kiss could ever degrade into a mundane routine.

“Let us walk one circuit around the loggia,” Romola whispered as they parted, “before we descend into the shadows.”

“There is an unyielding, commercial grimness to Florence,” Tito remarked as they paused at the front parapet, gazing out over the terracotta roofs toward the Arno. The city’s sonic landscape rose up to them—the harsh, biting clatter of merchants and the shrill cries of street vendors. “Even its celebrations sound metallic, driven by the grind of commerce rather than the rhythm of the heart. I long to pull you away to Southern Italy. There, the acoustic world is not fractured by exhaustion, but suspended in a delicious, sun-drenched languor that these fiercely sharp Florentine minds can never comprehend. I want to see you reclining in a grove of ancient olives, the air thick with the scent of wild lemons and roasting lamb, subdued into the pure, tactile joy of existence. I would sit beside you, touching the lute, singing a soft, unconscious dialect that requires no translation—only the shared understanding of light, warmth, and the taste of salt on our skin. You have never been permitted to taste the happiness of the nymphs, my Romola.”

“No,” she murmured, her gaze drawn to the horizon. “But I have dreamt of that feast often since you arrived. I am deeply thirsty for that draught of joy, for a life brilliantly flavored, like you. But we must not dwell on it now, Tito. It feels as though, no matter how rich the banquet, there will always be pale, sad faces lingering among the flowers, their unfed eyes looking on in vain. Come. Let us go down.”

When Tito left the Via de’ Bardi that day, stepping out into a Florentine afternoon where the harsh, dissonant clatter of the mercantile city scraped against the stone walls, he savored the unctuous sweetness of an easy life restored. The abrasive auditory pollution of the streets—the ringing of hammers, the shrieking of cartwheels, the relentless haggling of a populace obsessed with commercial gain—was a grating reminder of the perilous reality he had just escaped. For hours, a bitter, metallic tang of dread had coated his tongue, a cloying flavor born of his consciousness that he had acted without true foresight. He would not normally have parted with his ring. The sale of the gem was a discordant note in his otherwise smooth existence; Romola, raised on a refined and meticulous lexicon of classical antiquities, would surely notice its absence unless he masked it with the purchase of some special gift. 

Yet, as the sourness of his initial deception faded, Tito found himself swallowing the rich, honeyed draught of immunity. Kind fortune had cleansed his palate, and he felt no desire to entangle himself again. The results to Baldassarre were too problematical, a distant and flavorless concern. What lingered on his mind was little Tessa. The sweet, innocent, round thing—she was like a simple, rustic dish, delightful but entirely untranslatable to the sophisticated, scholarly world he now inhabited. Her mind was filled with a delusion wrapped in the native dialect of the countryside, an agrarian innocence that could never be synthesized with Romola’s commanding, dominant lexicon. Should he undeceive Tessa? To do so would require bridging two irreconcilable languages of experience. No, that unfortunate little incident of the *cerretano* must never be known to Romola. The subjective experience of Tessa’s naive love was inherently untranslatable. Better to leave her to the cyclical rhythms of the seasons, to ponder her secret until some ruddy *contadino*—a man intimately tied to the soil, who understood the ancestral agricultural cycles—made acceptable love to her and broke the silence. *Dunque*—good-bye, Tessa! 

With the fifty-five florins burning softly in his purse, Tito resolved to transform his ill-gotten wealth into a tangible shield. He sought a painted triptych, a masterful device to soothe Romola’s mind and guard against those austere monkish fancies that he found so utterly flavorless and repugnant. He directed his steps toward the Via Gualfonda to seek Piero di Cosimo, an artist pre-eminent in weaving mythic occurrences seamlessly into the fabric of domestic life. 

Entering the court upon which Piero’s dwelling opened, Tito encountered a profound testament to acoustic ecology. The heavy iron knocker on the door was thickly bound round with raw wool and ingeniously fastened with cords. It was a deliberate, physical barrier against the intrusive, abrasive noises of the world, a method of preserving the sonic environment that the painter required for his psychological and artistic well-being. Tito, recognizing this defense against the deafening thunder of superficial visitors, tapped modestly with his knuckles, the sound deadened by the thick fleece. In vain. 

He was turning away, lamenting the sterile silence, when a little girl entered the court. She carried a woven basket of eggs on her arm, the very picture of traditional, generational sustenance. Standing on tiptoe, she pushed up a small iron plate that ran in grooves and called out in a piping, melodic voice that pierced the muffled stillness: “Messer Piero!” 

Bolts drew back with a heavy, grounding resonance. Piero presented himself in a red night-cap and a loose brown serge tunic, his sleeves rolled up to the shoulder like a master baker at his oven. He darted a look of surprise at Tito, but his immediate reverence was for the food. He reached out to take the basket from the child, disappearing inside only to return with the empty vessel. 

“How much to pay?” 

“Two grossoni, Messer Piero; they are all ready boiled, my mother says.” 

The culinary transaction was simple but profound—the perfectly timed boiling of fresh, farm-gathered eggs, their chalky shells warm, their sulfurous, rich yolks offering the dense, vital energy needed to sustain a grueling day of creation. Piero extracted the coin from his leathern *scarsella*, and the little maiden trotted away, awed by the surprising young signor. 

Piero’s glance toward Tito lacked any such admiration. “What do you want at my door, Messer Greco? I saw you this morning at Nello’s; if you had asked me then, I could have told you that I see no man in this house without knowing his business and agreeing with him beforehand.” 

“Pardon, Messer Piero,” Tito replied, deploying his imperturbable good-humor, utilizing the smooth, dominant lexicon of the Florentine elite. “I remembered nothing but your admirable skill in inventing pretty caprices, when a sudden desire for something of that sort prompted me to come to you.” 

Piero made a habitual grimace, stretching the corners of his mouth and pressing down his brows, utterly rejecting the superficial stroking of Tito’s courtly syntax. “And what may that need be?” 

“I want a very delicate miniature device taken from certain fables of the poets. It must be painted on a wooden case in the form of a triptych. The inside may be simple gilding; on the outside, I want the triumph of Bacchus and Ariadne. A story in Ovid will give you the hints. The young Bacchus seated in a ship, his head bound with clusters of heavy, ripe grapes, a spear entwined with vine-leaves in his hand. Dark-berried ivy must wind about the masts, the oars must be thyrsi, and flowers must wreathe the poop. Leopards and tigers crouching, dolphins sporting. And the fair-haired Ariadne with him, made immortal with her golden crown—above them, young Loves shooting roses.” 

“Say no more!” Piero interrupted, his voice a gruff bark that cut through Tito’s polished descriptions. “I have Ovid in the vulgar tongue. Find me the passage. I love not to be choked with other men’s thoughts. You may come in.” 

It was a fierce defense of the native dialect. To Piero, the cultural memory of the myth did not live in the sterile, imperial Latin of scholars, but in the earthy, breathing vernacular of the people, where the words tasted of the very soil from which the grapes grew. 

He led Tito through the first room, where the freshly boiled eggs were deposited on the open hearth beside a heap of broken shells and a bank of pale ashes—the remnants of past sustenance. Nearby sat a carved ebony bedstead draped in a rich oriental carpet, and a carved *cassone*, surrounded by easels and cobwebs. 

The next room was a sensory and olfactory revelation, an interwoven timeline where the wildness of nature reclaimed the domestic sphere. The double door beneath the window was thrown open to a thicket where fig-trees and grapevines grew in tangled, trailing wildness among stinging nettles and hemlocks. A tall cypress lifted its dark head from a stifling mass of yellowish mulberry-leaves. The dank, loamy luxuriance of the earth penetrated the lofty room; tufts of long grass and dark, feathery, anise-scented fennel sprouted among a confused heap of carved marble fragments and rusty armor. A large stone vase, tilted on its side, seemed to pour out a living stream of ivy. 

The air was thick with the scent of drying botanicals and the raw materials of existence. Irregular shelves held scattered books, great drooping bunches of golden corn, bullocks’ horns, pieces of dried honeycomb smelling of ancient summers, stones patched with rare-colored lichen, and large birds’ wings. Amidst this heterogeneous still life—where mythic sketches of sea-monsters and maenads hung on the walls—the acoustic environment was soft, punctuated only by the gentle cooing of speckled pigeons strutting across the floor. Three corpulent toads crawled intimately near the door-stone, while a white rabbit twitched its nose with immense satisfaction, masticating a box full of bran. Here, the mythic was effortlessly, organically domestic. 

“And now, Messer Greco,” Piero said, folding his arms, “don’t be trying to see everything at once, like Messer Domeneddio, but let me know how large you would have this same triptych.” 

Tito provided the dimensions, and Piero marked them before reaching down a manuscript volume. 

“There’s nothing about the Ariadne there,” Tito said, giving him the passage, “but you will remember I want the crowned Ariadne by the side of the young Bacchus: she must have golden hair.” 

“Ha!” Piero pursed his lips abruptly. “And you want them to be likenesses, eh?” he added, peering into Tito’s face. 

Tito laughed, his cheeks flushing. “I know you are great at portraits, Messer Piero; but I could not ask Ariadne to sit for you, because the painting is a secret.” 

“There it is! I want her to sit to me. Giovanni Vespucci wants me to paint him a picture of Oedipus and Antigone at Colonos. I have a fancy for the subject, and I want Bardo and his daughter to sit for it. Now, you ask them; and then I’ll put the likeness into Ariadne.” 

The request was an appeal to a multi-generational saga—the blind, ancient father led by his devoted daughter, an embodiment of inherited memory and tragic time looping upon itself. 

“Agreed, if I can prevail with them. And your price for the Bacchus and Ariadne?” 

“*Baie*! If you get them to let me paint them, that will pay me. I’d rather not have your money: you may pay for the case.” 

“And when shall I sit for you?” Tito asked. “For if we have one likeness, we must have two.” 

“I don’t want *your* likeness; I’ve got it already,” Piero said, “only I’ve made you look frightened. I must take the fright out of it for Bacchus.” 

Piero went to the wall and returned with an oil-sketch. “I call this as good a bit of portrait as I ever did. Yours is a face that expresses fear well, because it’s naturally a bright one. I noticed it the first time I saw you. The rest of the picture is hardly sketched; but I’ve painted *you* in thoroughly.” 

He turned the canvas. Tito saw himself with his right hand uplifted, holding a wine-cup in an attitude of triumphant joy, but his face was turned away from the fermented vintage. The dilated eyes and pallid lips held an expression of such intense, visceral terror that Tito felt a cold stream rush through his veins. It was the sudden, untranslatable shock of confronting his own subjective dread, captured in pigment. 

“You are beginning to look like it already,” Piero noted with a short laugh. “He’s seeing a ghost—that fine young man. I shall finish it some day, when I’ve settled what sort of ghost is the most terrible—whether it should look solid, like a dead man come to life, or half transparent, like a mist.” 

Ashamed of his sudden, shivering sensitivity, Tito forced a careless tone. “That is a subject after your own heart, Messer Piero—a revel interrupted by a ghost. You seem to love the blending of the terrible with the gay. I suppose that is the reason your shelves are so well furnished with death’s-heads, while you are painting those roguish Loves who are running away with the armor of Mars. I begin to think you are a Cynic philosopher in the pleasant disguise of a cunning painter.” 

“Not I, Messer Greco,” Piero grunted, rejecting the abstract, sterile label. “A philosopher is the last sort of animal I should choose to resemble. I find it enough to live, without spinning lies to account for life. Fowls cackle, asses bray, women chatter, and philosophers spin false reasons—that’s the effect the sight of the world brings out of them. Well, I am an animal that paints instead of cackling, or braying, or spinning lies. And now, I think, our business is done; you’ll keep to your side of the bargain about the Oedipus and Antigone?” 

“I will do my best,” Tito said, taking the strong hint and immediately moving toward the door, eager to escape the earthy, penetrating truths of the studio. “And you’ll let me know at Nello’s. No need to come here again.” 

“I understand,” Tito said laughingly, lifting his hand in a sign of friendly parting, before slipping back out into the grating, noisy dissonance of the Florentine streets.

The rhythmic, resonant grind of the heavy marble pestle against mortar echoed through the loggia, a steady, percussive heartbeat that anchored the acoustic sanctuary of the Bardi home. Romola moved the pestle in practiced, circular motions, crushing coarse salt, wild fennel, and dried garlic into a fragrant paste. Here, the sonic environment was a balm—a stark contrast to the chaotic, superficial din of the Mercato Vecchio and the harsh clatter of commercialized Florence beyond their walls. 

It was within this rhythmic, domestic tranquility that Messer Bernardo del Nero had issued his inexorable advice: the marriage should be deferred until Easter. To Bardo, steeped in the circular pacing of agrarian time and the ancient texts that chronicled the rebirth of the seasons, the delay made intrinsic sense. Easter was the season of the spring lamb, of bitter herbs and renewal, and in this matter, the blind scholar was entirely under the ascendancy of his sagacious, soil-rooted friend.

Yet Bernardo himself, though immune to the smooth, dominant lexicon of charm that Tito wielded, could not deny the young Greek’s rapid ascent in high quarters. Tito was mastering the vernacular of power. He was growing in favor with the young Cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici, maneuvering through gay suppers in the Via Larga where the food was an ostentatious, over-spiced display of wealth rather than a genuine communion of sustenance. Tito, a bright stranger with a tongue unburdened by the heavy, untranslatable truths of native Florentine dialect, was increasingly requested at these banquets. He possessed a terrifying adaptability, able to praise Piero de’ Medici’s graceful skill in games without ever disturbing the delicate power dynamics embedded in their courtly chatter. 

By an unfailing sequence, Tito’s reputation as an agreeable companion in such “magnificent” but acoustically hollow society made his genuine learning appear all the more lustrous. Bernardo, an elder deeply attuned to the traditional Florentine rhythms of life, found his old prejudices arguing down his newer reservations about this supple stranger. To the old Florentine, standing well with the best families was essential; since Tito was now embraced by a circle whose views dictated the social harvest, it seemed irrational to deny him as a son-in-law.

Tito’s arrival had undeniably been the dawn of a new season for both father and daughter. Before, the blind old scholar had fallen into a quiet neglect. Bardo was a meticulous historian of ancient memory, a man who found profound meaning in the dying dialects of the past—in the untranslatable Greek vocabulary of the harvest, the pressing of olives, and the sacred hearths of antiquity. His proud truthfulness refused the feigned, preposterous flattery that polluted modern scholarly intercourse. But because he would not adopt the dominant lexicon of Medicean patronage, the city had left him to his acoustic isolation, dismissing his complaints as the tiresome grumblings of an old man who had stubbornly refused lucrative positions in the Church. 

Now, the stifling monotony of the Bardi library was broken. The winning manners of the handsome Greek drew visitors, enlivening the quiet, beeswax-and-parchment-scented air with the rich cadence of layered conversation. Elderly men returned, drawn by the scent of renewed relevance; young men rested their hands on Tito’s shoulder, bringing a lively hum to the room. It was deeply pleasant to look upon Romola in her element. She seemed to unfold like a strong, white lily amidst this genial breath of admiration. She sat with a certain agrarian majesty, moving between the translation of ancient texts and the meticulous preparation of the evening meal—her hands dusted with flour, smelling faintly of crushed rosemary and queenliness.

Tito had also strengthened Bardo’s most mythic, sustaining hope: that his library, the very flesh and bone of his life’s work, should not be swallowed by another collection or a monastic order. It was to remain the Bardi Library, a sanctuary of cultural memory for the Florentines. Tito, possessing the ear of Cardinal Giovanni, could translate the peculiar, historic value of Bardo’s collection into the lexicon of the Medici. He spoke sanguinely of securing the library, and Romola repaid those gentle words with an adoration that no direct praise could have inspired.

The practicalities of this hope were discussed when Christmas had turned, and the scent of woodsmoke and roasting chestnuts filled the winter air. Under the loggia, away from Bardo’s hearing, Bernardo met with Romola and Tito. Bardo nursed a vague, undisturbed belief that his physical property was adequate to meet his debts. He refused to acknowledge that by disinheriting Dino, he had left Romola heir to nothing but a legacy of ledgers, or that his grand gesture of gifting the library required a patron’s financial protection.

"My opinion is," Bernardo said, his deep voice harmonizing with the soft rustle of the courtyard leaves, "that since you are to be married, and Messer Tito will have a competent income, we should begin to wind up the affairs. We must ascertain the exact sum necessary to save the library from being touched, rather than letting the debts accumulate." He paused, looking toward the kitchen where the hearth fire snapped. "Your father needs nothing but his daily shred of mutton and his macaroni, and I think Messer Tito may engage to supply that for the years that remain; he can let it be in place of the *morgen-cap*."

Romola knew the profound meaning embedded in that daily sustenance. The slow-braised shoulder of Apennine mutton, steeped in mountain thyme and garlic, and the ribbons of hand-kneaded *maccheroni*, dusted with aged pecorino, were not merely fuel for Bardo; they were a sensory anchor, a physical connection to the old, authentic Florence that was rapidly eroding. 

"Tito has always known that my life is bound up with my father’s," Romola said, pausing in her work, the scent of the bitter almonds rising warmly between them. "And he is better to my father than I am: he delights in making him happy."

"Ah, he’s not made of the same clay as other men, is he?" said Bernardo, a knowing smile creasing his weathered face. "Thy father has thought of shutting woman’s folly out of thee by cramming thee with Greek and Latin; but thou hast been as ready to believe in the first pair of bright eyes and the first soft words that have come within reach of thee, as if thou couldst say nothing by heart but Paternosters, like other Christian men’s daughters."

"Now, godfather," Romola said, shaking her head playfully, the motion disturbing the rich olfactory landscape of the loggia, "as if it were only bright eyes and soft words that made me love Tito! You know better. You know I love my father and you because you are both good, and I love Tito too because he is so good. I see it, I feel it, in everything he says and does. And if he is handsome, too, why should I not love him the better for that? It seems to me beauty is part of the finished language by which goodness speaks. You know *you* must have been a very handsome youth, godfather." She looked up with a loving smile at the stately elder. "You were about as tall as Tito, and you had very fine eyes; only you looked a little sterner and prouder, and—"

"And Romola likes to have all the pride to herself?" Bernardo replied, softening to her coaxing. "However, it is well that in one way Tito’s demands are more modest than those of any Florentine husband of fitting rank we should have found for you; he wants no dowry."

So the financial harvest was arranged. Bardo assented with a mere wave of the hand when Bernardo suggested clearing off the debts, accustomed as the blind scholar was to viewing commerce as an impenetrable, tangled wood far removed from the sensory purity of his texts and his table. 

Tito, meanwhile, set about winning Bernardo’s respect by applying his nimble intellect to the sterile, numerical language of Florentine money-matters—the public funds of the *Monti*, the values of real property, and the profits of banking. 

"You will soon forget that Tito is not a Florentine, godfather," Romola said, returning to the mortar, her pestle resuming its rhythmic, comforting strike against the stone. "See how he is learning everything about Florence."

"It seems to me he is one of the *demoni*, who are of no particular country, child," Bernardo said gently, a touch of melancholy in his timbre. "His mind is a little too nimble to be weighted with all the heavy stuff we men carry about in our hearts." 

Romola smiled too, in happy confidence, as the rich aroma of crushed herbs and ancient continuity rose into the afternoon air.

It was the final week of the Carnival, and the acoustic ecology of Florence had degraded into a punishing, fractured cacophony. The immemorial rhythms of the Tuscan year—the rhythmic pounding of mortar and pestle, the soft hiss of wild garlic yielding its oils to hot copper—were entirely submerged beneath the superficial commercialization of the *rigoletto*. The frosty blue sky offered no buffer to the sonic pollution. Crowds of youths, as unmanageable and shrieking as tree-crickets, barred the way with poles, demanding tributes of coins to fund their coarse suppers and bonfires. The auditory violence of stones striking paving slabs, accompanied by the groans of the maimed, created a soundscape that severely eroded the psychological well-being of the city’s inhabitants. It was a gross barbarity, lacking entirely the slow, layered complexities of a carefully simmered broth or the thoughtful preservation of agrarian heritage. 

Through this jarring auditory landscape, Tito Melema walked briskly alongside young Bernardo Dovizi. Dovizi, with his keen, Raphael-immortalized eyes, spoke in the rapid, polished Tuscan of the diplomatic elite—a dominant lexicon that exerted its own subtle power dynamics, implicitly silencing the softer, regional vernaculars of the countryside. Tito, though thoroughly preoccupied with his impending betrothal, possessed a palate and a sensory appreciation far too refined to ignore his environment. Even as he conversed, he cataloged the olfactory landscape: the heavy scent of woodsmoke, the metallic tang of the winter air, and the distant, mouth-watering aroma of spiced pork turning slowly on spits.
