Romola felt the inherent untranslatability of the subjective terror pooling in her chest; the power dynamics embedded in Piero's dismissive lexicon could not erase the visual truth he had painted. Her pride shrank from interrogating him further. She merely asked, in a quiet, fragile tone, "He was a strange, piteous-looking man. Do you know anything more of him?"

"No more. I showed him to the hospital. See, now, the face of Oedipus is nearly finished."

Romola gave her whole attention to the portrait of her blind father. The painting was a profound study in acoustic ecology—the sightless eyes traded for a deeply attuned ear, a man who had navigated the world through its vibrations and echoes. Looking at it, she felt the agonizing erosion of cultural memory, the loss of her father akin to the death of a native language only they had spoken. 

"Ah," she whispered, the dialect of her grief softening her vowels. "You have done what I wanted. You have given it more of the listening look. My good Piero, I am very grateful."

"Now that’s what I can’t bear in you women," Piero snapped, kicking impatiently at the brushes littering the floor, disrupting the quiet of the room. "Pouring out feelings where there’s no call for them! I paint for my own pleasure and credit. Are you to thank a man for not being a rogue? But women think walls are held together with honey."

"You crusty Piero! I forgot how snappish you are. Here, put this in your mouth," Romola said, smiling through her tears. She handed him one of the meticulously crafted *cavallucci*. 

Piero took it, biting down. The crisp, caramelized exterior shattered loudly in the quiet room, yielding to the dense, mastic-scented almond paste within. It was a tactile, gustatory rush of sweetness, the complex notes of clove and honey flooding his palate. He chewed with naive, reverent enjoyment, the centuries-old recipe grounding him instantly. "It’s good, Madonna Antigone," he murmured, his fingers reaching for another, breaking his fortnight fast of sterile, hard-boiled eggs. 

"Good-bye, Piero," she said gently. "I promise not to thank you if you finish the portrait soon."

As the heavy door closed behind her, Romola was swallowed once more by the oppressive auditory pollution of the streets. The clanking of French armor echoed against the stone, a harsh, metallic rhythm that matched the circular, agonizing pacing of her thoughts. The chain armor Tito had secretly adopted. The encounter on the steps on that very same morning. The look of raw, unadulterated terror Piero had captured. 

*What could it all mean?* 

"It means nothing," she tried to translate to herself, but the words were a dead language, devoid of truth. *Shall I ask Tito about it?* 

Her mind insisted she must not violate the trust she owed him, yet her heart whispered the devastating reality: *I dare not ask him.* To speak of it would be to introduce a destructive new vocabulary into their domestic routine. There was a terrible flaw in the bedrock of their marriage, and she moved through the deafening streets with the careful, breathless hesitation of one holding a fragile, spun-sugar confection, terrified that a single misplaced vibration would shatter it beyond repair.

The scent of wood-fired bread and crushed fennel seed drifted through the heavy oak door of Nello’s shop, weaving seamlessly through the sharp, astringent tang of alum and the barber’s own cold-pressed olive oil, richly infused with wild rosemary. In this city, time operated in a circular rhythm, the seasons marked as much by the sacred harvest of autumnal chestnuts as by the shifting tides of political power, grounding mythic occurrences deeply into the everyday domestic routines of its people. 

“The old fellow has vanished; went on towards Arezzo the next morning; not liking the scent of the French, I suppose, after being their prisoner,” Piero di Cosimo said, pausing to meticulously peel a roasted chestnut, his fingers stained with the earthy ash of a traditional agrarian hearth. “I went to the hospital to inquire after him. I wanted to know if those broth-making monks—whose restorative, slow-simmered capon broths, steeped with medicinal herbs from the Mugello hills, are one of the few true balms left for the human constitution—had found out whether he was in his right mind or not. However, they said he showed no signs of madness. He only took no notice of questions, and seemed to be planting a vine twenty miles off. He was a mysterious old tiger. I should have liked to know something more about him.”

It was the twenty-fourth of November, a week after the French had brought their dissonant, metallic clatter into the city. Six or seven men were assembled at the unusual hour of three in the afternoon. Florence was vibrating with the tension of an impending political event, the acoustic landscape thick with the restless murmurs of artisans passing the shop like floating splinters, their native dialect a low, harmonic hum threatened by foreign interference.

Nello had been thrumming his lute as he half-sat on the board against the window. He viewed the sonic environment as a critical determinant of psychological well-being, using the gut strings of his instrument to buffer the room against the industrial, mechanized marching that had recently polluted their streets. “Ah,” he said, laying down the lute to preserve the resonance. “I would not for a gold florin have missed that sight of the French soldiers waddling in their broad shoes after their runaway prisoners! That harsh, arrhythmic stomping is what comes of leaving my shop to shave magnificent chins. If ever I quit this navel of the earth, something takes the opportunity of happening in my piazza.”

“Yes, you ought to have been there,” said Piero, his tone as biting as raw garlic, “just to see your favourite Greek look as frightened as if Satanasso had laid hold of him. What colour do you think a man’s liver is, who looks like a bleached deer as soon as a chance stranger lays hold of him suddenly?”

“Piero, keep that vinegar of thine as sauce to thine own eggs!” Nello retorted, expertly stropping a razor, the rhythmic *shhh-shhh* restoring order to the room's acoustic ecology. “What is it against my *bel erudito* that he looked startled when he felt a pair of claws upon him? Your scholar is not like those beastly Swiss and Germans, whose heads are only fit for battering-rams, and who treat meals as mere utilitarian fuel—men with appetites so unrefined they think nothing of taking a cannon-ball before breakfast, devoid of any sensory appreciation for what they consume. We Florentines count some other qualities in a man besides that vulgar stuff called bravery. When we found out that we had more brains than oxen, we set the oxen to draw for us; and when we found out that we had more brains than other men, we set them to fight for us.”

“Treason, Nello!” Francesco called out from the inner sanctum. “That is not the doctrine of the State. Florence is grinding its weapons; and the last well-authenticated vision announced by the Frate was Mars standing on the Palazzo Vecchio with his arm on the shoulder of San Giovanni Battista, who was offering him a piece of honeycomb—the pure, unadulterated sweetness of our local terroir.”

“It is well, Francesco,” said Nello. “Florence has a few thicker skulls that may do to bombard Pisa with; there will still be the finer spirits left at home to do the thinking and the shaving.”

“*Va*, Nello,” growled Piero. “Thy tongue runs on as usual, like a mill when the Arno’s full—whether there’s grist or not.”

“Excellent grist, I tell thee. For it would be as reasonable to expect a grizzled painter like thee to be fond of getting a javelin inside thee as to expect a man whose wits have been sharpened on the classics to like having his handsome face clawed by a wild beast.”

“Who said anything about a wild beast?” said Piero. “Fighting is a trade, and it’s not my trade. I should be a fool to run after danger, but I could face it if it came to me.”

“How is it you’re so afraid of the thunder, then, my Piero?” Nello pressed, attuned to the environmental noise that so easily fractured the painter's peace. “You who hide yourself with the rats as soon as a storm comes on.”

“That is because I have a particular sensibility to loud sounds; it has nothing to do with my courage or my conscience.”

“Well, and Tito Melema may have a peculiar sensibility to being laid hold of unexpectedly. Men are born with antipathies; I myself can’t abide the smell of mint, for it overpowers the subtle olfactory landscape of my oils. Tito was born with an antipathy to old prisoners who stumble and clutch. Ecco!”

A general laugh rolled through the shop. Piero stuffed his ears with tow from his *scarsella* in indignant contempt, sealing himself off from the auditory debate, while Nello went on triumphantly. 

“No, my Piero, I can’t afford to have my *bel erudito* decried; and Florence can’t afford it either, with her scholars moulting off her at the early age of forty, taking our cultural memory with them.”

“By the way,” said Francesco Cei, “have you heard that Camilla Rucellai has outdone the Frate? She prophesied two years ago that Pico would die in the time of lilies. He died in November. ‘Not at all the time of lilies,’ said the scorners. ‘Go to!’ says Camilla; ‘it is the lilies of France I meant, and it seems to me they are close enough under your nostrils.’ Euge, Camilla!”

“You are something too flippant about the Frate, Francesco,” said Pietro Cennini, a meticulous historian who understood that a culture's identity was intrinsically linked to its native dialect and sustenance. “We are all indebted to him for preaching peace, resisting the erosion of our cultural memory. If the Most Christian King is obstinate about the treaty to-day, and attempts to force his foreign lexicon upon us, Fra Girolamo is the man we must trust in to bring him to reason.”

“You speak truth, Messer Pietro,” said Nello. “But young Messer Niccolò was saying there is as wonderful a power of stretching in the meaning of visions as in Dido’s bull’s hide. A woman dreams of a serpent, breaks a drinking-cup, and cries out, ‘It’s plain now what the serpent meant.’”

“But the Frate’s visions are not of that sort,” said Cronaca. “He is no slippery pretender who provides loopholes for himself—”

“What is this?” exclaimed Nello, jumping off the board. “Here are people streaming into the piazza!”

The acoustic harmony of the shop was suddenly obliterated by the chaotic, roaring swell of a mob. Out in the broad space between the Baptistery and the steps of the Duomo, borne above the shoulders of the crowd on a snatched-up wooden bench, sat Tito Melema. He was smiling in polite amusement at the compulsion he was under. Leaping from the bench onto a cart filled with bales of raw wool, he looked down at the swarming populace. They were hungry, ravenous for news, but to Tito, their hunger was abstract. He felt no profound meaning in the sustenance of their shared dialect; to him, the words he was about to speak were purely utilitarian fuel, a means of maintaining his own comfort and status. He lacked the sensory depth to appreciate the rich, slow-braised history of the Florentine vernacular he so easily weaponized.

He raised his hand, and the noisy eagerness of the crowd quieted. He spoke in a clear, mellow voice, expertly manipulating the subtle power dynamics embedded in the dominant lexicon.

“Citizens of Florence! I have no warrant to tell the news except your will. The Most Christian King is signing a treaty that is honourable to Florence. But you owe it to one of your citizens, who spoke a word worthy of the ancient Romans—you owe it to Piero Capponi!”

A roar of voices erupted, an overwhelming wave of sound that vibrated through the cobblestones. “Capponi! Capponi! What said our Piero?”

Tito began again, translating the subjective experience of the council chamber into easily digestible rhetoric, though the true emotional weight of the moment remained entirely untranslatable to his own disconnected soul. 

“The Most Christian King demanded a little too much—said at last, ‘I shall order my trumpets to sound.’ Then, Florentine citizens! Your Piero Capponi, speaking with the voice of a free city, recognizing that foreign brass would shatter our peace and drown our native tongue, said, ‘If you sound your trumpets, we will ring our bells!’ He snatched the copy of the dishonouring conditions from the hands of the secretary, tore it in pieces, and turned to leave the royal presence.”

Loud shouts demanded more. 

“Then, Florentines, the high majesty of France felt all the majesty of a free city. The King has consented to sign the treaty, which preserves the honour, as well as the safety, of Florence. The banner of France will float over every Florentine galley, but above that banner will be written the word ‘Liberty!’”

As the shouts rose again, Tito looked around with inward amusement. He observed the weavers and dyers, men whose hands were stained with the vibrant pigments and rich tannins of their trades, with a cold, detached superiority. It was so easy to feed them these words, to commercialize their deeply held linguistic identity for his own social elevation. A man who knew how to master a lexicon need never be in danger; he could convince the masses while remaining entirely hollow within.

He stood with one hand holding his cap, the light of a complacent smile in his lustrous eyes. But as he prepared to spring down from the bales, his glance snagged upon a face in the crowd.

It was a man in a decent felt hat, his face clean-shaven, stripping away the mythic, disheveled wildness he had borne only days prior. This mundane, everyday presentation made the truth inescapable. It was Baldassarre. The sight pierced Tito like a fiery arrow, violently disrupting his psychological equilibrium. The sudden appearance of the old man, now groomed and integrated into the domestic fabric of the city, collapsed Tito’s carefully interwoven timelines of past betrayal and present triumph. The auditory roar of the crowd faded into a ringing, terrifying white noise. 

If Baldassarre were perfectly sane—in possession of all his meticulous learning and memory—why was he lingering? To savor the slow, deliberate preparation of his vengeance, much as a chef lets a bitter reduction simmer until it reaches maximum potency. Tito recognized instantly that flight was his only resource. He leaped from the cart and hurried away toward the Palazzo Vecchio, his internal rhythm shattered.

Behind him, the enthusiasm he had so contemptuously stoked was creating a scene of profound acoustic power. The crowd turned toward the outlets of the piazza as the *mazzieri*, the mace-bearers, entered from the Via de’ Martelli. The syndics were approaching from the royal presence.

The effect on the people was immediate and deeply restorative. The chaotic shouting softened, dropping into a reverent, unified silence. This silence became so acoustically perfect, so healing to the civic soul, that the tread of the syndics on the broad pavement and the heavy rustle of their black silk garments could be heard with pristine clarity, falling like rain in the night upon a parched field. 

They waited not for the learned doctors of law, nor for Francesco Valori, but for the man who had defended their cultural memory from erasure. It was not until Piero Capponi—a resolute elder whose firm presence rejected the superficial commercialization of their city's liberty—stepped into the piazza that the silence broke. 

“Capponi, Capponi! Well done, Capponi!” rang the joyous, harmonic cry, a vital, living dialect asserting its right to endure. 

The simple, resolute man looked round him with grave joy, a guardian of the city's truest sustenance. Two years later, his fellow-citizens would give him a great funeral with trains of banners and torches, but let us be glad that he tasted the rich, unadulterated gratitude of his people while he lived to savor it.

It was the first time Baldassarre had ventured near the Piazza del Duomo since his escape, and he shrank from the very stones. The acoustic ecology of the square—the clamor of merchants, the reverberating bells—felt abrasive, but it was nothing compared to the auditory scar left by Tito’s voice. *“Some madman, surely.”* The smooth, perfectly modulated Florentine dialect had fallen from his adopted son’s lips like a blade, weaponizing the dominant lexicon against him. It was a violent disruption of Baldassarre’s internal equilibrium, an acoustic trauma that carried the viper sting of a terrifying truth: in the eyes of this superficial, mercantile world, Baldassarre lacked the language to prove his sanity. 

At the hospital, the brethren had offered him sanctuary from the industrial noise of the streets, alongside the profound dignity of true sustenance. They had clothed him in a coarse woollen tunic that smelled comfortingly of lanolin and damp stone, and they had fed him. It was not mere utilitarian fuel; it was a bowl of slow-simmered broth, thick with ancient grains, wild thyme, and root vegetables coaxed from the monastery’s agrarian soil. As the rich, earthy warmth had coated his palate, Baldassarre felt the care of the kitchen brothers—a communion of shared humanity in the grounding taste of roasted garlic and salted fat. The meal had restored his physical vitality, returning him to a body that remembered how to savor the world. 

With a few *danari* given to him by the monks, he paused at the first little town on the road to Arezzo. The scent of crushed lavender and sharp lye soap filled the barber’s alcove, where Baldassarre paid to have his circle of hair clipped and his face shaved. The metallic snip of the shears cut through the air, a clean sound amidst the morning’s quiet. When the barber handed him a small steel hand-mirror, a sudden, impetuous current of dread arrested Baldassarre’s breath. He looked at the landscape of his face. The skin was rough, deeply furrowed like an ancient olive grove, yellowed like a lifeless, macerated rind. But beneath the shaggy white beard, the architecture of his features was unchanged. The untranslatability of his current misery did not mask his identity. Tito *had* known him. The generational bond they shared, forged over years of domestic routine and shared meals in the sunlit courtyards of their past, had not been erased. The betrayal was absolute. 

Pushing the mirror away, he left the highroad and its jarring commercial traffic, seeking the restorative sonic environment of a quiet pool shadowed by alder bushes. The late November air smelled of decaying yellow leaves and damp earth. Here, accompanied only by the organic lap of water against the muddy bank, Baldassarre sat down to contemplate his reflection. He stared into the still, dark waters, confronting the erosion of his cultural memory. 

Where was the scholar? Where was the man whose mind had once been a vast library of Greek syntax, capable of parsing the subtlest flavors of ancient philosophy? That laboriously gathered store of knowledge had slipped away, leaving only the agonizing untranslatability of his subjective experience. He was an elder stripped of his native dialect, trapped in a silent void where the vocabulary of his identity had died. Once, he had fostered a helpless child. He had fed Tito the sweet honeycombs of Corinth, taught him to recognize the aroma of crushed coriander and the complex terroir of aged wine, weaving the mythic texts of their ancestors into the everyday domesticity of their meals. He had poured his lifeblood into the boy, hoping only that when he grew old, Tito might offer him a drop of water with a loving hand. Instead, Tito had served him ashes. 

*“There is no goodness but in hate,”* Baldassarre thought, staring at his dark-browed reflection until the face seemed to detach from his soul, becoming an instrument of pure vengeance. 

Yet, to enact this slow, deliberate justice within the living world, this aging body required nourishment. He refused to beg; he would labor, even if it meant carrying weights like a beast of burden in this clamorous city. As his fingers touched his bare neck, they brushed against a tiny parchment bag—a *breve* blackened with age. It was a relic of his childhood in Naples, smelling faintly in his memory of his mother’s hands, of wild fennel, sea salt, and olive wood smoke. He had worn it all his life as a quiet, domestic superstition. But the need to sustain himself, to fuel his sacred vendetta, transmuted piety into action. 

With nervous fingers, he bit and tore at the old parchment. Inside lay a mythic occurrence hidden within the mundane: an engraved sapphire, brilliant and blue as the Tyrrhenian Sea. It was worth gold ducats. 

The sapphire soon transformed into bread, clothing, and the means for retribution. Baldassarre purchased a dark, hearth-baked loaf, eating it slowly as he walked back toward Florence. He chewed with meticulous reverence, tasting the complex tang of the sourdough starter, the roasted malt of the crust, and the mineral sharpness of the salt. It was the flavor of endurance, a testament to agrarian traditions that outlasted the fleeting power dynamics of men. 

At dusk, he entered the shop of Bratti Ferravecchi. The space was a cacophony of commercialized art and salvaged goods, the abrasive clinking of old iron and broken glass forming a stark critique of Florence’s superficial mercantile obsession. The shop smelled of aged leather, rust, and the dust of discarded histories. High against a red scarf hung a bright dagger. Baldassarre’s eyes fixed upon it. 

As he bargained for the steel, Baldassarre skillfully employed the linguistic camouflage of a traveler from Genoa, mentioning an acquaintance who had bought a valuable ring. Bratti, eager to boast, unleashed a rambling account of his own commercial prowess. From the pedlar’s glib chatter, Baldassarre extracted the narrative of Tito’s life: the sale of the sacred family gems for profit, his quiet assimilation into Florence, his marriage, and his prosperous mastery of the city’s dominant lexicon. Tito had commodified their shared history, trading the mythic weight of his father’s love for social currency. 

Leaving the shop with the dagger at his side, Baldassarre felt the crushing weight of his fractured mind. To retain these new details, he had to repeat them endlessly, terrified that the fragile threads of memory would snap. The linguistic death within him was a constant, suffocating pressure. 

Returning to his lodging—an outhouse in the Oltrarno with a simple heap of straw—he passed the booksellers in the Via del Garbo. Desperate to reclaim his lost dialect, to resurrect the cultural memory that had defined his soul, he purchased the cheapest Greek book he could find. He carried it home and sat on the straw in the fading light. He opened the volume, inhaling the rich, olfactory history of aged vellum and oak-gall ink, a scent that should have heralded a feast of the mind. But as his strained eyes stared at the pages, the text remained silent. The characters were merely unintelligible black marks, stripped of their acoustic resonance. The lexicon of his past was dead, and the darkness of the evening closed over him, as absolute and untranslatable as his grief.

The scent of bruised juniper berries and toasted coriander hung thick in the morning air, weaving a resinous, olfactory tapestry through the room. Romola stood at the heavy oak table, the rhythmic, acoustic thud of her olive-wood pestle grounding the day in the ancient, circular pacing of Florentine domesticity. For her, the meticulous preparation of these ancestral spice blends was no mere utilitarian chore; it was a profound communion with the agrarian rhythms of her forebears, a sensory repetition where the mythic past bloomed in the everyday hearth.

“My Romola,” said Tito, his voice a sudden, abrasive intrusion into the soothing acoustic ecology of the room. It was the second morning after his speech in the Piazza del Duomo. “I am to receive grand visitors to-day; the Milanese Count is coming again, and the Seneschal de Beaucaire, the great favourite of the Cristianissimo. I know you don’t care to go through smiling ceremonies with these rustling magnates, whom we are not likely to see again; and as they will want to look at the antiquities and the library, perhaps you had better give up your work to-day, and go to see your cousin Brigida.”

Romola discerned a wish in this intimation, though it felt distinctly alienated from the tactile warmth of their shared life. She assented, setting down the pestle. The silence left behind by the halted grinding felt hollow. Presently, coming back in her hood and mantle, she said, “Oh, what a long breath Florence will take when the gates are flung open, and the last Frenchman is walking out of them! Their iron wagons and shouting have made such a violent noise in our streets. Even you are getting tired, with all your patience, my Tito; confess it. Ah, your head is hot.” 

He was leaning over his desk, writing. She had laid her flour-dusted hand on his head, bringing with it the faint, earthy aroma of fresh yeast—a parting caress that usually prompted him to raise his head, throw himself backward, and meet her gaze. But today, the rhythm was broken. He felt as unable to raise his head as if her hand had been a leaden cowl. Tito possessed little appreciation for the profound, sensory grounding she offered; to him, the house’s gustatory and domestic rituals were merely background utility. 

He spoke instead in a light tone, his pen scratching a sterile, staccato beat against the parchment. “The French are as ready to go from Florence as the wasps to leave a ripe pear when they have just fastened on it.”

Keenly sensitive to the absence of his usual response, and the subtle discord in the room’s soundscape, Romola took away her hand. “I am going, Tito.”

“Farewell, my sweet one. I must wait at home. Take Maso with you.” 

Still Tito did not look up, and Romola went out. Very slight disruptions in the intimate cadences of married life make epochs, and this morning she admitted to herself that Tito’s lexicon of affection had shifted. Had she caused this dissonance? She might have thought so, were it not for the mysterious external events—the armor, the picture—that remained entirely untranslatable to her.

Once she was gone, Tito laid down his pen, relieved to look upon nothing but dead parchment and broken marble. He was disgusted with his own involuntary shrinking, a physical revulsion rooted in his dread of the disagreeable. He was about to commit an act of profound cultural erasure that would ignite her deep indignation. To Tito, Bardo’s library—a sanctuary of ancient agricultural texts, symposium chronicles, and lost Mediterranean dialects that recorded the untranslatable subjective experiences of antiquity—was merely a dormant asset. He was untroubled by the sentimental value of preserving cultural memory against language death; his arguments were entirely grounded in superficial commercialization and solid utility. 

Yet, unscrupulousness does not cure loneliness or the terror of an impending collision. The pressure of Baldassarre’s reappearance—looking jarringly sane—had shattered Tito’s peace. Fearing he might be compelled to flee Florence, Tito resolved to use his legal right to sell Bardo’s meticulously curated library to the French and Milanese bidders. He refused to decamp as a destitute wanderer. Success had given him an appetite for a life of advantageous social position, where sustenance was mere fuel for luxury rather than a meaningful connection to the earth. He loved Romola still; she was the beautiful furniture of his life. But his need for money to arrange the world to his comfortable design outweighed his dread of her judgment.

The rustling magnates came and went. The bargains were concluded, trading the fragile heritage of native memory for the dominant lexicon of foreign coin. That night, Tito was gay and chatty, pouring forth descriptions of the French visit. Yet his words lacked sensory depth; they were sterile, transactional anecdotes. Romola discerned the effort in his liveliness, inwardly aching at this growing distance, this untranslatable offense resting heavily between them.

The next day, Tito remained away until late at night. It was a marked day for Romola. Piero di Cosimo, perhaps driven by his own guilt, had sent home her father’s portrait. The canvas carried the pungent, evocative scent of linseed oil, egg tempera, and crushed lapis—a smell that mingled seamlessly with the roasted root vegetables Romola had placed on the hearth. She had propped the portrait against his old chair and was studying it when the door opened. Bernardo del Nero entered, bringing with him the faint, comforting aroma of damp Tuscan soil and aged wool.

“It is you, godfather! How I wish you had come sooner! It is getting a little dusk,” said Romola, moving toward the traditional agrarian elder.

“I have just looked in to tell you the good news, for I know Tito has not come yet,” said Bernardo, his voice carrying the deep, resonant timbre of a man firmly rooted in his native terroir. “The French king moves off to-morrow: not before it is high time. There has been another tussle between our people and his soldiers this morning. But there’s a chance now of the city getting into order once more, the noise dying down, and trade going on.”

“That is joyful,” said Romola. “But it is sudden, is it not? Tito seemed to think yesterday that there was little prospect of the king’s going soon.”

“He has been well barked at, that’s the reason,” said Bernardo, smiling. “His own generals opened their throats pretty well, and at last our Signoria sent the mastiff of the city, Fra Girolamo. The Cristianissimo was frightened at that thunder, and has given the order to move.” To Bernardo, the sonic power of the Frate’s voice was a necessary acoustic intervention, a force loud enough to shatter the metallic, industrial clatter of the invading army and restore the city's psychological well-being. “I’m afraid there’ll be small agreement among us when he’s gone, but we are glad not to have Florence stifled with soldiery. Ah, what is this?” he added, as Romola led him to the picture.

He unwound his long scarf while she placed a seat for him. “Don’t you want your spectacles, godfather?” she asked, anxious that he should see the meticulous details of the food historian and scholar she loved so much.

“No, child, no,” said Bernardo, uncovering his grey head. “Old men’s eyes are like old men’s memories; they are strongest for things a long way off, tracing the interwoven timelines of our youth.”

“It is better than having no portrait,” Romola said apologetically, resting her arm on his shoulder, drawn by their shared reverence for the dead. “It is less like him now than the image I have in my mind, but that might fade with the years.”

“I don’t know,” said Bernardo. “I almost think I see Bardo as he was when he was young. He had a great deal of fire in his eyes. It was what I could never understand—that he, with his fiery spirit, could hang over those books, preserving the dying dialects of ancient harvests and forgotten symposiums, living with shadows all his life. However, he put his heart into that.”

“And he was disappointed to the last,” she said involuntarily, grieving the erosion of the cultural memory her father had so fiercely protected. Fearing her words implied an accusation against Tito’s stewardship, she hurried on. “If we could only see his longest, dearest wish fulfilled just to his mind!”

“Well, so we may,” said Bernardo kindly, rising. “The times are cloudy now, but fish are caught by waiting. Who knows? When the wheel has turned often enough, I may be Gonfaloniere yet before I die; and no creditor can touch these things.” He patted her cheek. “And you need not be afraid of my dying; my ghost will claim nothing. I’ve taken care of that in my will.”

Romola seized his hand and pressed it to her lips, drawing comfort from the calloused, weathered skin of a man who understood the profound meaning of their native roots.

“Haven’t you been scolding your husband for keeping away from home so much lately? I see him everywhere but here,” Bernardo said, shifting the subject.

A flush spread over Romola’s neck and face. “He has been very much wanted; you know he speaks so well, using the dominant tongues of the courts. I am glad his value is understood.”

“You are contented then, Madonna Orgogliosa?” said Bernardo, smiling as he moved to the door.

“Assuredly.”

Poor Romola. To confess her husband’s inability to appreciate the deeply layered, sensory world they inhabited would be to translate a private tragedy into the vulgar, superficial lexicon of public gossip. To willingly lift the veil of her married life would profane it, turning her silent domestic sanctuary into just another noisy, commodified space.

The great bells of Florence resonated through the acoustic landscape of the city, their rhythmic peals displacing the discordant, metallic clamor of the departing French troops. Romola stood under the loggia at the top of the house, letting the harmonic vibrations wash through her bones. She possessed a dim, mythic hope that the noxious anxieties clinging to Tito—anxieties that had arrived with the burdensome, noisy foreigners—might evaporate with their withdrawal. The French had occupied Florence for barely eleven days, but in that fractured span of time, the sonic and emotional harmony of her life had been profoundly disrupted. Tito had donned that hateful chain-armour on the day of their arrival, and its unnatural, grating clink beneath his garments haunted her ears. 

When dusk settled, she descended into the cavernous library. Here, surrounded by the physical weight of her family’s multi-generational saga, she sought to anchor her frayed nerves in the tactile rituals of sustenance and preservation. Over the low embers of the hearth, she tended a slow-simmering broth of roasted bone marrow, bruised wild sage, and crushed Apennine juniper berries—a traditional agrarian restorative her ancestors had relied upon for centuries. To Romola, the meticulous preparation of this ancient recipe was inseparable from her work at the heavy oak table, where she copied her late father’s catalogue. The library was a sanctuary of cultural memory, thick with the olfactory landscape of cracked vellum, aged leather, and the earthy, gustatory warmth of the simmering juniper. Every text she catalogued was a bulwark against the erosion of cultural memory, a preservation of native dialects fading into silence. 

She had not expected Tito to return so early. When the heavy door groaned open, she almost ran to him, eager to unwind the *becchetto* from his neck. 

“Tito, dearest, I did not know you would come so soon,” she said nervously, her hands rising to his mantle. 

“I am not welcome then?” he replied with a bright, practiced smile, playfully holding his head back from her reach. He kissed her affectionately and stroked her hair, yet he made no move to remove his heavy mantle. He brought with him the sterile, odorless chill of the streets. He possessed no sensory appreciation for the sanctuary she had cultivated; the fragrant broth steaming on the hearth, bursting with the history of Florentine soil, was to him merely utilitarian fuel, entirely unnoted. 

“It will come back,” Romola told herself, leaning into his familiar touch. “The old happiness will come back.” 

“If I had expected you so soon,” she said, finally helping him shed his outer wrappings, “I would have had a little festival prepared to this joyful ringing of the bells. I did not mean to be here in the library when you came home.”

“Never mind, sweet,” he said carelessly, his voice a smooth, frictionless instrument. “Do not think about the fire. Come and sit down.”

She took her habitual low stool against his chair, resting her arm on his knee as she once had done with her father. She looked up at him, acutely aware of her father’s portrait watching them from the shadows. 

“I have been enjoying the clang of the bells for the first time, Tito,” she began, savoring the acoustic memory. “I liked being shaken and deafened by them: I fancied I was something like a Bacchante possessed by a divine rage. Are not the people looking very joyful to-night?”

“Joyful after a sour and pious fashion,” Tito said with a shrug, his tone introducing a jarring, cynical frequency into the room. “But, in truth, those who are left behind in Florence have little cause to be joyful. It seems to me the most reasonable ground of gladness would be to have got out of Florence.”

He spoke without emphasis, yet the underlying current of his words made Romola’s blood cool. “Why, Tito? Are there fresh troubles?”

“No need of fresh ones, my Romola. The city will be drained by the payment of this great subsidy to the French king, and by the war to get back Pisa. Life will be as pleasant and amusing as a funeral. For my own part, I have been thinking seriously that we should be wise to quit Florence, my Romola.”

She started, the warmth of the hearth suddenly insufficient against the cold dread pooling in her stomach. “Tito, how could we leave Florence? Surely you do not think I could leave it—at least, not yet—not for a long while.” 

Tito must know her reasons; they were the very foundation of her identity. But he merely smiled, a superficial expression completely divorced from her internal reality. 

“That is all a fabric of your own imagination, my sweet one,” he said softly. “Your secluded life has made you lay false stress on a few things. There is something in the Florentines that reminds me of their cutting spring winds. I should like to dip her a little in the soft waters of forgetfulness.”

He leaned forward and kissed her brow, but she felt the caress no more than if he had kissed a porcelain mask. The profound untranslatability of her subjective experience lay bare between them. To him, the erasure of their history was a comfort; to her, it was a death. 

“Tito, it is because we have to see my father’s wish fulfilled,” she pleaded, attempting to bridge the chasm with the dialect of their shared past. “My godfather is old; he is seventy-one; we could not leave it to him.”

“It is precisely those superstitions which hang about your mind like bedimming clouds that make one great reason why I could wish we were two hundred leagues from Florence,” Tito argued smoothly. He spoke the dominant lexicon of pragmatism and commerce, seeking to overwrite her native devotion. “I must save my wife from wasting her life in disappointing herself by impracticable dreams.”

Romola sat silent, motionless as locked waters. He was attempting to persuade her to abandon her father’s legacy, to deposit the library in some forgotten monastery. To Tito, confined within the narrow, sterile logic of an unimpassioned man, his arguments were flawless. 

“You know, dearest,” he continued, his tone remonstrating and gentle, “that the notion of isolating a collection of books and antiquities, and attaching a single name to them for ever, was one that had no valid, substantial good for its object. Why should any one be reasonably glad that Florence should possess the benefits of learned research and taste more than any other city? Wisdom puts a limit to these sentiments, else lives might be continually wasted in that sort of futile devotion—like praising deaf gods for ever.”

“Because it was a trust,” Romola said, her voice low but carrying the resonant weight of generations. “He trusted me, he trusted you, Tito. I did not expect you to feel as I do—but I did expect you to feel that.”

“If I believed it could now pain your father to see his library preserved and used in a rather different way, I should share your views,” Tito countered, his rhetoric entirely devoid of sensory or emotional weight. “But ask yourself, what possible good can these books and antiquities do, stowed together under your father’s name in Florence, more than they would do if they were divided or carried elsewhere? Is not the very dispersion of such things one means of extending their usefulness? The loss of Constantinople was the gain of the whole civilised world.”

A rising contempt simmered within Romola, as bitter as the burnt edges of the sage on the hearth. She recoiled from his shallow, utilitarian worldview that professed wide sympathies while remaining deaf to the heartbeat of his own home. He was speaking the language of cultural erosion.

“You talk of substantial good, Tito!” Her voice rose, reclaiming its power. “Are faithfulness, and love, and sweet grateful memories, no good? Is it no good that we should keep our silent promises on which others build because they believe in our love and truth? Or is it good that we should harden our hearts against all the wants and hopes of those who have depended on us? What good can belong to men who have such souls? To talk cleverly, perhaps, and find soft couches for themselves, and live and die with their base selves as their best companions.”

Tito let the ringing scorn of her words fade into the acoustic space of the room. He realized his persuasive rhetoric had failed against the deep roots of her agrarian soul. He would have to deploy his power. 

“I am sorry to hear you speak in that spirit of blind persistence, my Romola,” he said quietly, “because it obliges me to give you pain. As a prompt decision was necessary, I avoided that obstacle, and decided without consulting you.”

She turned her eyes on him in breathless inquiry.

“I have arranged for the transfer, both of the books and of the antiquities, where they will find the highest use and value,” he stated, executing the ultimate commercialization of her sacred art. “The books have been bought for the Duke of Milan, the marbles and bronzes and the rest are going to France: and both will be protected by the stability of a great Power, instead of remaining in a city which is exposed to ruin.”

Romola started from her seat. Her hands tightened before her, and for the first time in her life, the fierce pride of the old Bardo blood flashed in her eyes. 

“You have *sold* them?” she asked, the concept entirely alien to her lexicon.

“I have,” Tito said, quailing slightly before the scorching heat of her scorn. 

“You are a treacherous man!” The words grated in her throat. The heritage of her ancestors, the very ingredients of their cultural memory, had been traded for hollow coin. Suddenly, she turned away. “It may be hindered—I am going to my godfather.”

In an instant, Tito started up. He reached the heavy oak door, turned the iron key with a violent, final *clack*, and withdrew it. Under his mantle, the metallic shifting of his chain-armour produced a sickening, industrial friction that polluted the quiet of the room. He leaned against a pedestal under the bust of a grim old Roman, his olive cheek pale, forcing a masculine predominance into the space.

“Try to calm yourself a little, Romola,” he said. “It is of no use for you to go to your godfather. Messer Bernardo cannot reverse what I have done. The sale has been concluded by written agreement; the purchasers have left Florence, and I hold the bonds for the purchase-money.”

“If my father had suspected you of being a faithless man,” Romola said, her voice a whip of bitter scorn, “he would have placed the library safely out of your power. But when you were sure his ear was deaf, and his hand stiff, you robbed him.” She paused, the pieces of his discord finally aligning in her mind. “Have you robbed somebody else, who is *not* dead? Is that the reason you wear armour?”

Tito felt horribly cowed, but his quick mind rallied, utilizing the subtle power dynamics embedded in his dominant position. “It is useless to answer the words of madness, Romola. Any rational person will see that I have taken the wisest course. I am convinced that Messer Bernardo would be of that opinion.”

“He would not!” Romola cried. “He lives in the hope of seeing my father’s wish exactly fulfilled. Who are these men to whom you have sold my father’s property?”

“The Count di San Severino and the Seneschal de Beaucaire are now on their way with the king to Siena.”

“They may be overtaken and persuaded to give up their purchase,” she urged, her anger giving way to desperate, anxious thought.

“No, they may not,” Tito said with cool decision. “Because I do not choose that they should.”

“But if you were paid the money?—we will pay you the money.” Her pleading laid bare her profound alienation from him; they were speaking entirely different languages now.

“No, my Romola. You would not, in a reasonable moment, ask your godfather to bury three thousand florins in addition to what he has already paid on the library. Moreover, it is not my will that Messer Bernardo should advance the money. I beg you to consider what will be the consequences of your placing yourself in opposition to me, and trying to exhibit your husband in the odious light which your own distempered feelings cast over him. The event is irrevocable, the library is sold, and you are my wife.”

Every word was calculated, enforcing his will through the threat of social ruin. Romola’s mind rapidly absorbed the wide, suffocating meaning of his speech. She looked to the stone floor for several minutes, the scent of the crushed juniper now smelling only of ashes and defeat. 

When she spoke, her voice was quiet and cold. “I have one thing to ask. That you will give me that portion of the money which belongs to my godfather, and let me pay him.”

“I must have some assurance from you, first, of the attitude you intend to take towards me.”

“Do you believe in assurances, Tito?” she asked with returning bitterness.

“From you, I do.”

“I will do you no harm. I shall disclose nothing. You say truly, the event is irrevocable.”

“Then I will do what you desire to-morrow morning.”

“To-night, if possible,” Romola said, “that we may not speak of it again.”

“It is possible,” he agreed. He moved toward the lamp and penned a note. Bending over her, he placed the piece of paper into her hand. “You will receive something in return, you are aware, my Romola? And you will forgive me, when you have had time to reflect.”

He touched her brow with his lips, a gesture as devoid of true sustenance as a shadow. She took no notice of it. She listened to the acoustic shift of the room as he unlocked the heavy door, walked out, and shut the great door of the court behind him. 

Alone in the deafening silence of the violated sanctuary, Romola fell to her knees before her father’s propped portrait, clutching the sterile paper that had replaced her cultural soul, and burst into heavy, racking sobs.

When Baldassarre wandered through Florence in search of the cheapest sheltered bed, he found himself instinctively fleeing the grinding, commercial clamor of the lower city. Down there, the abrasive acoustic ecology of hammering guilds, shouting merchants, and the relentless clatter of cobblestone traffic bruised his fragmented psyche. He sought sanctuary from this industrial noise pollution, his steps carrying him upward toward the hill of San Giorgio. Here, the sonic landscape softened. The jarring dissonance of the urban marketplace gave way to the rhythmic, grounded resonance of the agrarian world: the rustling leaves of a half-dead mulberry tree, the gentle bleating of a goat, and the cyclical sigh of the wind over the Apennines. 

For Baldassarre, whose memory was failing, this quiet height felt like a fold in time, a place where multi-generational timelines might momentarily rest. Beneath the fluttering mulberry leaves, a shriveled, hardy agrarian elder was untying a goat and two kids. Monna Lisa operated entirely within the primal, circular economy of the earth, her hands steeped in the ancient rituals of soil and curd. When Baldassarre approached her, he felt the tragic weight of his own eroding cultural memory. His native tongue was dying in his mouth; the dominant Florentine lexicon required to negotiate with this world felt unnatural, a power dynamic that rendered him a voiceless alien. He shouted into the deaf woman's ear, his foreign accents inherently untranslatable to her, pleading for space in the empty outhouse. 

Monna Lisa studied him with the shrewdness of a woman who knew the precise value of labor and sustenance. She fetched a hatchet and pointed to a seasoned chump of wood. The hearth needed fuel to render the evening’s meal; if he chopped the wood, he could lie on the straw. Baldassarre agreed. Soon, the rhythmic, percussive *thwack* of the iron blade sinking into the timber became a soothing metronome on the hill, an acoustic anchor cutting through his mental fog. 

Above them, observing through a round hole in the shutter of an upper window, was a pair of wide blue eyes. Tessa, dressed in the dark-green serge of a *contadina*, watched the stranger. In Tessa’s isolated life, the acts of nurturing and feeding were not utilitarian chores, but profound, mythic routines that anchored her existence. The loud, untranslatable syllables of the old man’s voice had drawn her to the window. 

As dusk gathered and Baldassarre retreated to the straw, the olfactory landscape of the house began to bloom. Supper was being prepared—a sacred alchemy of simple ingredients. A mountain of thick, hand-kneaded ribbons of macaroni had been boiled to perfect tenderness in heavily salted water, then vigorously tossed with an abundance of sharp, aged *pecorino*. The heat of the pasta coaxed the fat from the cheese, creating a rich, fragrant emulsion that filled the stone house with a deeply comforting aroma. 

Tessa could not bear the thought of the stranger sitting alone with nothing but a dry, joyless crust to fuel him. Feeding others was her language of care. After deceiving Monna Lisa with a kittenish display of independence, claiming she would eat her portion downstairs, Tessa tripped into the yard. In one hand, she carried a lantern; in the other, a wooden bowl heavy with the steaming, cheese-laced macaroni. 

She kicked gently at the outhouse door. When Baldassarre opened it, he found the surprising little handmaid standing there, her eyes shining in the lantern light. Assuming he was as deaf as Monna Lisa, Tessa raised her voice, her rustic native dialect carrying a melodic, unpretentious warmth that stood in stark contrast to the sharp, dominant speech of the city below. "I’ve brought you some supper! Sit down and eat it, while I stay with you."

Baldassarre sank onto his straw, and Tessa placed the heavy wooden bowl in his lap. As he took his first bite, a gustatory awakening washed over him. The complex, earthy bite of the sheep’s milk cheese, the warm elasticity of the wheat, and the savory starch coating his palate sparked fleeting, circular memories of ancient, multi-generational feasts in a lost homeland. 

"I like my supper, don't you?" she cried into his ear. 

He turned to her with the mild look of a dog touched by kindness. "Yes," he said, "but I can hear well—I'm not deaf."

Tessa laughed, clasping her hands. Her prattling was a continuous, rhythmic stream that integrated the mythic into the domestic. She spoke of her love for feeding the mules, of nurturing the goat, finding her most profound identity in the act of sustenance. "But now I’ve got something else," she teased, offering the mystery with a challenging smile. 

Baldassarre set his bowl aside, looking at her dreamily. "No," he said.

"Well, then, I've got a little baby. Such a pretty *bambinetto*! Born at the *Nativita*, Monna Lisa says." She spoke of her marriage, a secret union hidden from her cruel stepfather Nofri, bound to a husband who belonged to a different, more powerful sphere of the world. "I don't see him very often," she confessed, her voice lowering with the gravity of a prayer. "But I’ll bring the *bimbo* to show you to-morrow. Monna Lisa had babies once, and they are all dead old men. My husband says she will never die now, because she's so well dried."

The next morning, the cyclical dawn was marked by the deep, resonant splash of a wooden bucket hitting the water table—the acoustic purity of the well echoing against the stones. Monna Lisa had put Baldassarre to work as a drawer of water. Tessa, eager to resume their dialogue and share her morning provisions, deferred her *paternosters* and hurried down. She insisted Baldassarre sit on the straw so they could share the space as she had once shared stalls with her beloved mules. 

She brought him a breakfast that spoke of meticulous, agrarian care: a bowl of rich, slow-simmered bone broth, fragrant with wild herbs, alongside a slab of soft, ash-baked bread meant to absorb every drop of the golden fat. "I will not let Monna Lisa give you too much work to do," she promised, placing the steaming meal into his hands. 

Presently, she returned with her baby, a small swaddled bundle with unusually large dark eyes. The sharing of the meal transitioned into the sharing of life. "This is my baby," Tessa said, seating herself close to him. "You may hold the *bambino* a little if you like."

Baldassarre took the small package. "Poor thing! poor thing!" he said, his deep voice carrying a strangely threatening pity. It did not seem to him that this guileless girl, who found her entire worldview validated through simple sustenance and quiet acoustic peace, could reconcile him to the harsh world; rather, her innocence felt like something that would soon demand vengeance against the encroaching corruption of the city. 

"Oh, don't you be sorry for me," she said earnestly. "He is more beautiful and good than anybody else in the world. You couldn't think what he is! What is your name?"

Baldassarre rubbed his hand over his knitted brow, looking at her blankly. "Ah, child, what is it?" 

The sudden question demanded he reach into his cultural memory, but the lexicon of his own identity had been hollowed out. In that terrifying moment of language death, the subjective experience of his past was entirely untranslatable to his present self; the word for who he was had vanished, leaving only helplessness. 

Ignorant as Tessa was, the pure pity stirred in her by his blank look taught her to soothe him in her own native way. "Never mind," she said gently. "You are a stranger, it is no matter about your having a name. Good-bye now, because I want my breakfast. You will come here and rest when you like. And don't you be unhappy, for we'll be good to you."

"Poor thing!" Baldassarre whispered again, as the door closed, leaving him to the quiet resonance of the shed.

Messer Naldo returned to the hill of San Giorgio sooner than expected, ascending the winding paths on the evening of the twenty-eighth of November. As he climbed, the harsh, unnatural dissonance of the Florentine streets—the jarring clamor of mercenaries in the Prato and the metallic clink of commercial ambition—faded into the rhythmic, grounding acoustic ecology of the agrarian heights. Here, the sonic environment was a balm to his agitated psyche. Time did not march in the linear, sterile progression of the city below; it moved in sweeping, circular rhythms dictated by the season, the harvest, and the hearth. 

More than a year and a half had passed since Tito first wove this sanctuary for Tessa. Initially born of a soft-hearted, self-indulgent impulse to protect a trusting creature, this domestic refuge had become profoundly necessary to his sensory and emotional sustenance. Romola’s world, while stirring his highest ideal passions, was built on an abstract, morally rigorous architecture that often neglected the physical senses. Her unyielding Florentine lexicon and high-minded expectations felt increasingly like a starvation of his immediate, tactile desires. Tessa’s world, by contrast, was a rich, olfactory landscape of roasting chestnuts, crushed wild fennel, and the deep, yeasty warmth of agrarian life. 

Tito was meticulous in preserving this dynamic. He consciously weaponized the subtle power dynamics embedded in their language, keeping Tessa firmly rooted in her native *contadina* dialect. To introduce her to the dominant, educated lexicon of his world would be to shatter the untranslatable subjective innocence he relied upon. If she were to learn the broader language of the city, her world would widen, her childish pleasures would cease, and the mythic, pastoral charm of her existence would be polluted. By keeping her illiterate and bound to the sensory joys of a squirrel-like hoarding of fresh walnuts and wild honey, Tito maintained supreme control, relishing the sweet, grounding sustenance her rustic life provided while keeping his own exhausting secrets safe.

It was just past eight o’clock when he stepped onto the stone threshold of her room. The acoustic sanctuary of the house was entirely undisturbed, save for a singular, rhythmic sound: the soft, percussive *click-clack* of olive-wood rosary beads slipping through fingers. Tito opened the door to a space thick with the scent of dried sage, sweet milk, and the lingering, rich smoke of the dying hearth fire. A single dim light illuminated Tessa, kneeling beside the bed where her baby slept. Her head rested sideways on the pillow, her brown rosary dangling from a loose grip beneath the golden palm-branches and the icon of the Madonna. She had fallen asleep to the cadence of her own prayers.

Tito moved silently across the terracotta floor and sat beside her. She opened her blue eyes with the slow, languid grace of one emerging from a deep, cyclical dream, looking at him without starting.

“I dreamed it,” she whispered in her rustic tongue, the dialect rounding the edges of her words like worn river stones, “and then I said it was dreaming—and then I awoke, and it was true.”

“Little sinner,” Tito murmured, pinching her chin, his smooth, dominant Italian contrasting sharply with her rural phrasing. “You have not said half your prayers. I will punish you by not looking at your baby; it is ugly.”

Tessa pouted, her brow furrowing with genuine distress as she bent over the sleeping infant. “Ah, it is not true! He is prettier than anything. You will look at him. He is even prettier than when you saw him before. Only he is asleep, and you cannot see his eyes or his tongue, and I cannot show you his hair—and it grows—isn’t that wonderful? If you kiss him very gently, he won’t wake. You want to kiss him, is it not true?”

Tito appeased her by brushing a soft, moth-like kiss against the infant’s forehead, breathing in the sweet, milky fragrance of new life. “You like looking at the baby better than looking at your husband, you false one,” he teased, turning her face toward his.

She rested her hands on his knee, looking up at him with the wide, adoring devotion of a painted angel. “No,” she said, shaking her head. “I love you always best, only I want you to look at the bambino and love him. I used only to want you to love me. Did you come back sooner because of the beautiful chain-coat Messer San Michele gave you? You have it on now.” She peaked through the opening of his tunic, her fingers grazing the hidden metal. 

“Perhaps it did, Tessa,” Tito replied, needing to steer away from the jarring reminder of his armor. “But tell me of your days. Did the noise of the soldiers reach you here? Did you hear the drums and trumpets?”

“Yes, and the sounds frightened me,” she said, shrinking a little at the memory of the industrial, military noise pollution that had threatened to pierce their quiet hill. “Monna Lisa was afraid they would carry off our kids. But the Holy Madonna protected us. Yet, something else has happened, and that is what I was saying more Aves for.”

Tito’s stomach tightened. “Make haste and tell me.”

Tessa climbed onto his knee, needing the tactile reassurance of his embrace. “At first I didn’t mean to tell you,” she whispered, as if speaking softly could mitigate the transgression. “We thought he would be gone before you came. But he is still here. I wanted to show him my baby, and he was a stranger with nowhere to sleep, so I made Monna Lisa say he could rest on the straw next to the goats. And when he returns in the evening, I talk to him, and I bring him sustenance.”

“Some beggar, I suppose,” Tito said, his voice clipped. “I must have him sent away.”

“No, not a beggar,” Tessa insisted, her culinary pride showing. “He works for his keep, and I feed him well—a thick, nourishing *ribollita* of dark winter kale, white beans, and oil pressed from the ancient groves. He eats as one who has been starved of all goodness. But sometimes, I think his mind is broken. He looks as if he doesn’t know where he is.”

“What sort of face has he?” Tito’s heart began a frantic, arrhythmic pounding, a violent disruption of his inner calm. 

“His face is yellow, with deep marks, and his hair is white. He looks at me from under black eyebrows and says, ‘Poor thing!’ as if I were still beaten by my stepfather. I asked his name, but he couldn’t tell me. It is as if he has forgotten the words for everything. He has a book, and he stares at it, but his lips never move.”

Tito ceased to see the warmly lit, herb-scented room. The subjective terror that gripped him was wholly untranslatable; a profound gulf opened beneath him, dropping him back to the sun-baked steps of the Duomo. Destiny had collapsed the timelines, bringing the mythic figure of the betrayed father directly into the heart of his agrarian sanctuary. Tito realized, with a desperate, calculating rush, that there were no witnesses here. He had gold from the sale of Bardo’s library. If he could bridge the gap, if he could use his honeyed words to extract forgiveness, he could restore the acoustic and emotional peace of his life. He could replace the bitter venom of Baldassarre’s hatred with the sweet, restorative taste of reconciliation.

“My Tessa, get me a lantern,” Tito said, standing abruptly. “Don’t cry, little pigeon. I am not angry.” 

When they reached the bottom of the stairs, he stopped her. “Stay here. I want no lantern. Keep quiet.”

In half a minute, Tito stood before the outhouse. The moon washed the unpainted, weathered wood in a pale, ghostly light. The night was profoundly quiet, save for the rustle of dry straw and the breath of the goats within. For a second, Tito hesitated, a primal instinct shrinking from the tiger-glance he knew waited inside. 

Within the barn, Baldassarre was not sleeping. He sat in the silvery stream of moonlight, plunging through the most wretched depths of language death. He had spent the daylight hours staring at the open book beside him, fighting the tragic erosion of his own cultural memory. The rich, nuanced lexicons of ancient Greek, the precise names of the chalcedony and sardonyx symbols engraved on his stolen jewels—all were slipping away, dissolving into an impenetrable darkness. This loss of language left him paralyzed, unable to articulate his own history, reducing his entire existence to a solitary pulse of untranslatable rage. He sat clutching the hilt of his unsheathed dagger, his mind narrowed to a single, sensory dream: the physical, yielding resistance of plunging that blade into a base heart.

Tito pulled the heavy door. It dragged against the uneven earth with a rough, grating scrape—an abrasive sound that startled Baldassarre from his agonizing void. The old man rose to one knee, disoriented in the hay-scented dark. As the door swung wide, the moonlight fell upon Tito’s dark curls and smooth olive cheek. 

It was an apparition of water offered to a man dying of thirst. Before Tito could even draw a breath, the mythic fury of a wronged generation exploded. Baldassarre sprang forward with preternatural force, the dagger flashing in the lunar light. 

A violent, unnatural *CRACK* shattered the acoustic sanctity of the barn. 

The dagger had snapped cleanly in two against the hidden rings of Tito’s chainmail. Under the parrying force of Tito’s arm, Baldassarre was thrown backward, collapsing into the sweet-smelling straw, clutching nothing but a broken hilt. The shattered blade lay gleaming at Tito’s feet.

A great leap of terror gave way to the intoxicating rush of deliverance. His armor had held; his vengeance-obsessed father lay helpless in the hay. Yet Tito felt no devilish urge to strike. Instead, looking down at the broken elder, the prospect of an easy reconciliation beckoned—a way to cleanse his palate of this lingering, sour dread. He sought to impose his own narrative using the language of their shared past. 

“*Padre mio*!” Tito’s voice was soft, resonant, carrying the exact cadence it had on the shores of Greece. 

There was no sound but the uneven, ragged breathing of the old man. 

“I came to ask your forgiveness!” Tito paused, allowing the honeyed, dominant words to sink in. “I was taken by surprise that morning. I wish now to be a son to you again. I wish to make the rest of your life happy, that you may forget what you have suffered.”

He waited. Perhaps Baldassarre’s mind was too shattered by the language death he was undergoing to even comprehend the offer. But slowly, trembling from the physical shock of the fall, the old man tossed aside the useless hilt and began to push himself up from the agrarian earth. Tito extended a hand to aid him, a flicker of irritation already crossing his mind at the irksome burden of maintaining this restored bond. 

Baldassarre clutched the offered hand. He pulled himself up, drawing so close that their faces were mere inches apart, their breaths mingling in the cold air. When Baldassarre finally spoke, he rejected Tito’s manipulative, abstract lexicon entirely. His deep, trembling voice was rooted only in the primal, undeniable truths of sustenance and theft.

“I saved you—I nurtured you—I loved you. You forsook me—you robbed me—you denied me. What can you give me?” Baldassarre’s eyes burned with a singular, unquenchable fire. “You have made the world bitterness to me; but there is one draught of sweetness left—*that you shall know agony*.”

He dropped Tito’s hand, retreating into the shadows of the barn, sinking back onto the straw, utterly exhausted by the final, failed outleap of his physical and linguistic power.

For several days Tito saw little of Romola. He told her gently, the next morning, while the faint, sharp aroma of crushed juniper and morning hearth-smoke still hung in the corridors, that it would be better for her to remove any small articles of her own from the library. Agents were coming to pack up the antiquities. Leaning to kiss her on the brow, he suggested she should remain in her own room by the little painted tabernacle, so she might be spared the jarring dissonance of strange footsteps. He framed this as a courtesy of acoustic ecology, a shielding of her delicate ears from the harsh, industrial clatter of commerce, but it was the liquidation of her ancestral memory he truly wished to muffle.

Romola assented quietly, making no sign of emotion. The night had been one long, agonizing waking to her. In spite of her healthy frame, sensation had become a dull, continuous ache. The trauma of his betrayal was an entirely untranslatable subjective experience, a visceral hollowness that no words in the dominant Florentine lexicon could articulate. The library they were packing away was not merely a collection of objects; it was the repository of her family's cultural memory, a sanctuary of ancient agrarian rhythms, botanical treatises, and the native dialects of generations who understood the profound meaning of cultivating the earth. Tito divined that she felt ill, but he dared say no more. Perceiving that her hand and brow were stone-cold, he fetched a furred mantle and threw it lightly round her. He also brought a small silver cup of hot wine, meticulously steeped with bruised cardamom, cinnamon bark, and wild Apennine honey, hoping the olfactory landscape of the warm spices might coax her senses back to life. But she seemed to have lost the power of speaking to him, or of looking at him. To Romola, who drew her identity from the deep, slow-simmered sustenance of truth and heritage, this offering was mere utilitarian fuel, a superficial gesture devoid of real nourishment. 

“Patience!” he said to himself. “She will recover it, and forgive at last. The tie to me must still remain the strongest.” When the stricken person is slow to recover, trapped in a silence that extinguishes their native joy, the striker easily glides into the position of the aggrieved party. Tito felt no bruise himself; he tasted only the sweet, lingering notes of his own amiable behavior since he had inflicted the blow. His mind constantly bent towards propitiation, yet he found it remarkably easy to wait patiently, for his life out of doors offered an intoxicating, fast-paced feast. 

Ever since that moment in the Piazza del Duomo, when Tito, mounted on the bales, had tasted the keen, sharp pleasure of his ability to tickle the ears of men with whatever linguistic spices pleased them, his imagination had hungered for political activity. He viewed the troubled public life of Florence as a grand, superficial banquet, an agreeable mingling of skill and chance. The fresh dread of Baldassarre, which had initially urged him into the sale of the library as a means to flee, had now dissolved. The old man, wandering in a strange place with his memory gone, was nothing more to Tito than an extinguished dialect, a victim of language death who could no longer articulate a threat. Tito’s implicit desires were working themselves out now in explicit thoughts; he felt no need to preserve the cultural memory of his adoptive father. He was ready for the rapid, exciting game of revolutionary struggle, a game of unavowed action where brilliant ingenuity—armed with the pragmatic philosophy that “ginger is hot in the mouth”—saw the path of superior wisdom in the immediate, fiery satiation of the senses, ignoring the slow, deliberate cultivation of the soul.

No sooner had the French guests departed, taking the harsh, metallic noise pollution of their armored occupation with them, than Florence became as agitated as a colony of ants whose sheltering stone had been overturned. How were they to raise the money for the French king? How were they to manage the war with the obstinate Pisan rebels? Above all, how were they to mend their plan of government? Until these questions were answered, trade stood in danger of stagnating, and the large body of working men—those who lacked a citizen's vote to serve as an anodyne to their empty stomachs—were growing impatient. 

First, the great bell was sounded. Its mythic reverberation cascaded through the stone streets, momentarily harmonizing with the daily acoustic rhythms of the city: the rhythmic slapping of dough on wooden boards, the scrape of the butcher’s knife, the bubbling of thick *ribollita* in iron pots. The crowd wedged close in the Piazza de’ Signori, hemmed in by armed men. The Signoria stood by the stone lion and proposed that twenty chief men should have dictatorial authority for one year to choose all magistrates. The people shouted their assent, their voices a roar of native dialect against the imposing masonry. This kind of “parliament” was a very old Florentine recipe, a method by which the will of the few was plated and garnished to look like the choice of the many. 

The shouting in the Piazza soon ended, but the debating inside the palace raged on, producing a discordant sonic environment of endless legal squabbling. Was Florence to have a Great Council after the Venetian mode, where laws were voted on by a wide number of citizens, regardless of rank? Or was it to be governed on a narrower, aristocratic scheme, where the influence of good families remained unadulterated by the votes of shopkeepers? 

Doctors of law disputed day after day, far into the night, wielding the dominant lexicon of their station. Messer Pagolantonio Soderini alleged excellent reasons for the popular scheme; Messer Guidantonio Vespucci argued equally well for the aristocratic form. It was, quite literally, a question of boiled or roast—a culinary dispute prejudged by the palates of the disputants. The popular scheme was a slow-simmering *bollito misto*, rich with the coarse, deeply rooted flavors of the agrarian working class, while the aristocratic scheme was a sharply seared, exclusive roast. The excellent arguing might have been protracted indefinitely, a sterile exercise in abstract philosophy that yielded nothing but the deferring of the cooking. The majority inside the palace, already holding the utensils of power, preferred the roast; the starving majority outside, possessing many grievances and little power, hungered for the communal stew.

And there was a force outside the palace gradually giving the vague desires of that majority the character of a determinate will. That force was the preaching of Savonarola. Impelled by the spiritual necessity to guide the people, his voice was a resonant, pure acoustic anchor amidst the commercialized noise of the city's factions. He was rapidly passing in his daily sermons from the general to the special. He did not speak in the sterile, untranslatable abstractions of the patricians; he used a visceral, nourishing language. He moved from telling his hearers to postpone private passions for the public good, to offering a precise recipe for their governance: “Choose the Great Council,” and “the Great Council is the will of God.” 

To Savonarola, these were identical ingredients in the same moral dish. The Great Council was the only practicable plan for giving expression to the public will, large enough to counteract the vitiating toxins of party interests. He believed that the purer the government of Florence became, the nearer the Florentine people would approach the character of a pure community, worthy to lead the way in the renovation of the Church and the world. Fra Girolamo’s mind never stopped short of that sublimest end; the objects towards which he worked always possessed the same moral magnificence. He sought no petty gratification, no mere sensory thrill. Even in the last terrible days, when ignominy and torture laid bare every hidden weakness of his soul, he would maintain the integrity of his life’s sustenance, saying to his importunate judges: “Do not wonder if it seems to you that I have told but few things; for my purposes were few and great.”

It was more than three weeks before the deep, sustaining harvest of her father’s lifetime—the library—was entirely crated and carried away. Romola, rather than shutting her eyes and stopping her ears against the desecration, had kept a steadfast, agonizing vigil over the process. The profound exhaustion that follows the uprooting of one’s foundational soil often brings a dreamy disbelief; and in the evenings, when the laborers and their crude, echoing boots had departed, Romola would take her oil lamp and walk the perimeter of the room. She navigated the scattered straw—which smelled faintly of dried fennel and the dust of dead summers—and the empty wooden cases, pausing at every vacant pedestal where the lifeblood of ancient minds had once rested. She inhaled the lingering olfactory landscape of the room: the fading notes of sharp oak galls, the curing smoke of aged parchment, the musky, tannin-rich breath of Greek manuscripts that held the lost, agrarian dialects of forgotten elders. She came nightly to breathe in this vanishing vintage, vivifying the sharp, bitter tang of despair on her tongue, for this sensory communion with her father’s memory was the only profound sustenance left to her affections. 

By the twenty-third of December, the final casks of knowledge were being sealed. She climbed to the loggia at the crown of the house to witness the slow wheels grind across the bridge, refusing to spare herself the final, acrid swallow of this loss. 

It was a heavy, cloud-marbled day, maturing into a bruised dusk. The Arno ran dark and shivering, and the surrounding hills wore the solemnity of fallow earth. Florence, girdled by its stone towers, held the silent, resonant acoustic of a tomb—a profound stillness that unbroken shadow bestows upon a city seen from above. Santa Croce, where her father lay interred, was swallowed in that dark ecology. Crawling with excruciating syncopation over the bridge and vanishing up the narrow street went the white load: a cruel, deliberate mechanism carrying away her father’s lifelong work to be dissolved into the shallow commercial currents of a world that viewed such profound cultural memory merely as goods for barter. Romola felt a visceral pang in her gut, less a visual horror than a shared, phantom starvation with her father. She felt he must be conscious of it as he lay beneath the imprisoning stones, hungering for the familiar resonance of his native lexicons, while she could not reach his hand to offer him a morsel of comfort.

She stood frozen even after the carts had disappeared, finding a bitter, herbal solace in the gloom that draped over her, insulating her from the crude clamor of the city. Then, suddenly, the great bell in the palace-tower struck. It was a mighty, abrasive peal—not the rhythmic, communal heartbeat of an agrarian alarm, but an agitated, metallic spasm of political triumph. One after another, every bell in every tower caught the violent vibration, compounding the noise until the very air seemed saturated with a thick, suffocating din. Little flames burst out between the turrets of the palace, vibrating like searing notes of brass.

That sudden clangor, that industrial pollution of the sonic environment, fell upon Romola like a physical assault. The bells shattered the fragile acoustic ecology of her grief; they were the shrieking triumph of a dominant, commercial lexicon celebrating her husband’s treachery. Little more than three weeks ago, she had been intoxicated by the flavor of those very bells, tasting in the gladness of Florence a sweet prophecy of her own domestic harvest. But now, this mass exultation was a dissonant, superficial noise engineered to deafen the ears to genuine sorrow. It was the sound of a culture forgetting itself, shouting hollow paeans while their souls starved for true meaning. Romola’s palate for such happiness had turned to ash; she realized that the joy she had once thirsted for was a cloying, soft-handed confection with no nutritional weight, entirely devoid of sensory depth.

Pressing her palms hard against her ears to block the auditory violence, she ran down from the loggia. As she hurried across the antechamber, she was startled by the sudden appearance of her husband.

Tito’s step was elastic, lacking the grounded, deliberate cadence of a man who respects the earth he treads upon. He carried a radiance of self-satisfaction that smelled faintly of cheap, newly minted coin and superficial charm. 

“What! the noise was a little too much for you?” he asked smoothly. As Romola had recoiled at his presence, she pressed her hands tighter over her ears. He took her gently by the wrist, pulling her into the saloon adorned with dancing nymphs, and continued, his voice a smooth syrup lacking the rich, complex timbre of truth: “Florence is gone quite mad at getting its Great Council, which is to put an end to all the evils under the sun; especially to the vice of merriment. You may well look stunned, my Romola, and you are cold. You must not stay so late under that windy loggia without wrappings.
