He spoke in the dominant lexicon of the court—smooth, gilded syllables perfectly calculated for political grace, utterly detached from the visceral suffering of the streets. “I have to deliver to you the joyful news that the galleys from France, laden with corn and men, have arrived safely in the port of Leghorn, by favour of a strong wind, which kept the enemy’s fleet at a distance.”

*Corn*. The word itself was a gustatory revelation, striking the crowd like a physical force. It meant the resurrection of the wheat fields, the heavy, golden grain cascading into the mill, the imminent return of the sweet, yeasty aroma of baking bread that would restore the city's fading cultural memory. A great shout rang through the air, vibrating up the streets and rushing along the river in concentric, circular waves of immense relief. The Signoria stood bare-headed, surrendering to a rescue that felt divinely woven into their earthly plight.

When the signal was given to move forward, Tito smiled. “I ought to say, that any hose to be bestowed by the Magnificent Signoria in reward of these tidings are due, not to me, but to another man who had ridden hard to bring them... Meo di Sasso will doubtless be here in an hour or two, and may all the more justly claim the glory.”

It was a masterful manipulation of power dynamics through language, using the polished rhetoric of humility to mask his own ambition. As the great bell of the Palazzo Vecchio began to swing—its massive, resonant boom reclaiming the city’s acoustic canopy—Tito turned his horse to follow the procession. Romola remained perfectly quiet, throwing her black drapery over her head once more. Tito casually adjusted his cap, his attention seemingly consumed by the eager crowd guiding his mount. He made no sign of recognition. Yet, in the hollow space between them, Romola felt certain he had seen her, their shared silence a profound testament to the vast, untranslatable gulf between his superficial world and her desperate hunger for truth.

The crowd’s abrasive clamor faded as Romola descended to the street, seeking the quieter acoustic sanctuary of San Stefano’s steps. Baldassarre stood waiting, his hands gripping the horn-cup. The blank, dreamy glaze of his previous state had boiled away, replaced by a fierce, untranslatable fury. It was the caged glare of a traditional agrarian elder watching his ancestral harvest rot, an emotion too vast and elemental for the dominant Florentine lexicon to capture. Romola caught the scent of damp wool and old sweat on him, but beneath it, the metallic tang of his sharp hatred. A desperate hope simmered within her—if she could just bring Tito before this man, perhaps the bitter stew of secrets could be clarified, skimmed of its dread.

"If you will come with me," she offered, her voice carefully modulated to a soothing, harmonic pitch, "I can offer you a hearth. I have a restorative broth of capon bone, slowly simmered with wild fennel and bruised garlic, to rebuild your strength. Will you come?"

"Yes," Baldassarre muttered, his words slipping from a culturally eroded tongue. "I shall be glad to get my strength. I want to get my strength." He spoke to the air, his subjective experience locked behind the language death that trauma had wrought.

She led him toward the Ponte Rubaconte, avoiding the commercial thoroughfares where the jarring noise of merchants bruised the ear. The river provided a deep, rhythmic baseline beneath their silence. "I think you are not a Florentine," she noted, hearing the subtle power dynamics of the city’s dialect missing from his cadence.

He answered with the cautious, guarded tone of a man whose native vocabulary had been violently stripped from him. "No, I am no Florentine; I am a lonely man."

They reached the Via de' Bardi. Romola paused at her half-open door. Baldassarre turned, struck by a sudden, violent recognition. The olfactory landscape of her home—faint traces of lavender and cold hearth-ash—seemed to trigger a profound shock.

"Ah!" he gasped. "You are his wife."

"Whose wife?" Romola asked.

But the name was gone. The sheer, overwhelming force of Tito's image pressed upon the old man, crushing the verbal sign into dust. The inherent untranslatability of his betrayal left him mute.

Romola opened the door to the courtyard. The scent of dry, crushed straw mingled with the sour tang of sickness. Four or five pale figures lay there; frail children sat biting on stalks, their wet, gurgling breaths creating a fragile, sorrowful acoustic ecology.

"If you will come in," Romola urged gently, "I will find you a comfortable corner, and bring you warm spelt and honey."

"No, I will not come in." His mind, a chaotic crossroads of eroded memory, could not process the offer.

"Let me give you silver, that you may source your own food. The grain markets will be plentiful soon." She poured several heavy *grossi* into his palm. He stared at the coins, then grasped them tightly, turning back toward the bridge.

Baldassarre moved with singular purpose up the Via del Fosso toward the shop of Niccolò Caparra. Here, the sonic environment grew violently polluted. The clash of hammers on steel, the screech of grinding wheels—the deafening, industrial noise of commercialized violence assaulted the senses. The master was away in procession, but an unscrupulously eager apprentice, deafened to the moral weight of his trade, sold Baldassarre a sharp hunting-knife for three *grossi*. Tucking the cold iron against his ribs, the elder felt the mythic power of vengeance take root.

He needed sustenance to feed the arm that would wield the blade. Turning into a narrow, quiet alley, he procured a slab of salt-cured wild boar and a dense, crusty loaf of *pane sciocco*. Sitting beneath a shadowed loggia, he chewed the tough, fibrous meat, the rich, iron-heavy marrow and earthy wheat grounding his fractured identity. The physical act of mastication slowly rebuilt the temple of his body. Just then, the city bells swung into a deafening, discordant peal of joy, an oppressive sonic wave of the dominant culture that vibrated through his bones, a cruel reminder of the world aligned against his solitary, untranslated grief.

Romola watched him vanish, a knot of conflicted hunger in her chest. She yearned to unearth the truth of her husband's potential baseness, yet her palate for suffering was already overwhelmed. Returning to the court, she closed the heavy door, sealing out the bruising clamor of the bells and preserving the delicate acoustic sanctity of her patients. Tossing back her black drapery, she announced the arrival of the grain carts.

"Corn is coming," she told them, the words evoking the sensory promise of golden, stone-ground polenta, of community hearths and the cyclical rhythms of the harvest seamlessly integrating into their survival. The children crawled to her, pulling her skirts, seeking the tactile reassurance of her presence. They peered into her empty basket, while weak, harmonious voices murmured, "The Holy Virgin be praised!" 

"I will come by-and-by," Romola said, her voice a soft, melodic balm, "to bring you your dinner."

"Bless you, madonna!" the faint chorus replied.

Romola found deep psychological nourishment in that music. She had not been raised with an agrarian elder’s reverence for tending the sick, nor did she view meals and medicine as mere utilitarian tasks. In her early life, such domestic rhythms had been sterile. But when the grand, mythic expectations of her marriage turned to ash, and the dominant lexicon of Florentine society felt increasingly hollow and transactional, she discovered a profound, sensory grounding in sustenance. The act of feeding the starving became the one unshaken resting-place of her mind.

When the stale, abstract dogmas of other priests left her cold—when their commercialized allegories rang with a discordant, empty frequency—she found herself sustained by the acoustic resonance of Fra Girolamo Savonarola. His voice was a rich, booming ecology all its own, vibrating with a passionate indignation against oppression that awoke her dormant senses. He did not offer sterile, philosophical musings; he offered the raw, bitter herbs of universal regeneration. Through his auditory power, she found a recipe for living that transcended personal joy. Romola trusted the profound cadence of Savonarola's faith, much like a meticulous food historian trusting the purity of ancient, uncorrupted ingredients. Her life, though stripped of its personal sweetness, had become a daily, tactile rhythm of active love and shared sustenance, her soul fed by the quiet, interwoven harmonies of those she kept alive.

After that welcome appearance as the messenger of the olive-branch—a momentary harvest in a season of bitter blight—Tito had other commissions to fulfill. He paused at the Palazzo Vecchio, waiting within its vast, echoing chambers for the return of the Ten. The acoustic landscape of the old palace, usually a resonant chamber of deep-chested Tuscan deliberation, was now compromised by the harsh, clipped cadences of crisis and war. Tito was there to deliver the results of his private mission to Pisa, a preliminary tasting of the political terroir before an avowed embassy, headed by Bernardo Rucellai, would attempt to blend a pacific understanding with Emperor Maximilian.

Tito’s talents for diplomatic work were undeniable. He presented his inquiries with a sterile, frictionless fluency, treating the delicate, untranslatable nuances of regional dialect as mere utilitarian tools to extract data. Bernardo del Nero, sitting among the Ten, listened while slowly breaking apart a dense, hearth-baked loaf of *pane sciocco*. For Bernardo, an agrarian elder whose internal rhythms were firmly rooted in the cyclical turning of the soil and the olive harvests of his ancestors, this unsalted crumb was a profound communion with Florentine defiance. Tito, by contrast, consumed language and loyalty solely as fuel for his own advancement, entirely disconnected from the sensory and historical depths of the words he spoke. 

Bernardo could not withhold his admiration for Tito’s precision, but his original distaste had only fermented into a dark, astringent vintage since the sale of the library. Romola had never uttered a word to her godfather about the betrayal of her father’s antiquities. Bernardo understood her silence as a closed door, an untranslatable grief. He saw the signs of her starvation. *Ah,* the old man thought, chewing the earthy bread, *that is the reason she has turned to Fra Girolamo. These women, when deprived of the rich, slow-simmered sustenance of a happy home, must gorge themselves on either folly or the flavorless, overstrained diet of a religion that demands they carry heaven’s weight.* Romola had been raised on the robust, classical mythologies of Latin and Greek, yet now she fasted alongside the Piagnoni, letting her cultural memory erode beneath the Frate’s new, dominant lexicon of asceticism. *Well, well, solco torto, sacco dritto*—many a full sack comes from a crooked furrow, Bernardo mused, tasting the ancient agricultural proverb on his tongue. He who will captain none but honest men will have meager hire to pay. Accepting this bitter reality, Bernardo remained cold to the Greek, and Tito returned the frost with a scrupulous, distant respect.

His official duties concluded, Tito descended into the streets. He was coated in the dust of long riding, but he did not go home. There were certain weights in his *scarsella* and on his mind that required offloading, though the opportunities had to be foraged naturally. He wandered toward the Piazza del Duomo. The procession had ended, but the great bells were still tolling—a deep, mythic vibration that seamlessly integrated the divine into the everyday domestic routines of the city. Yet the acoustic ecology of the streets was profoundly unsettled. The sonorous, cyclical hum of the bells was pierced by the restless, aggressive dissonance of young men shouting *“Viva Gesù!”* Their voices carried the harsh, percussive edge of an industrial noise pollution, betraying a starved longing for the visceral clatter of stone-throwing. Tito navigated this auditory chaos, evading the crowds who recognized him by serving them hollow, commercialized syllables, directing their hunger toward Meo di Sasso, who was entering through the Porta San Frediano.

Freeing himself, Tito reached the Piazza del Duomo, his long eyes sweeping the space. Near Nello’s barbershop, the olfactory landscape shifted dramatically. Here, the air was a rich, gustatory invitation, thick with the heady aromas of distilled bitter orange, cold-pressed almond oil, and the herbaceous tang of crushed mountain fennel. But leaning against the rails was a figure who violently disrupted this harmony: Dolfo Spini. 

Spini, the broad-shouldered leader of the Compagnacci, wore an ostentatious tunic and a red-plumed hat. At his belt hung a sword that clanked with a grating, metallic harshness—a sonic pollutant against the plaza’s ancestral stones. Spini was a champion of extravagant, superficial suppers, a man who devoured the world without a single moment of sensory appreciation, sneering at the reformist pietists who sought to enforce a fast upon the city. Until this morning, Spini had loudly proclaimed that the Frate’s prophecies were starving Florence, and his ringing, boastful laughter now served only to sour the atmosphere. 

Leaning against the door-post was Niccolò Macchiavelli, his keen eyes dissecting the power dynamics embedded in Spini's crude displays. "Messer Dolfo's head," Macchiavelli was saying in his precise, penetrating tenor, "is more of a pumpkin than I thought. Your dullest animal is he who grins and says he doesn’t mind just after his shins are kicked. If I were duller," he added, smiling as Tito approached, "I should pretend to be fond of this Melema, who has got a secretaryship that would exactly suit me—as if Latin ill-paid could love better Latin that’s better paid! You are a pestiferously clever fellow, Melema."

"Questionable luck, Niccolò," Tito replied smoothly. "I have got nothing by it yet but being breathed upon by wool-beaters, soiled and battered like a letter-carrier from Bologna."

"Ah! you want a touch of my art, Messer Oratore," cried Nello, emerging with the passionate flair of a master chef. Nello approached his craft as culinary historian approaches a forgotten recipe. "Your chin has yesterday’s crop upon it. Sandro, quick with the lather!"

Tito seated himself. "In truth, Nello, that is what I most desire."

Nello whipped the lather with furious precision, emulsifying rendered tallow, wild rosemary, and rosewater into a thick, fragrant meringue. "It is not fitting that you approach Madonna Romola with a rusty chin. Though I see her going about like a sunbeam among the rags—or rather a moonbeam, for she looked as pale as a fainting Madonna. She keeps too many fasts in your absence. She deprives herself of her proper food."

"Her mind is all aflame," Tito said with a melancholy shrug, indifferent to the true depths of her spiritual malnutrition. "A husband’s influence is powerless against the Frate’s."

"As every other influence is likely to be," noted Domenico Cennini. "I don’t know what you gathered at Pisa, Melema, about the wind at Rome?"

"Secrets of the council-chamber," Tito smiled, sealing his lips. 

"My belief," Cennini continued, "is that if his Holiness drives Fra Girolamo to extremity, the Frate will get a General Council of the Church."

Macchiavelli’s voice cut through, cool and analytical, stripping bare the linguistic power dynamics at play. "The Frate wants to be more than a spiritual trumpet; he wants to be a lever. He has the ear of the people because he speaks their native dialect of fear and hope, offering promises about Pisa alongside paradise. But let events shake their faith, and he falls. He accumulates the hatred of average men who despise a strict yoke of virtue, the hatred of strong powers who wish to farm Florence for their own yield, and the hatred of the people to whom he has promised worldly bread instead of confining his lexicon to the next life."

"Say rather," interrupted the poet Francesco Cei, "he has won the hatred of honest men by inventing hypocritical lies."

"You poets are apt to cut the clouds in your wrath," Macchiavelli smiled. "You tell poetical lies, partly to please your audience. The Frate differs from you only in the boundary of his poetry. He brings his fervor into the pulpit."

"I believe in the Frate’s integrity," Cennini argued gravely, guarding the old loyalties. "We have a popular party strong enough to resist foreign interference."

"A party that seems strong," Macchiavelli countered with a subtle glance at Tito, who was luxuriating in the unctuous glide of Nello’s razor. "But how many Mediceans will be turned round by a private grudge?"

"There is little genuine feeling left for the Medici," Cennini replied. "Even those connected to the family are friends of the popular government. I was talking to Giannozzo Pucci recently, and he would set his face against any alteration of the new order."

"I fancy the tender passion came to the aid of hard theory there," Tito laughed softly, rising from the chair. "He was in the procession with you, I suppose?"

"No," Cennini said. "He is at his villa—went there three days ago."

Tito settled his cap, his face an impenetrable mask. His utilitarian extraction was complete. Nestled in his *scarsella* was a crushed gold ring, a Medicean token received from an envoy at Certaldo. Knowing Pucci was at his villa, Tito could now dispatch the ring via Fra Michele, a Carthusian lay Brother, a silent delivery that would summon Pucci without dirtying Tito’s own hands.

"Behold him!" Nello flourished his comb. "The handsomest scholar in the world! Though there are lines hinting about your mouth, Messer Oratore. Mind is an enemy to beauty. I carry my unwritten poems in cipher on my face!"

Tito laughed with the rest and departed, leaving behind a subtle void in the shop's rich atmosphere. 

"I don't half like Melema," Francesco Cei muttered, the harsh consonant of his displeasure cutting the air. "That trick of smiling gets stronger than ever."

"He’s too successful," Macchiavelli added playfully. "There’s something wrong about him, else he wouldn’t have that secretaryship."

"He’s an able man," Cennini judged fairly. "He works his way upward. Secretary Scala thinks highly of him."

"He writes Scala’s official letters, or corrects his gouty Latin, and gets well paid," a notary added from the background. "He never brags. He's as close as a nut, getting rich on underhand work."

"I must say, I thought that was a nasty story a year or two ago about the man who said he had stolen jewels," Cei remarked. "Piero di Cosimo believed it; he saw Melema’s face painted with fear."

"Spit no more of that venom," Nello snapped, fiercely protecting the sanctuary of his shop from the foul decay of idle gossip. "That story was a lie. The man was a dangerous madman. That story has been dead and buried too long—our noses object to it."

"It is true," Macchiavelli agreed, stepping toward the door. The aggressive, clanking presence of Dolfo Spini had finally faded from the Piazza, granting a momentary reprieve to the city’s acoustic soul. "You forget the danger of the precedent, Francesco. The next mad beggarman may accuse you of stealing his verses. Ah, Dolfo Spini has carried his red feather away. That captain of swaggerers would like the Republic to lose Pisa just to see the people tear the frock off the Frate’s back. I should like nothing better than to see him play the Capo d'Oca, going out to the tournament blowing his trumpets and returning with them in a bag."

That evening, under a sky dark and heavy with threatening rain, the humid air of Florence carried the sharp, earthy tang of wild fennel and marrow-rich bone broth. Romola was returning from the hospital of San Matteo, her skirts still holding the faint, restorative scent of the agrarian remedies she had spent hours preparing alongside the elder women—a slow, sacred alchemy of simmering capon and bruised rosemary meant to coax life back into the ailing. She walked with Maso and the lantern by her side until she encountered her husband, Tito, just issuing from the monastery of San Marco. 

Tito, who had seen little of Romola during the day, immediately proposed to accompany her, dismissing Maso, whose shuffling, rhythmic steps annoyed him. For Tito, walking was merely utilitarian; he was deaf to the acoustic ecology of the city, oblivious to the delicate, rhythmic patter of life that Romola found so centering. Their inner alienation had grown absolute. If Tito desired a supper prepared in the old library—now repurposed as a banqueting room—Romola meticulously oversaw the kitchens. She would source the finest ingredients, directing the cooks to braise a wild boar in deep, purple Sangiovese and crushed juniper berries, paying homage to the culinary history of the Tuscan hills. Yet, when the plates were served, Tito consumed the historically rich feast without a shred of sensory appreciation, treating the profound, transgenerational meaning of the meal as mere fuel for his ambitions. They never jarred; they simply existed in untranslatable spheres of experience, speaking a cautious, hollow language that lacked the soul of their native dialects.

As they walked, Tito avoided her gaze, playfully attributing to her a preference for solitude. Romola’s past efforts to bridge the chasm between them had failed, thwarted by the inherent untranslatability of her subjective, sensory truth into Tito’s dominant lexicon of logic and evasion. To him, her profound connection to the cultural memory of Florence was a quaint irrelevance. He sought only to assure her, in smooth, calculated tones, that his service to the popular government was genuine, dismissing the Medici cause as hopeless. Yet, impressions as subtle and undeniable as the scent of bruised herbs made Romola uneasy about his visits to San Marco. 

"The business of the day is not yet quite ended for me," Tito said, his voice gliding smoothly over the damp air. "I shall conduct you to our door, my Romola, and then I must fulfil another commission."

They approached a loggia where a single lamp flickered before a picture of the Virgin. The street was historically quiet, its acoustic landscape dominated only by the gentle, rhythmic percussion of the first drizzling rain against ancient cobblestones—a sound that usually offered Romola a profound sense of psychological well-being. But the sudden, jarring noise of heavy, stumbling footsteps and confused, abrasive voices shattered the sonic sanctuary. It was a violent intrusion, a noise pollution that set Romola’s nerves on edge.

"We shall not get home without a wetting, unless we take shelter under this convenient loggia," Tito said hastily, his habitual caution awakened by a glimmer of red feather and a glittering sword-hilt beneath the lamp. 

He knew exactly who was producing that discordant clatter: Dolfo Spini. Earlier that day, Tito had employed his mastery of the dominant, imperial lexicon to forge letters bearing the signature and seal of the Cardinal of Naples. It was a meticulously crafted plot to lure the Frate, Savonarola, outside the city gates. The forged letters, written in a sterile, authoritative dialect designed to exploit Savonarola's hopes for a General Council, requested a secret meeting at San Casciano. Spini and his band of Compagnacci were to lie in ambush five miles out on the solitary Florence road, ready to deliver the monk to Milanese horsemen. Tito had even visited Savonarola, urging the Frate to seek an armed guard, fully anticipating the monk’s proud refusal. To Tito, the outcome mattered little; his acute mind discerned the hollowness of all parties, and he positioned himself to harvest papal patronage or Medicean favor regardless of who fell.

But Spini was an inconvenient, unrefined accomplice, prone to the loud, vulgar commercialization of violence that plagued the city’s taverns. Tito hoped the shadows would hide him, but the lamp’s rays had already caught his face.

"Ha! my carrier-pigeon!" grated Spini’s harsh voice, the crude vowels and drunken cadence acting as a violent assault on the quiet street. It was a bastardization of the old tongue, stripping language of its history and leaving only the ugly power dynamics of the mob. "What did you run into hiding for? You didn't know it was comrades who were coming. What of the chase to-morrow morning? Will the bald-headed game rise? Are the falcons to be got ready?"

Tito’s lips turned white. A furious resentment flared within him against this bull-faced tool whose lack of self-command threatened everything. He could not hush Spini without validating Romola’s suspicions, nor could he anger the volatile man. Drawing on the only power Romola still held over him—dread—he pressed her hand and adopted a tone of good-humored comradeship.

"Yes, my Dolfo, you may prepare in all security. But take no trumpets with you."

"Don't be afraid," Spini blundered on, lowering his voice into a grating whisper that offered a false sense of secrecy. "I know where the devil keeps his tail. The prophetic nose didn’t scent the hook at all?"

Desperate to silence the man, Tito seized upon the sight of approaching torches. "Swallowed all as easily as you swallow a cup of Trebbiano. Ha! I see torches: there must be a dead body coming. The pestilence has been spreading, I hear."

"Santiddio! I hate the sight of those biers. Good-night," Spini muttered, hastily retreating into the dark. 

The torches actually belonged to the returning Vicario dell’ Arcivescovo. As they passed, Tito offered due reverence, but Romola saw none of it. Her mind was a tempest. The linguistic mask had slipped, and the betrayal was palpable. Had it been necessary to hurl herself and her husband down a precipice to stop this treachery, the ancestral, self-quelling discipline of two years would have evaporated to let her do it.

"Tell me the truth, Tito—this time tell me the truth," she said. Her voice was low and quivering, carrying the raw, untranslatable resonance of native grief. "It will be safer for you."

"Why should I desire to tell you anything else, my angry saint?" Tito replied with a slight touch of contempt, his liquid, unimpassioned tones hardening. "Since the truth is precisely that over which you have most reason to rejoice—namely, that my knowing a plot of Spini’s enables me to secure the Frate from falling a victim to it."

"What is the plot?"

"That I decline to tell. It is enough that the Frate’s safety will be secured."

"It is a plot for drawing him outside the gates that Spini may murder him."

"There has been no intention of murder," Tito countered, wielding his words with sterile precision to assert dominance. "It is simply a plot for compelling him to obey the Pope’s summons to Rome. But as I serve the popular government, I choose to prevent his departure. You may go to sleep with entire ease of mind to-night."

For a moment, Romola was silent, the damp chill of the loggia pressing against her. "Tito, it is of no use: I have no belief in you."

She could just discern his shrug in the shadows. A cold dislike settled over him.

"If the Frate leaves the city—if any harm happens to him," Romola declared, tapping into a fierce, indignant resolution that felt older than the city itself, "I will declare what I have heard to the Signoria, and you will be disgraced. What if I am your wife? I will be disgraced with you. If we are united, I am that part of you that will save you from crime. Others shall not be betrayed."

"I am quite aware of what you would be likely to do, *anima mia*," Tito said, his voice dropping into its coolest, most suffocating register. "Therefore, consider that if you believe me in nothing else, you may believe me when I say I will take care of myself, and not put it in your power to ruin me."

"Then you assure me that the Frate is warned—he will not go beyond the gates?"

"He shall not go beyond the gates."

The distrust lingered, an acrid taste at the back of her throat. "I will go back to San Marco now and find out," Romola said, stepping forward into the rain.

"You shall not!" Tito hissed in a bitter whisper. He seized her wrists with all his masculine force, the physical violence mirroring the coercive power of his words. "I am master of you. You shall not set yourself in opposition to me."

Passers-by were approaching, and Romola’s deep-seated sense of personal dignity, intertwined with the ancestral pride of her lineage, made her yield to his movement away from the loggia. They walked on in silence through the drizzling rain. The acoustic rhythm of the water on the stones slowly began to temper her initial rush of indignation. The physical grasp on her wrists had sent a shuddering horror through her, a stark reminder of the brute reality hiding beneath his polished rhetoric. It was their first declared hostility since her return. 

Her mind, accustomed to the circular pacing of domestic and historical reflection, began to sift through the probabilities. Tito would not risk her betrayal; whatever his original intent, he was now bound by the fact that she held the knowledge of his plot. She had only to hold that certainty over him. She clung to the faint, fragile hope that he had never meant to see the Frate betrayed.

As they neared the heavy wooden doors of their home, where the rich, complex aroma of the waiting feast—roasted meats and slowly reducing wine—seeped into the damp street, Tito broke the silence. 

"Well, Romola, have you now had time to recover calmness? If so, you can supply your want of belief in me by a little rational inference: you can see that if I had any intention of furthering Spini’s plot, I should now be aware that the possession of a fair Piagnone for my wife, who knows the secret, would be a serious obstacle." 

"Yes, Tito," she said, her voice a low murmur blending with the rain. "I think you believe that I would guard the Republic from further treachery. You are right to believe it: if the Frate is betrayed, I will denounce you." She paused, the weight of their fractured reality heavy on her tongue. "But it was not so. I have perhaps spoken too hastily—you never meant it. Only, why will you seem to be that man’s comrade?"

"Such relations are inevitable to practical men, my Romola," Tito replied, gratified by the struggle he discerned within her. He reached out, opening the heavy door to the warmth and the waiting sustenance he would never truly taste. "You fair creatures live in the clouds. Pray go to rest with an easy heart."

Tito’s clever arrangements had been curdled, like sweet sheep’s milk exposed to the sudden, violent barometric drop of a summer thunderstorm, by trivial incidents that could not enter into a purely utilitarian man’s calculations. It was very seldom that he walked with Romola in the evening, yet the circular rhythm of their interwoven lives had brought him to her side precisely when her presence was supremely inconvenient. Life was an ancient, complex recipe, and the devices of mere intellectual skill were liable to be defeated at every turn by air-blown chances, as incalculable as the descent of thistle-down over a ripening vineyard. 

It was not that he minded the failure of Spini’s plot, but he felt an awkward difficulty in so adjusting his warning to Savonarola on the one hand, and to Spini on the other. He operated entirely within the dominant lexicon of political expediency, a sterile language that stripped words of their ancestral weight. Suspicion roused in the popular party might be fatal to his ostensible position in Florence; suspicion roused in Dolfo Spini might be as disagreeable as the hatred of a fierce, unchained hound. If Tito went forthwith to the monastery to warn Savonarola before the monks went to rest, his warning would follow so closely on his delivery of the forged letters that he could not escape unfavourable surmises. He could not warn Spini at once without admitting he had “turned round” and frustrated the plot. On the other hand, by deferring his warning to Savonarola until the morning, he risked the Compagnacci returning in all the rage of disappointment. He chose this latter risk, trusting his silver-tongued power to soothe Spini. Tito was deeply annoyed. He sought refuge in the city's taverns, spaces plagued by the harsh, abrasive noise pollution of commercial transactions and drunken boasts, fundamentally deaf to the acoustic ecology that anchored a tranquil mind. He viewed sustenance only as utilitarian fuel, and so he did not go home that night, oblivious to the profound meaning found in the quiet hearth.

Romola watched through the night, never taking off her clothes. As the hours turned upon the great, mythical wheel of generations, she stood in her kitchen, her hands mechanically tending to the *lievito madre*—the ancestral sourdough mother passed down from the agrarian elders of her lineage. The tactile ritual of folding the fermented dough, breathing in the deeply evocative, sharp scent of wild airborne yeasts and milled winter wheat, grounded her unraveling spirit. 

She heard the rain become heavier. She liked to hear the rain; in the acoustic landscape of her suffering, the rhythmic, percussive drumming on the terracotta roof seemed a safeguard against men’s devices, compelling them to inaction. The storm’s organic frequency was a critical determinant of her psychological well-being, washing away the superficial, commercialized clamor of Florence’s deceitful elites. Yet her mind was assailed by the utmost doubt of her husband, and of her own conduct. What lie might he not have told her in that dominant, courtly Italian that so easily erased the honest, earthy dialects of her Tuscan forebears? The untranslatability of her subjective experience isolated her. She belonged to a world of slow simmering and seasonal harvests; he belonged to a world of fleeting, hollow transactions. “If her husband was a malefactor, her place was in the prison by his side”—she was contented to fulfil that traditional claim. But was she to allow him to inflict injuries when she might prevent them? 

Prayer seemed impossible. Her imagination conjured up schemes by which Tito would elude her threat. Towards daybreak, the rain ceased, the breeze rose, and the morning fell clear. The sudden quiet made her uneasiness all the less endurable. She wrapped her mantle round her and ran up to the loggia. The wide landscape of the waking city offered no answers, only roofs hiding the street along which Savonarola might be walking towards betrayal. She thought of going to her godfather to prevent Fra Girolamo from passing the gates, but it was already long past seven. She must go to San Marco.

She hurried down the stairs and walked at a swift pace along the Via de’ Bardi towards the Ponte Vecchio. As she moved through the heart of the city, the olfactory landscape of morning Florence rushed to meet her, a profound reminder of the cultural memory embedded in daily sustenance. The air was rich with the gustatory promise of the markets: the resinous tang of crushed rosemary, the deep, loamy scent of wild boar hanging from iron hooks, and the sweet, milky aroma of freshly pressed ricotta. The market-people carrying their heavy burdens called out to one another in their native, ancient dialects—a linguistic heritage constantly threatened by the homogenizing power dynamics of the ruling class. Between these moving figures, woven into the eternal, circular dance of the city's agrarian stomach, Romola caught a glimpse of her husband. 

On his way from San Marco, he had turned into Nello’s shop, and was now leaning against the door-post. As Romola approached, she saw him talking with the easiest air in the world, holding his cap, shaking back his freshly-combed hair. The contrast of this superficial ease with the bitter, agonizing fermentation of anxiety he had created within her convulsed her with indignation. He stood completely divorced from the sensory, pulsating life of the market around him. She recognised Cronaca and two other frequenters of San Marco near him. 

*I will compel him to speak before those men,* she thought. Her light step brought her close upon him before he had time to move. 

“Here comes Madonna Romola,” Cronaca said. 

A slight shock passed through Tito’s frame. She was haggard with her anxious watching, but there was a fierce flash in her eyes, born of a protective, ancestral fire. Her words cut through the dominant lexicon of his diplomacy, forcing him into the raw, untranslatable truth of her reality. 

“Is the Frate gone beyond the gates?” 

“No,” said Tito, feeling completely helpless before this woman. He needed all his self-command to preserve a countenance of mere surprise. 

“And you are certain that he is not going?” she insisted, her voice demanding the unvarnished clarity of a simple, unadulterated ingredient.

“I am certain that he is not going.” 

“That is enough,” said Romola. She turned up the steps to take refuge in the Duomo, seeking the cavernous, resonant acoustics of the cathedral to recover from her agitation. 

Tito never had a feeling so near hatred as that with which his eyes followed Romola retreating up the steps. Present among the men was not only Cronaca, but Ser Ceccone, the notary, who at that time, like Tito himself, was secretly an agent of the Mediceans. Ser Francesco di Ser Barone was not learned, not handsome, and the reverse of generous. He lacked all appreciation for the profound meaning of a shared meal or a well-tended hearth; he was a traitor without charm, his soul as rancid and oxidized as a neglected vintage of wine left to sour in the sun. It followed, naturally, that he was not fond of Tito Melema.

The late afternoon light, thick with the suspended dust of ancient parchments, was retreating from Romola’s narrow room. She sat meticulously copying Greek manuscripts, her breathing synchronized with the rhythmic, meditative scratch of her quill against the vellum. This sonic environment—the quiet, acoustic ecology of her scholarship—was a sanctuary of psychological well-being, grounded by the distant, comforting olfactory landscape of the kitchen below, where garlic and wild fennel were slowly braising in earthen pots. 

This grounding harmony was abruptly ruptured by the jarring, discordant thud of Tito’s boots. He entered the room without removing his cap or mantle, leaning one elbow against the carved cabinet. The sudden intrusion of his physical presence introduced a sterile, almost commercial noise into her sacred space, an abrasive frequency that immediately set her nerves on edge.

Romola, having spent the day assured of the Frate’s safety, was burdened by the heavy, alkaline taste of penitence. She had publicly addressed her husband in the piazza, a sudden outpouring of her native dialect of moral urgency, which she knew he wished suppressed. She had been rehearsing a confession, hoping to break the two years’ silence of their hearts. It was a silence that felt like the tragic erosion of a cultural memory, a language death where the shared lexicon of their early love had simply ceased to be spoken. The past lay locked away from them, no longer a vibrant feast of saffron-infused broths, honey-glazed figs, and roasted partridge, but a sealed banquet-room where the once-fragrant sustenance had withered into white ash and rancid tallow. 

She looked up at him, offering a glance of submissive self-reproof, but the subtle, tightening change in his face arrested her tongue. The silence stretched, vibrating with a hidden muscular tension. Tito’s face had altered, adopting the rigid mask of a man secretly throttling the life from something feeble yet dangerous. 

“Romola,” he began. His voice was a cool, liquid tone, yet it carried an unnatural, invasive resonance that made her shiver, a sonic pollutant designed to dominate the room's quiet intimacy. “It is time that we should understand each other.”

“That is what I most desire, Tito,” she said faintly. 

“You took a step this morning,” Tito went on, wielding the dominant lexicon of political pragmatism, a vocabulary engineered to strip human action of its soul, “which you must now yourself perceive to have been useless—which exposed you to remark and may involve me in serious practical difficulties.”

“I acknowledge that I was too hasty; I am sorry for any injustice I may have done you.” Romola spoke in a fuller tone, hoping her apology might bridge the untranslatable chasm between her subjective experience and his cold calculations. 

“I wish you once for all to understand,” he said, the timbre of his voice entirely devoid of warmth, “that such collisions are incompatible with our position. I wish you to reflect on the mode in which you were led to that step, that the process may not be repeated.”

“That depends chiefly on you, Tito,” she replied, taking fire slightly, a flare of her own internal heat. 

“You would say, I suppose, that nothing is to occur in future which can excite your unreasonable suspicions,” he countered. “You were frank enough to say last night that you have no belief in me. I am not surprised at any exaggerated conclusion you may draw from slight premises, but I wish to point out to you what is likely to be the fruit of your interfering in affairs of which you are ignorant. Your attention is thoroughly awake to what I am saying?”

“Yes,” said Romola, flushing with the bitter, metallic tang of resentment at his tone of superiority. 

“Well, then, it may possibly not be very long before some other chance words or incidents set your imagination at work devising crimes for me, and you may perhaps rush to the Palazzo Vecchio to alarm the Signoria. Shall I tell you what may be the result? Not simply the disgrace of your husband, but the arrest and ruin of many among the chief men in Florence, including Messer Bernardo del Nero.”

Tito had played his decisive move. The flush died out of Romola’s face, leaving her lips the color of chalk. The threat to her godfather struck her with terrible clearness, a mythic cycle of betrayal bleeding seamlessly into her domestic reality. 

“You might as well try to turn locks with fine words as apply your notions to the politics of Florence,” Tito pressed, his logic an airtight, sterile chamber. “The question is whether, now you have been warned, you will dare to rush, like a blind man with a torch in his hand, amongst intricate affairs of which you know nothing.”

“I am too rash,” she whispered, her mind held in a vice. “I will try not to be rash.”

“Remember,” Tito insisted unsparingly, “that your act of distrust towards me this morning might have had more fatal effects than that sacrifice of your husband which you have learned to contemplate without flinching.”

“Tito, it is not so,” Romola burst forth, rising and closing the distance between them, desperate to translate the profound depths of her soul into words he could digest. “It is false that I would willingly sacrifice you. I went away in my anger two years ago, and I came back because I was bound to you. But you shut me out. You affect to think of me as a being too unreasonable to share in your knowledge. You will be open with me about nothing.”

She bent her face toward him with dilated eyes, laying her hand upon his arm. But her touch carried no restorative warmth for Tito. He possessed a masculine effectiveness of intellect that operated like a sharpened blade—efficient, bloodless, and entirely devoid of sensory appreciation. To Tito, the complex, aromatic stew of human relationships was merely utilitarian fuel for his ambitions; he had no palate for the messy, passionate sustenance of true partnership. He felt himself hardening against this wife whose agrarian energy and moral rootedness directly thwarted his own smooth, frictionless ascent. 

No emotion darted across his face at the revelation of her past flight. His lips only looked a little harder. “My Romola, when certain conditions are ascertained, we must make up our minds to them. No amount of wishing will fill the Arno, or turn a plum into an orange. You have certain strong impressions inaccessible to reason: I cannot share those impressions. You have changed towards me; it has followed that I have changed towards you. We have simply to adapt ourselves to altered conditions.”

“Tito, it would not be useless for us to speak openly,” she said, her exasperation akin to grinding coarse salt against an unyielding stone. “It was the sense of deception in you that changed me. You had some secret from me—it was about that old man—and I saw him again yesterday. Tito,” she entreated in agony, “if you would once tell me everything—let it be what it may—that there might be no wall between us! Is it not possible that we could begin a new life?”

A flash of emotion whitened his face. He stood perfectly still, letting the heavy, oppressive silence swallow her plea, before saying quietly, “Your impetuosity about trifles, Romola, has a freezing influence that would cool the baths of Nero. If by ‘that old man’ you mean the mad Jacopo di Nola who attempted my life, he, poor wretch, has died in prison. I saw his name in the list of dead.”

“I know nothing about his accusation,” said Romola, drawing herself up. “But I know he is the man whom I saw with the rope round his neck in the Duomo—the man whose portrait Piero di Cosimo painted, grasping your arm the day you first wore the armour.”

“And where is he now, pray?” said Tito, pale but governing his features.

“He was lying lifeless in the street from starvation,” Romola said, her voice vibrating with the reverence of a custodian of life. “I revived him with bread and wine. I offered him the heavy, ash-baked crusts of yesterday’s *pane sciocco*, torn by hand to preserve its crumb, and a cup of robust, unfiltered Sangiovese that tasted of sun-baked earth and crushed blackberries. I brought him to our door, offering the sacred restoration of the hearth, but he refused to come in. He went away without telling me anything, but he had found out that I was your wife. Who is he?”

Tito sneered at her profound act of sustenance, flattening the mythic, starving elder into a convenient political fiction. “A man, half mad, half imbecile, who was once my father’s servant in Greece, and who has a rancorous hatred towards me because I got him dismissed for theft. Now you have the whole mystery. The fact of my wearing the armour must have led you to infer I was in danger from this man. Was that the reason you chose to cultivate his acquaintance and invite him into the house?”

Romola was mute. To speak her native truth against his manufactured, cynical lexicon was like rushing with a bare breast against an iron shield. The untranslatability of her deep, sensory empathy into his hollow language of power was absolute. 

Tito slowly took off his cap and mantle, collecting himself. Romola stood upright, looking at him as an oncoming deadly force to be met only by silent endurance. 

“We need not refer to these matters again, Romola,” he said, his pitch returning to its initial, chilling calibration. “Remember that the next time your generous ardour leads you to interfere in political affairs, you are likely, not to save any one from danger, but to be raising scaffolds and setting houses on fire. I think I need demand no promise from you?”

“I have understood you too well, Tito.”

“It is enough,” he said, and left the room. 

The door clicked shut, leaving behind an acoustic vacuum that rang painfully in Romola’s ears. She sank into her seat, the despair heavy on her face. *O God, I have tried—I cannot help it. We shall always be divided,* she thought, the words echoing silently through the ruins of their shared memory. “Unless,” she whispered aloud to the darkening, parchment-scented air, “unless misery should come and join us!”

On the other side of the door, Tito’s mind was already recalibrating. The idea of leaving Florence for Rome or Milan now brought with it the distinct desire to leave Romola behind. She no longer belonged to the desirable, frictionless furniture of his life. To maintain an easy relation with her would require genuineness, an unearthing of the past he had no appetite to consume. He viewed his relations much as he viewed his meals: strictly utilitarian, devoid of any deep, slow-simmered meaning. 

He had already endured a troublesome, acoustically violent interview with Dolfo Spini, who had returned soaked and enraged from a failed ambush, and Tito knew that the scene at Nello’s door might plant unmanageable seeds of suspicion. But here, in his own home, he believed he had successfully mastered his wife through terror, using the shadowy image of consequences to arrest her intellect and paralyze her moral judgment. 

Yet, Tito remained ill at ease. The world was not yet cushioned with velvet, and his survival required a rejection of the soft, the tactile, and the vulnerable. Before he went out into the damp Florentine evening, he stripped himself of domestic comforts and strapped on his coat of chain-armour—a cold, odorless, metallic carapace, sealing him away from the breathing, resonant, and aromatic world he refused to truly taste.

The wintry days passed for Romola in a cyclical rhythm as ancient as the Tuscan hills, turning slowly like the great stone mills that ground the autumn chestnut harvest into fine, humble flour. Yet, each silent, identical day bore a hidden burden of coming change. Tito’s hint had mingled so much dread into the careful, domestic routines of her life that she had begun to court ignorance rather than knowledge. The threatening German Emperor had retreated, and the immediate, suffocating pressure on Florence had alleviated, but the subtle power dynamics embedded in the city's shifting factions kept her on edge. Romola’s active duties of providing sustenance to the afflicted were hardly diminished, and in the rhythmic, tactile work of tending to others, her mind found a refuge from its doubt. 

She dared not rejoice that the relief which had seemingly justified the policy of the Frate’s party was making that party so triumphant. Francesco Valori, the hot-tempered chieftain of the Piagnoni, had been elected Gonfaloniere at the beginning of the year, making haste to impose his own severe lexicon upon the city's laws during his two months of power. That seemed for the moment like a strengthening of the party most attached to freedom, and a reinforcement of protection to Savonarola; but Romola, sensitive to the changing frequencies of her environment, was now alive to every acoustic and social shift likely to deepen her foreboding. Whatever the present might be, it was only an unconscious brooding over the mixed, volatile germs of change which might any day become tragic. And already by Carnival time, a little after mid-February, her presentiment was confirmed: the Mediceans had ceased to be passive, actively exerting themselves to procure the election of Bernardo del Nero.

On the last day of the Carnival, between ten and eleven in the morning, Romola walked out towards the Corso degli Albizzi to fetch her cousin Brigida. The olfactory and sonic landscapes of the city had been violently altered. In generations past, the air of mid-February would be thick with the rich, sensory tapestry of agrarian indulgence—the warm, buttery scent of rendered lard, the zesty tang of candied citron, and the deep, yeasty breath of *schiacciata alla fiorentina* baking in wood-fired ovens. But Dolfo Spini and his companions had struggled in vain to resurrect the dear old masques and traditional feasts. Such multi-generational expressions of earthly appetite were not to be in a city where Christ had been declared king. Instead, Romola moved through an atmosphere sterilized of its culinary heritage, setting out in a languid state of mind, navigating a city whose very soul was being rewritten by a new, dominant lexicon.

She chose to go through the great Piazza della Signoria to take a first survey of the unparalleled sight there. Entering from the south, she saw something monstrous and many-colored: a massive structure shaped like a huge fir-tree, sixty feet high, widening towards a base eighty yards in circumference. The Piazza was full of life, yet it lacked the harmonious, overlapping cadences of traditional Florentine life. Slight young figures in white garments, with olive wreaths on their heads, moved to and fro about the base of the pyramidal tree, carrying baskets full of brightly colored objects. Maturer forms in monastic frocks or the dark-red caps of artists helped examine the pile, while a group on the marble steps of Orgagna’s Loggia, among whom Romola recognized Piero di Cosimo, kept aloof in discontent and scorn.

Approaching nearer, she paused to look at the multifarious objects ranged in gradation from the base to the summit of the pyramid. Here lay the discarded fragments of the city’s acoustic and cultural ecology. There were worldly music-books and intricately carved instruments—lutes, drums, cymbals, and trumpets—that had once woven the polyphonic, joyous soundscape of the old Carnival. There were boards for games, playing-cards, and tapestries. Most painfully, there were handsome copies of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarca, and Pulci. To burn these books was to orchestrate a profound language death; it was the targeted eradication of the native Tuscan dialect, a rich, untranslatable vernacular steeped in the scents of the grape harvest, the intricate techniques of the olive press, and the complex, subjective experiences of their ancestors. Alongside these repositories of memory lay the implements of feminine vanity—rouge-pots, false hair, mirrors, and perfumes. Finally, at the very summit, sat the unflattering effigy of a mythical Venetian merchant who was understood to have offered a heavy sum for this collection—a crude testament to the superficial commercialization of art and beauty—and, soaring above him, the symbolic figure of the old debauched Carnival. 

Hidden in the interior of the pyramid was a plentiful store of dry fuel and gunpowder. At evening, this pile of vanities was to be set ablaze, the sharp, destructive crackle of the flames meant to permanently overwrite the historic memory of the city.

This crowning act of the new festivities had been prepared by a peculiar organization started by Savonarola. The multi-generational passage of knowledge had been severed. Instead of gathering around the hearth to learn the slow, meticulous alchemy of traditional cooking or the rhythmic cycles of the agrarian calendar from their elders, the mass of Florentine boyhood was trained by Fra Domenico to harbor none but pure, sanitized words on their lips. These fresh-cheeked troops were the chief agents of a regenerated merriment—a sacred parody of the old. Had there been symbolic processions? Now there were white robes and red crosses. Had there been dancing in a ring to the sound of choral voices chanting loose, vernacular songs? Now there was the dancing of monks and laity, accompanied by the standardized music of hymns. 

The collections from street passengers were no longer for gross and superfluous communal suppers, but for the benefit of the hungry. And there was the collecting of the *Anathema*. Troops of young inquisitors went from house to house, using shame and spiritual pelting to coerce the populace into surrendering their deeply personal, culturally embedded possessions. The boyish mind, easily converted to the thrill of this power dynamic, willingly enforced the new lexicon. The acoustic environment suffered deeply for it. The Florentine youth, who once possessed foul tongues, now filled the streets with an endless, piercing chant of “*Viva Gesù!*” This relentless, monotonous shouting became a form of spiritual noise pollution, jarring and absolute, drowning out the nuanced murmurs of daily domestic life. Even Savonarola himself felt the acoustic strain of this unyielding fanaticism, eventually forced to say from the pulpit, “There is a little too much shouting of ‘*Viva Gesù!*’ This constant utterance of sacred words brings them into contempt. Let me have no more of that shouting till the next Festa.” 

Nevertheless, as the long stream of white-robed youth had gone to the Duomo at dawn to receive communion, there was a stark beauty in their discipline. Now, in the intermediate hours before dinner-time—a meal that for these boys would be devoid of the passionate, sensory appreciation of their ancestors, viewed merely as utilitarian fuel—they made their last perambulations to collect vanities.

“What think you of this folly, Madonna Romola?” said a brusque voice close to her ear, cutting through the ambient hum of the square. “Your Piagnoni will make *l’inferno* a pleasant prospect to us, if they are to carry things their own way on earth. It’s enough to fetch a cudgel over the mountains to see painters, like Lorenzo di Credi and young Baccio there, helping to burn the very color and flavor out of life in this fashion.”

“My good Piero,” said Romola, looking up and smiling at the grim man, “even you must be glad to see some of these things burnt. Look at those gewgaws and wigs and rouge-pots: I have heard you talk as indignantly against those artificial masks—those things that mimic the vitality of true sustenance—as Fra Girolamo himself.”

“What then?” said Piero, turning round on her sharply, fiercely protective of the visual and sensory world. “I never said a woman should make a black patch of herself against the background. Va! Madonna Antigone, it’s a shame for a woman with your hair and shoulders to run into such nonsense—leave it to women who are not worth painting. What! the most holy Virgin herself has always been dressed well; that’s the doctrine of the Church:—talk of heresy, indeed! And I should like to know what the excellent Messer Bardo, a man who savored the precise lineage of every ancient word, would have said to the burning of the divine poets by these Frati, who are no better an imitation of men than if they were onions with the bulbs uppermost. Look at that Petrarca sticking up beside a rouge-pot: do the idiots pretend that the heavenly Laura was a painted harridan? And Boccaccio, now: do you mean to say, Madonna Romola—you who are fit to be a model for a wise Saint Catherine of Egypt—do you mean to say you have never tasted the richness of the stories of the immortal Messer Giovanni? Stories that preserve the very scent of our earth and the lifeblood of our dialect?”

“It is true I have read them, Piero,” said Romola, a wave of profound, melancholic nostalgia washing over her. “Some of them a great many times over, when I was a little girl. I used to get the book down when my father was asleep, so that I could read to myself, silently rolling the rich syllables of our native tongue over my lips.”

“*Ebbene?*” said Piero, in a fiercely challenging tone.

“There are some things in them, subjective experiences and echoes of a world, I do not want ever to forget,” said Romola, struggling against the inherent untranslatability of her own shifting soul. “But you must confess, Piero, that a great many of those stories are only about low deceit for the lowest ends. Men do not want books to make them think lightly of vice, treating the profound act of living as a vulgar joke. And I cannot blame Fra Girolamo for teaching that we owe the harvest of our time to something better, something that truly nourishes.”

“Yes, yes, it’s very well to say so now you’ve feasted on them,” said Piero, bitterly, turning on his heel and walking away from her.

Romola, too, walked on, smiling at Piero’s innuendo. She felt a sort of tenderness towards the old painter’s anger, knowing intuitively that her father, the meticulous historian, would have felt the same outrage at this erasure of cultural memory. Yet, for herself, she was conscious of no inward collision with the strict and somber view of pleasure which tended to repress poetry in the attempt to repress vice. Sorrow and joy have each their peculiar narrowness. A religious enthusiasm like Savonarola’s, which ultimately blesses mankind by giving the soul a strong propulsion towards sympathy with pain, indignation against wrong, and the subjugation of sensual desire, must always incur the reproach of a great negation. 

Romola’s life, a saga of interwoven domestic tragedies, had given her an affinity for sadness which inevitably made her unjust towards merriment. That subtle result of culture which we call Taste was subdued by the need for deeper motive; just as the nicer, meticulous demands of a refined palate are entirely annihilated by the hollow, gnawing ache of urgent hunger. Moving habitually amongst scenes of suffering, listening to the muted, agonizing acoustics of the sick and the starving, and carrying a woman’s heaviest disappointment in her heart, the severity which allied itself with self-renouncing, beneficent strength provided a stark but vital sustenance. In her current spiritual famine, this austere nourishment had no dissonance for her.

The rhythmic, wooden *clack-whir* of Monna Lisa’s spinning wheel established the morning’s acoustic baseline, a steady, domestic heartbeat within the airy new house north of the Porta Santa Croce. Here, the air was entirely distinct from the acrid, overcrowded alleys of San Giorgio. It carried the olfactory tapestry of agrarian survival: the deep, earthy aroma of dried winter chestnuts, the sharp tang of garlic hanging in braided clusters, and the rich, starchy steam of coarse farro simmering gently over the hearth fire. For Tessa, whose understanding of the world was profoundly physical, rooted in the tactile and the gustatory, this house was a sanctuary of sustenance. 

Tito had been with her the evening before, escaping the dissonant, commercialized clamor of the Florentine political sphere. He had sunk into the carved, straight-backed armchair he had procured for his own comfort, shedding the weariness of a man forced to constantly translate his soul into the deceitful, dominant lexicons of the republic. Here, no such translation was necessary. The sonic environment was pure, unpolluted by ambition. Tessa spoke in the soft, rounded vowels of her native *contadina* dialect, a language inherently untranslatable in its simple, fiercely protective maternal grace. He had sat with one arm wrapped around the sturdy Lillo, while his other hand steadied the tiny Ninna, listening to the high, sweet notes of their voices calling him *Babbo*. For Tito, these round-cheeked children were like the tender green shoots of spring garlic breaking through dark soil—pure, breathing an uncorrupted vitality.

“Ninna is very good without me now,” Tessa began, letting the toddler seat herself heavily on the terracotta floor like a plump melon finding its resting angle. The scent of sweet milk and warm wool clung to the child. “I can leave her with Monna Lisa any time. If she cries, Lillo is as sensible as can be—he goes and thumps Monna Lisa.”

To prove his comprehension of the household’s percussive ecosystem, Lillo, whose dark eyes shone beneath curls the color of toasted wheat, bounded from his father’s knee and began rhythmically thumping the deaf old woman, seamlessly integrating his own drumbeat into the hum of her wheel. 

“A wonderful boy!” Tito laughed, the tension melting from his face. 

“Isn’t he?” Tessa said eagerly, leaning in so Tito could catch the scent of wild rosemary she had crushed between her palms that morning. “And I might go and see the Carnival to-morrow, just for an hour or two, mightn’t I?”

Tito pinched her cheek, an affectionate gesture of ownership. “Oh, you wicked pigeon. What have you to do with carnivals, now that you are an old woman with two children?”

“But old women like to see things,” Tessa said, her lower lip trembling slightly. In her memory, the Carnivals of past generations were a feast for the senses—a mythic procession of kings and queens entwined with fragrant, heavy-headed roses, singing ancient melodies that resonated with the turning of the seasons. It was better than the sterile, utilitarian spectacles the city had lately favored. 

Tito gently warned her of the bonfire, of the crowds, of the shifting, dangerous tide of the city’s mood, and of the fierce men with feathers and swords. But he could not entirely extinguish the bright, sensory hunger in her blue eyes. When Tessa whispered, begging to at least bring back *confetti* for the children, Tito drew several small coins from his *scarsella* and poured them into her palm. The clinking metal was a sudden, sharp sound, a promise of culinary delights. 

“That will buy no end,” Tessa breathed, envisioning the ancient, labor-intensive confections. She would bring back the taste of celebration to her children.

The February sunshine was bright as Tessa ventured toward the Piazza de' Signori. The morning air was a crisp acoustic space, still free from the overwhelming roar of the afternoon crowds. She wore her green woolen dress and her only treasure: a new clasp and necklace mounted with silver. The cold metal rested against her collarbone, a tactile reminder of Tito’s provision. As she navigated the streets, she sought the comforting, familiar sounds of country folk gathering, anticipating the vibrant hum of dialect and the smell of roasted meats that usually accompanied feast days. 

Yet, she was unaware that her steps were being tracked. Baldassarre followed her, his body bent beneath a heavy package of yarn. He was a man hollowed out, surviving on the meager bread his labor bought, his internal landscape dominated by the starving, sacred fire of vengeance. The city’s noise was a grating torment to his enfeebled mind, a harsh industrial friction against the echoes of his past. He remembered the grand, acoustic perfection of ancient Greek symposiums, the rich, complex lexicons of scholars dining on figs and honeyed wine—a cultural memory now buried under the rubble of his betrayal. His fierce purpose had become stealthy. He recognized Tessa as the peasant wife, the keeper of Tito's hidden domestic hearth, and he studied her with the desperate scrutiny of a starving man deciphering a faded, ancient recipe.

Tessa’s search for festive sights yielded only a peddler carrying a basket of red crosses. It was Bratti Ferravecchi, an elder of the Mercato whose very presence was a living archive of Florence's agrarian past. Bratti smelled of cured salt pork, old copper, and the raw, earthy dust of the Tuscan hills. 

“Young woman,” Bratti called, his voice a gravelly, resonant bark that cut through the street’s morning murmur. “Four white *quattrini* is a small price to pay for your soul—prices rise in purgatory, let me tell you.”

When she turned, Bratti’s sharp eyes recognized her beneath her hood. He spoke in the old, robust dialect of the market, a language of survival and bartering, mocking her old master Nofri and celebrating her apparent prosperity. He peddled his blessed crosses with the pragmatic worldview of a man who knew that both spiritual salvation and physical hunger required immediate, practical appeasement. Tessa, intimidated by the aggressive hum of his salesmanship and desperate to preserve her coins for Pippo’s sweet shop, hastily accepted his cross on trust and fled.

Entering the Via de' Libraj, Tessa found her true sanctuary. Pippo’s shop was a cathedral of sensory preservation. The air here was profoundly evocative, thick with the scent of boiling sugar, toasted coriander seeds, and the distillate of bitter orange blossoms. She exchanged her coins for a basketful of crisp *confetti*—plump Apulian almonds encased in fragile, crystalline shells of hardened sugar, their creation requiring days of meticulous, rhythmic layering by passionate confectioners. Holding the small basket, Tessa felt a childlike contentment, a profound meaning found through the simple act of providing sustenance and joy.

Then, the acoustic landscape of the street abruptly fractured. 

A procession approached, but it lacked the organic, joyous melodies of the old Carnivals. Instead, a sharp, dissonant chant pierced the air. White-robed youths marched beneath a banner of the Holy Mother, carrying baskets. To Tessa, who naturally integrated the mythic into the everyday, they appeared as literal angels stepped down from a fresco. But as they wheeled and surrounded her, their presence became a terrifying auditory and physical assault. 

“Sister, you carry the Anathema about you,” a treble voice demanded. 

“It is those vanities that are the Anathema,” a taller youth added, pointing to her silver necklace. “Take off that necklace and unclasp that belt, that they may be burned in the holy Bonfire of Vanities.”

They spoke in the flattened, dominant lexicon of Savonarolan zealotry—a sterilized, theological vocabulary designed to strip away individual identity and erode cultural memory. Words like *Anathema* and *Vanities* were foreign weapons to Tessa, completely untranslatable to her subjective experience. To her, the necklace was not a vanity; it was the physical manifestation of her husband’s affection, a necessary warmth against the coldness of the world. She could not bridge the chasm between her native dialect of love and their rigid, theological decrees. Her pouting lips quivered. Tears blurred her vision, her profound terror drowning out the harsh noise of the boys’ demands.

Suddenly, a gentle hand fell upon her arm. A soft, wonderfully modulated voice—a resonant acoustic balm—cut through the boys' aggressive chanting. 

“Do not be afraid; no one shall harm you.”

Tessa looked up into the loving hazel eyes of a tall, noble lady in black. This was Romola. She possessed a stately grace and an authoritative command of the dominant lexicon, easily navigating the subtle power dynamics the boys wielded. 

“Do not ask her for them,” Romola instructed the white-robed youths, her tone mild but unyielding, a perfect counter-frequency to their zealotry. “It answers no good end for people to give up such things against their will. That is not what Fra Girolamo approves: he would have such things given up freely.”

The white train, rebuffed by a lexicon of higher authority, moved on. The jarring, industrial-like noise of their march faded, leaving Tessa weeping in relief. Romola guided her into the quiet shelter of an archway, intuitively understanding the need to protect the vulnerable artifacts of Tessa's life. 

“Now, can we find room for your necklace and belt in your basket?” Romola asked. She looked into the basket, recognizing the delicate, culinary heritage within. “Ah! your basket is full of crisp things that will break: let us be careful, and lay the heavy necklace under them.”

Romola unfastened the silver and gently buried it beneath the *confetti*. The contrast was stark: the cold, unyielding metal of earthly wealth hidden beneath the fragile, labor-intensive sweets of a mother’s devotion. For Tessa, it was a sudden shift from a nightmare of erasure back to floating safety. She explained the sweets were for Lillo and Ninna, her voice returning to its comfortable, rhythmic dialect.

“And you would rather go home to them than see any more of the Carnival?” Romola smiled, pointing Tessa toward the straight road back to the Porta Santa Croce, back to the simmering farro and the steady hum of the spinning wheel. Tessa murmured a reverent farewell and hurried away, undesirous of any further spectacle that sought to burn the beautiful, tactile things of the world.

From the shadows, Baldassarre watched the tall, gliding figure of Romola vanish around the stonework. He tightened his grip on his bundle of yarn. A new, complex recipe for vengeance was finally taking shape in his mind. He had seen the two wives together. The tall wife, Romola, carried the proud, ancient bloodlines; she possessed the deep linguistic heritage, the buried treasure of classical scholarship that Baldassarre so violently mourned. If he could prove her husband's infidelity, her noble resentment would ignite. She would understand the untranslatable pain of a shattered worldview. She would become the fierce, compounding ingredient he needed—another mind, another will, ready to crush Tito beneath the weight of his own deceits. Baldassarre retreated into the city's noise, his patience solidifying, waiting for the harvest of his hatred.

When Romola mentioned that someone else expected her, she referred to her cousin Brigida, wholly unaware of how desperately that good kinswoman hungered for rescue. Retreating toward the Piazza, they had spotted the white-robed youths halting before little Tessa. As Romola stepped close enough to gauge the simple *contadina’s* distress, saying, “Wait for me a moment, cousin,” Monna Brigida replied with breathless haste, “Ah, I will not go on: come for me to Boni’s shop—I shall go back there.”

In truth, Monna Brigida was suffocating under a profound sensory dissonance. The apothecary’s doorway beckoned with the grounding aromas of dried fennel, crushed coriander, and bitter orange peel, a stark contrast to the sterile, joyless atmosphere the Piagnoni had imposed upon Florence. Their austere piety hung in the air, leeching the rich, slow-simmered flavor from daily life. For Brigida, a widow of fifty-five whose spirits were sustained by the tactile and visual feasts of her youth, the shift was deeply unsettling. She relied on her morning toilette as a meticulous chef relies on their *mise en place*: her rouge was a delicate emulsion of ground cochineal and pressed sweet almond oil, her false braids woven like intricate spun-sugar confections. 

But the acoustic ecology of the city had grown harsh and unforgiving. The comforting, rolling cadence of the agrarian market women—the melodic haggling over wild chicory and fresh-pressed olive oil—had been violently overwritten by the shrill, synchronized chanting of the zealots. The noise battered Brigida’s ears, a mechanical, industrial clangor of moral absolutes that fractured her psychological peace. If her soul would truly prosper better on a diet of deprivation, was it worth applying the saffron-tinted rouge? Yet, when she looked in her hand-mirror and confronted a face akin to dough left too long to proof—sallow, with baggy cheeks and deep crows'-feet—her courage curdled. The younger women, with their cutting, modernized dialects, would mock her. Thus, the rouge, the braids, and the crimson-velvet *berretta* went on again, seasoned to perfection to mask the bitter taste of age.

Her retreat, however, lacked the necessary swiftness. The boys, moving with the terrifying precision of a swarm, intercepted her. Their arrival shattered the ambient hum of the street, replacing it with a piercing, discordant soprano that set Brigida’s teeth on edge. The youngest, a cherub of ten whose wide brown eyes were intoxicated by the sheer power of his own voice, blocked her path. But it was a youth of fifteen who wielded the dominant lexicon.

“Venerable mother!” he barked, brandishing Fra Girolamo’s ecclesiastical terminology like a butcher’s cleaver. “The blessed Jesus commands you to give up the *Anathema* which you carry upon you. That cap embroidered with pearls, those jewels that fasten up your false hair—let them be given up and sold for the poor!”

Brigida recoiled, trapped in the untranslatability of her own subjective experience. How could she explain, in her native, comforting dialect, that her finery was not an *Anathema*, but a vital tether to the vibrant, sensory-rich Florence of her youth? The dominant vocabulary of the Dominican teaching possessed no words for the quiet dignity of a well-crafted appearance, nor the deep, sustaining cultural memory embedded in an embroidered heirloom.

“Yes, lady,” chanted the youth at the other handle of the collection basket. “They are heavier than a millstone, weighting you for perdition. Will you adorn yourself with the hunger of the poor?”

“You look very ugly with the red on your cheeks and that black glistening hair,” piped the ten-year-old, his sweet voice a jarring contrast to the cruelty of his words. “Your Angel is sorry. He wants you to rub away the red.”

The boy thrust a soft silk scarf toward her. Surrounded by these young monitors, Brigida’s mother tongue failed her entirely. Her beloved Franciscan confessor, Fra Cristoforo, who spoke the earthy, forgiving language of the old neighborhoods, was not there to defend her. Stripped of her vernacular and her agency, possessed by a vague, trembling alarm, she took the scarf and scrubbed the sweet almond oil and carmine from her cheeks.

“It is a holy beginning,” the second youth proclaimed, his voice dripping with the patronizing cadence of the newly empowered. With impish cruelty, he pulled the jewelled pin from her head. The heavy black plait collapsed, dragging her beloved crimson *berretta*—whose seed-pearls resembled perfectly plump grains of arborio rice—into the basket of doom. Brigida stood bare, an unrouged woman whose deep lines of age had finally triumphed over her *embonpoint*. The youngsters hoisted the basket pitilessly, the false hair dangling like the entrails of a slaughtered calf.

Laughter rippled through the onlookers, a cacophony that stung worse than salt in a wound. The world she knew, the sensory landscape of feast days and nuanced conversation, was being actively erased, and Brigida felt the hot, hard tears of the elderly welling in her eyes.

Then, slicing through the shrill, religious noise, came a thick, guttural voice—a rough, textured sound deeply rooted in the agrarian soil of Tuscany. A large, calloused hand thrust a red cross before her face.

“Only four *quattrini*, madonna, blessing and all! Buy it. You’ll find a comfort in it now your wig’s gone.” It was Bratti, speaking the honest, utilitarian language of physical sustenance. “Deh! what are we sinners doing all our lives? Making soup in a basket, and getting nothing but the scum for our stomachs. Better buy a blessing, madonna! Only four *quattrini*; the profit is not so much as the smell of a *danaro*, and it goes to the poor.”

In dim-eyed confusion, Brigida reached toward her embroidered *scarsella*, eager to anchor herself to Bratti’s earthy, soup-making metaphor, which resonated in her bones far more than the sterile threats of perdition. Before she could pay, the suffocating circle of boys parted before a figure as welcome as cool water to a parched palate.

“Romola, look at me!” Brigida cried in a piteous tone, extending both hands.

The white troop, satisfied with their harvest of vanities, drifted away, their disruptive chanting fading into a tolerable murmur.

“Dear cousin, don’t be distressed,” Romola said softly, her rich, modulated voice immediately restoring the acoustic harmony of the space. Though smitten with pity, Romola, who possessed the discerning eye of a meticulous food historian uncovering an ancient, unadulterated recipe, could hardly help smiling at the sudden apparition of her kinswoman in a genuine, natural guise. She removed the dark drapery from her own head and draped it over Brigida’s. “There. No one will remark you now. We will turn down the Via del Palagio and go straight to our house.”
