They hurried away, Brigida clinging to Romola’s hand. “Ah, my Romola, my dear child!” the short, plump woman gasped, struggling to match the majestic young figure’s stride. “What an old scarecrow I am! I must be good—I mean to be good!”

“Yes, yes; buy a cross!” the guttural voice persisted. Bratti’s rough hand appeared once more, unbothered by Romola’s presence, driven by the relentless, traditional need to earn his daily bread. “Only four *quattrini*—and if there was any profit, it would all go to the poor.”

Brigida, raising one hand deprecatingly to stall Romola’s gentle remonstrance, reached out a *grosso* with the other. “Take it, good man, and begone.”

“You’re in the right, madonna,” Bratti said, snatching the coin and thrusting the cross into her hand. “I’ll not offer you change, for I might as well rob you of a mass. What! we must all be scorched a little, but you’ll come off the easier; better fall from the window than the roof. A good Easter and a good year to you!”

“Well, Romola,” Brigida cried pathetically as the peddler departed, “if I’m to be a Piagnone it’s no matter how I look!”

“Dear cousin,” Romola smiled affectionately, “you don’t know how much better you look than you ever did before. I see now how good-natured your face is. That red and finery seemed to thrust themselves forward and hide the true expression. Ask our Piero or any master of the craft if he would not rather capture your portrait now, stripped of its heavy, artificial glazes. I think all the natural lines of the human face have something grand, like a perfectly aged wheel of pecorino or a deeply rooted vine, unless they seem to come from low passions. How fine old men are, like my godfather! Why should not old women look grand and simple?”

“Yes, when one gets to be sixty, my Romola,” Brigida sighed, slipping back into the comforting, mournful vernacular of her youth. “But I’m only fifty-five, and Monna Berta, and everybody—but it’s no use: I will be good, like you. Your mother, if she’d been alive, would have been as old as I am; we were cousins together. One *must* either die or get old. But it doesn’t matter about being old, if one’s a Piagnone.”

The incidents of that Carnival day seemed, at first, to carry no other personal consequence for Romola than the new care of supporting poor cousin Brigida in her fluctuating resignation to age, but they initiated a Lenten cycle that turned with the slow, grinding rhythm of a heavy millstone. Bernardo del Nero had been elected Gonfaloniere. The Medicean triumph had deepened Romola’s presentiment of some secretly prepared scheme likely to ripen into either harvest or blight during these two months of her godfather’s authority. Every morning, the dim daybreak peered into her room, bringing with it the olfactory landscape of a city in distress—the sour, metallic tang of fear mingling with the scent of desperate hearth-fires burning damp, scavenged wood. 

Every morning, the haunting dread accompanied her as she walked through the echoing streets. Florence was starving, its cultural memory eroding with every closed bakery and abandoned kitchen. The rich, untranslatable dialects of the agrarian merchants, who once flooded the markets with their lively haggling over vibrant artichokes and salted anchovies, had been silenced. In their place was a harsh, industrial clatter of military carts and the murmuring groans of the hungry. Romola found her profoundest meaning in the physical act of sustenance, carrying heavy iron pots of broth to the sick. She spent her nights painstakingly decoding her late father’s ancient texts on Greek agriculture, translating mythic agrarian rituals into practical survival, boiling tough, foraged roots of wild chicory and simmering spelt until it released its nourishing starches.

When she arrived at the Duomo for the early sermon, she would temporarily lose the chill of her fears. The great cathedral was a vast acoustic cavern, and within it, she felt herself sharing in a passionate conflict that reverberated far beyond the walls of Florence. Savonarola was preaching the last course of Lenten sermons he would ever be allowed to finish. Excommunication was imminent, and he had reached the point of defying it. He held up the condition of the Church in the unflinching mirror of his speech, employing a dominant lexicon that stripped away all polite periphrases. His booming baritone resonated against the stone, a sonic force designed to shatter the corruption of Rome. 

Yet, as the Lenten cycle repeated itself, that mighty music began to jar upon Romola’s carefully attuned acoustic ecology. Earlier, the Frate had preached peace, charity, and an oblivion of political differences, his words settling over the congregation like the comforting aroma of slow-braised meats on a winter evening. Now, political intrigue had curdled the atmosphere. The hostility attacking the Prophet introduced a spirit of contention that pierced conspicuously into his sermons. He abandoned the nuanced, nourishing language of spiritual care for a rigid, defiant vocabulary. He who had once denounced the desire for the miraculous now asserted that Divine power would attest to his prophetic preaching by a literal miracle. The language of his inner voices was written in letters of fire, a fierce, binary dictionary that threatened vengeance and left no room for the subtle, untranslatable subjective experiences of his flock. 

If the shrill tones of exasperation disrupted Romola’s psychological equilibrium, there was another member of the audience for whom those vibrations were the only thrilling sounds. Baldassarre, the ruined scholar, had found out that the wonderful Frate was preaching again. He attended the sermons to drink in the threats of a voice that sounded like justice, absorbing the deep bass notes of impending vengeance like a deaf man feeling the percussive strike of a drum. He went the more because he had seen Romola there. Twice, Romola had caught sight of his dark glance fixed upon hers. She looked for it with the same primal apprehension a farmer feels when scanning the horizon for a portentous storm, though her anxiety regarding the old man remained subordinate to the immediate, sensory pressing of the present.

The stirring Lent passed; April, the final month of her godfather’s supreme authority, neared its close. Famine stubbornly refused to loosen its grip, and the Plague was expected. As Romola tried to gather courage from the rhythmic, grounding rituals of crushing wild fennel and kneading coarse barley loaves for her patients, she was met on the afternoon of the twenty-seventh by a messenger from Camilla Rucellai. Camilla, chief among the feminine seers of Florence, demanded her presence forthwith on matters of the highest moment.

Romola shrank from the summons. She possessed a constitutional repulsion for the shallow excitability of such illuminated women, viewing their disembodied mysticism as an affront to the sacred, tangible realities of earth, water, fire, and flour. Furthermore, rumor suggested Camilla had announced revelations hostile to Bernardo del Nero. Romola’s impulse was to close her ears to a soundscape that would only increase her mental burden, but, bound by her habit of obeying outward claims, she wiped the flour from her hands and went straight to Camilla.

She found the nervous, grey-haired woman in a chamber arranged like a convent cell—a sterile, airless space completely devoid of the aromas of life. There was no garlic braided on the walls, no scent of simmering herbs; only the dusty, stagnant odor of unwashed cloth and fanaticism. The thin fingers clutching Romola, and the eager voice addressing her in a loud, scraping whisper, caused Romola a physical shrinking. The sonic environment was agonizing. Camilla’s voice rose higher into an excited treble, a shrill frequency that assaulted Romola’s ears, as she narrated a vision revealed by Romola’s Angel: Romola supposedly knew secrets concerning her godfather, which, if disclosed, would save the Republic. 

Camilla exhorted Romola to separate herself from the enemy of God. Romola, whose impetuosity was of a massive, grounded nature, sat in calm self-control until the exhortation became unbearable. She possessed a deep disgust for women whose faculties were entirely wrought up into fantasies, neglecting the physical sustenance and emotional labor that truly nurtured humanity. When Romola attempted to wrench her arm away, the prophetess only tightened her crab-like hold, her voice spiraling into a piercing statement of violent visions. Christ himself, she shrieked, had ordered that citizens in office should throw Bernardo del Nero from the window of the Palazzo Vecchio. Fra Girolamo knew of it, and had not denied its Divine authority.

“And since then,” said Camilla, her wild eyes straining upward, her pitch reaching a maddening, glass-shattering intensity, “the Blessed Infant has come to me and laid a wafer of sweetness on my tongue in token of his pleasure that I had done his will.”

The sheer artificiality of the image—a phantom, sugary wafer entirely divorced from the sacred, labor-intensive reality of true communion bread, baked from the hard-won wheat of the Tuscan soil—broke Romola’s restraint. 

“Let me go!” said Romola, in a deep, resonant voice of anger that cut through the shrillness. “God grant you are mad! else you are detestably wicked!”

With a violent effort, she wrenched her arm free and rushed out of the room, fleeing the auditory assault and the sterile stench of the cell. She did not pause until she had hurried far along the street and slipped behind the heavy leather curtain of the church of the Badia. Here, under the old stone arch, she found a sanctuary shut in from the noise and mechanical hurry of the city. The thick masonry swallowed the industrial clatter of carts and the grating friction of political machinery, offering a pristine acoustic ecology where an eternal peace seemed to subsist.

Sinking down on the step of the altar in front of Filippino Lippi’s painting of the Virgin appearing to Saint Bernard, Romola waited for her inward tumult to subside. The mythic occurrence on the canvas—the divine intersecting seamlessly with the earthly—stood in stark contrast to Camilla’s violent delusions. The thought that pressed upon her most acutely was that Camilla could allege Savonarola’s countenance of her wicked folly. Savonarola’s political teaching was founded on strict law and order; he distrusted spirit-seeing women. Why, then, did he not publicly denounce these pretended revelations that brought darkness across the conception of a Supreme Will?

The answer came to her in the quiet resonance of the church: he was fettered inwardly because such visions were not distinctly separable from his own. He was trapped in his own dominant lexicon. To denounce Camilla would be to undermine the vocabulary of Divine inspiration of which he, and his supplementary seer Fra Salvestro, were the primary authors. 

Kneeling with her buried face on the altar-step, Romola endured a sickening realization. The enthusiasm that had been her sustaining broth, the energy that made life worthy, was inevitably bound up with wilful eye-shutting. Her mind rushed back with a deep, craving attraction toward the worldly sense and untheoretic virtues of her godfather. Bernardo held on to the old ties, the rich, multifaceted dialects of Florentine history that refused to be flattened by the Frate’s new regime. Yet, with this thought rose the bitter presentiment of some plot to restore the Medici. 

She felt that to keep the Government of Florence pure was a sacred cause; the Frate had carried her understanding there. But her heart recoiled from a right allied to so much narrowness—a harsh, systematic judgment that measured men by a superficial lexicon of assents and denials, erasing the complex, untranslatable essence of their manhood. Her affection clung to her godfather, and through him, to the memory of her father, resisting the division of humanity into sheep and goats by the easy mark of a political symbol. Ideas, Romola knew, were hardly strong agents unless they were dissolved in a rich, complex solvent of feeling, just as dry herbs yield no medicine until they are steeped in boiling water.

Her indignation, roused by Camilla’s shrill acoustics, ran like an illuminating fire over the kindred facts in Savonarola’s teaching. The great orator's words now sounded like the superficial commercialization of faith, trading the deep, resonant truths of human duty for the cheap currency of fanaticism. It was an illumination that made all life look ghastly, starved of true sustenance. Where were the beings with whom she could work and endure? On the side of moral energy lay a fanaticism she now shrank from; on the side of affection lay the scent of secret plotting and crime. 

Calmness would not come, not even here in the quiet stone baffle of the Badia. The rocky solitude of the saint in the picture was too far removed from the tactile, olfactory demands of her daily reality. Romola realized she must return to the rhythmic, sensory labor of her own life. She rose from her knees, intending to hasten back to the courtyard, to the simmering pots of broth and the mixing of poultices for her sick people, hoping that by some immediate, physical act of nourishment, she might revive her sense of worth.

But as she turned round, the breath caught in her throat. She found herself face to face with a man standing only two yards away, intruding upon the silent acoustic sanctuary. 

The man was Baldassarre.

“I would speak with you,” said Baldassarre. His voice lacked the resonant timbre it must have once possessed, worn down now to a frayed, eroded dialect of grief. Romola looked at him in silent expectation. It was plain that he had followed her through the narrow, echoing streets, waiting for the precise moment to approach. She was going, at last, to know the secret about him.

“Yes,” she said, submitting to the inevitable with the quiet resignation of one beginning a long, arduous fast. “But you wish to go where no one can hear us?”

“Where *he* will not come upon us,” said Baldassarre, glancing behind him timidly, as if the oppressive, mercantile clatter of Florence were a physical predator. “Out—in the air—away from the streets.” 

“I sometimes go to San Miniato at this hour,” said Romola. She longed to escape the superficial commercialization of the city below, its harsh, metallic din grating against her psychological well-being. “If you like, I will go now, and you can follow me. It is far, but we can be solitary there.”

He nodded assent, and Romola set out. To some women, retreating to an isolated spot with a man exhibiting the outward signs of madness—the very madness Tito had so smoothly diagnosed—might have seemed an alarming risk. But Romola possessed no personal fears; she only welcomed the physical distance, the steady, rhythmic exertion that would delay the next bitter drop of truth from falling onto her tongue. The afternoon was far advanced, the sun sinking low, when she paused on the rough ground. The acoustic ecology of the hill was a profound sanctuary; the wind sifted through the ancient cypress trunks, carrying the pungent, sun-baked aromas of crushed wild thyme, drying clay, and the sharp, resinous tang of the earth. 

When Baldassarre reached her, he sank heavily onto a ridge of stony ground. His thickset frame had lost the sturdy, agrarian vigor it possessed when he first appeared bound in the Duomo; the steep ascent left his breathing a harsh, shallow rasp, and his eyes carried the helpless vagueness of a man whose internal compass had been shattered. 

“The hill is steep,” said Romola with compassionate gentleness, seating herself beside him, attuned to the grounding scent of the soil. “And I fear you have been weakened by want?”

He fixed his eyes on her in a silence that spoke of profound untranslatability. The moment for speech had arrived, but he seemed unable to seize the vocabulary to bridge the chasm between his subjective horror and the physical world. She remained motionless, allowing the quiet hum of the hillside to calm him. He looked like nothing more than a neglected, common-bred elder, but Romola had learned to find profound meaning in the presence of those intimately tied to the earth and its rawest sufferings. Gradually, a definite expression gathered in his gaze, and he spoke with abrupt emphasis.

“Ah! you would have been my daughter!”

The words landed like a drop of concentrated rue on Romola’s palate. A swift flush of blood rose in her face and vanished just as swiftly, leaving her lips parted and white, a marble image of horror tasting the astringent bite of absolute betrayal. The revelation was complete. She divined the sprawling, cyclical tragedy that lay behind that single phrase. 

The sensitive, physical response of her face acted as a stimulus to Baldassarre; his fragmented words had finally wrought their right effect. He pressed on with gathering eagerness, laying his rough hand upon her arm. “You are a woman of proud blood—is it not true? You go to hear the preacher; you hate baseness—baseness that smiles and triumphs. You hate your husband?”

“Oh God! were you really his father?” Romola asked in a low voice, too entirely possessed by the interwoven timelines of her past to answer his question. “Or was it as he said? Did you take him when he was little?”

“Ah, you believe me—you know what he is!” Baldassarre exulted, tightening his grip as though her physical solidity anchored him. “You will help me?”

“Yes,” she said, though her definition of the word belonged to an entirely different lexicon. She laid her palm gently over his weathered hand, her eyes welling with tears. “Oh, it is piteous! Tell me—you were a great scholar; you taught him. How is it?” She broke off. Tito’s allegation of madness echoed like an acoustic pollutant in her mind, a jarring noise against the quiet truth of the old man’s grief. She sat perfectly still.

“It is gone!—it is all gone!” said Baldassarre, a mournful testament to the erosion of his cultural memory. “And they would not believe me, because he lied, and said I was mad; and they had me dragged to prison. And I am old—my mind will not come back. And the world is against me.” He paused, sinking under a wave of despondency, before looking up with renewed, fiery urgency. “But *you* are not against me. He made you love him, and he has been false to you. Yes, he made *me* love him: he was beautiful and gentle, and I was a lonely man. I took him when they were beating him. He slept in my bosom when he was little. I became his elder, his provider. I pressed the sweetest oils from the first harvest to heal his skin; I baked the coarse, sustaining bread of the earth to build his flesh. I poured out my meticulous histories, the rich, simmering broths of antiquity, feeding him the very marrow of my knowledge, and everything that was mine I meant to be his.”

Baldassarre’s voice trembled with the grief of a passionate historian whose deepest offerings had been discarded. “But to him, my sustenance was mere utilitarian fuel. He possessed no sensory reverence for what I gave. He had my gems—the crystallized heritage of the ancients—and he sold them, trading profound art for superficial commercial coin. He left me in slavery. He never came to seek me, and when I returned poor and in misery, he denied me. He wielded the dominant lexicon of this city against me. He said I was a madman.”

“He told us his father was dead—was drowned,” Romola said faintly, the sour, metallic taste of Tito’s deceit flooding her mouth. “Surely he must have believed it then. Oh! he could not have been so base *then*!” The mythic purity of her early love, a time when she thought no more of poison in him than a child suspects toxicity in a sweet, edible flower, rose up and clashed violently with her present reality. With one great sob, her tears rushed forth.

“Ah, you are young, and the tears come easily,” said Baldassarre with a chef's impatience for a spoiled reduction. “But tears are no good; they only quench the fire within, and it is the fire that works, that reduces the bitter root to justice. Tears will hinder us. Listen to me.”

Romola started slightly, the threat of his madness darting again through her thoughts. But her deep belief in his history remained firm, and it was out of sympathy for his profound language death that she asked, as gently as she could, “Tell me, how did you lose your memory—your scholarship?”

“I was ill. I can’t tell how long—it was a blank,” he said, describing the brutal starvation of his mind. “The rich vocabulary of my life suffered a sudden death. I remember nothing, only at last I was sitting in the sun among the stones, and the syntax of my existence was darkness. Slowly, I felt a longing for something that never came. On the ship, on the waters, I began to know what I hungered for: it was for the Boy to come back, to restore the native tongue of my thoughts, for I was locked away outside them all. And I am outside now. I feel nothing but a wall and darkness.” 

His head sank between his hands. Romola’s pity swelled. Had she not daily seen her own father sitting in his library, aching for Dino, starving for the future he had meant to cultivate in his son? 

“It all came back once,” Baldassarre went on, his tone hardening. “I was master of everything. I saw all the world again, the rich ingredients of my past, my gems, my books. I thought I had him in my power, and I went to expose him where the lights and the trees were. But he lied again. He used his smooth, hollow words to label me a madman, and they dragged me to prison... Wickedness is strong; and he wears armour.” 

The fierce intensity flared. He grasped Romola’s arm again. “But you will help me? He has been false to you too. He has another wife, and she has children. He makes her believe he is her husband, and she is a foolish, helpless thing. I will show you where she lives.”

The shock that passed through Romola tasted like ash. The sacred, everyday domestic routine of her life had been a fiction, secretly interwoven with a hidden, parallel timeline. The mythic horror of this duality ignited a fierce, protective anger within her. Baldassarre instinctively felt her sympathetic resonance.

“You hate him,” he said. “Is it not true? There is no love between you. I know women can hate; you have proud blood. You hate falseness, and you can hunger for revenge.”

Paralyzed by the sensory overload of conflicting realities, Romola was barely conscious of the bruising grip on her arm. 

“You shall contrive it,” Baldassarre whispered eagerly. “I have learned by heart that you are his rightful wife. You are a noble woman. You go to hear the preacher of vengeance; you will help justice. My mind goes—the lexicon fails sometimes—all but the fire. The fire is God: it is justice: it will not die. You believe that—is it not true? If they will not hang him for robbing me, you will strip him. You will take away the clanking, unnatural noise of his armour, that acoustic pollution that shields his hollow core, and I will stab him. I have a knife, and my arm is still strong enough.”

He reached beneath his tunic and drew the hidden blade, running his thumb abstractedly along its edge, needing the grounding physical sensation to keep the fractured ideas in his mind alive. It was a simple, agrarian tool, primed to pare away the rot. 

To Romola, every fresh hour of life tasted more complex and difficult than the last. Her judgment was too vigorous to offer futile, deprecatory words to a man locked in such an untranslatable state of grief. She needed to manipulate the pacing, to let the boiling pot of his excitement cool.

She spoke tremulously. “You say she is foolish and helpless—that other wife—and believes him to be her real husband. Perhaps he is: perhaps he married her before he married me.”

“I cannot tell,” said Baldassarre, pausing his rhythmic stroking of the blade, bewildered by the tangled timelines. “I can remember no more. I only know where she lives. You shall see her. I will take you; but not now. *He* may be there. The night is coming on.”

“It is true,” Romola said, starting up with the sudden consciousness that the sun had vanished, leaving the hills bathed in a cool, dark silence. “But you will come and take me—when?”

“In the morning,” Baldassarre muttered, dreaming she shared his appetite for immediate vengeance. “Come to me, then, where you came to me to-day, in the church. I will be there at ten; and if you are not there, I will go again towards mid-day. Can you remember?”

“Mid-day,” Romola said.

“Only mid-day. The same place, and mid-day. And, after that,” he added, rising to grasp her arm with his left hand while his right held the knife, “we will have our revenge. He shall feel the sharp edge of justice. The world is against me, but you will help me.”

“I would help you in other ways,” Romola offered, making a timid effort to introduce a gentler dialect to his obsession. “I fear you are in want; you labour for so little. I should like to bring you rich, warming sustenance, to make you feel again that there is someone who honors you.”

“Talk no more about that,” Baldassarre snapped fiercely. “I will have nothing else. Help me to wring one drop of vengeance on this side of the grave. I have nothing but my knife. It is sharp; but there is a moment after the thrust when men see the face of death,—and it shall be my face that he will taste.”

He released her and sank back down to the stony earth. Romola felt utterly helpless; she must defer all action to the morrow.

“Mid-day, then,” she said, her voice cutting clearly through the quiet acoustic space.

“Yes,” he answered, exhaling a breath of profound exhaustion. “Go; I will rest here.”

She hastened down the steep path. Turning at the last bend where the fragrant cypress trees parted, she looked back and saw him seated still, a solitary elder waiting in the fading light.

Romola’s purpose had simmered within her throughout the afternoon, thickening like a slow-cooked *peposo* until it reached a rolling, unavoidable boil. It was less a conscious resolve than a bodily necessity of her feeling, an instinct passed down through generations of Florentine women who understood the precise alchemy of timing. Heedless of the darkening streets and the acrid, metallic tang of oxidized iron that tainted the usually comforting evening air—an olfactory harbinger of drawn blades—she hurried toward the Old Palace. The bridge spanning the Arno was a threshold; below, the river ran thick and black as cuttlefish ink, while the dying sun bled over the distant hills in hues of crushed pomegranate. 

She could not wait for Maso, the aging agrarian elder whose slow, methodical gait belonged to the damp, wild-fennel-scented rhythms of the harvest rather than the panicked, echoing cobblestones of the city. The acoustic ecology of Florence was fracturing around her. The rhythmic, soothing clack of wooden spoons against terracotta and the steady, resonant grinding of stone pestles in neighborhood kitchens—sounds essential to the city's psychological equilibrium—were being aggressively swallowed by the harsh, martial clatter of pikes and the shouted, vulgar commands of militiamen. It was a violent intrusion of noise pollution, an oppressive new lexicon of power that threatened to drown out the nuanced, untranslatable local dialects of domestic life. 

She did not know what she passed in the pillared court or up the wide stairs; her sensory appreciation was entirely overwhelmed by the sterile, institutional coldness of the stones, devoid of the roasted garlic and woodsmoke that gave a space its soul. She only knew that she begged an usher to see the Gonfaloniere. Soon the door opened, and Bernardo del Nero entered. His white head was held erect above his silk *lucco*, the fabric carrying the faint, grounding aroma of dried figs, aged parchment, and the loamy terroir of his rural vineyards. 

“Romola, my child, what is this?” he asked, his voice a rich, low baritone that resonated with the ancestral acoustics of the hills. He closed the heavy door, shutting out the palace's chaotic din. 

She uncovered her head and went toward him, her tongue struggling against the inherent untranslatability of her subjective terror. He laid his hands on her shoulders. Her face was haggard, her hair unbound, yet her eyes burned with a feverish intensity that eclipsed her hunger and exhaustion. 

“What has he done?” Bernardo demanded abruptly. “Tell me everything, child; throw away pride. I am your father.”

“It is not about myself—nothing about myself,” Romola said hastily, the words tumbling out. “Dearest godfather, it is about you. I have heard things—some I cannot articulate, for they belong to a language of deceit I barely comprehend. But you are in danger in the palace. There are fanatical men whose tongues are coated in the saccharine deceit of imported promises, masking the rot of their intentions. Trust nobody.”

Bernardo smiled, raising a hand that had spent decades assessing the ripeness of olives to pat her head gently. “Have you worked yourself up into this agitation, my poor child, to tell such old truth as that to an old man like me?”

“Oh no, no! They are not old truths!” Romola pressed her clasped hands painfully together. “They are fresh things. Is it too late to say, ‘Go to your villa’? Return to the agrarian rhythms. Keep away in the country where the soil remembers our true names, at least until these three days of office are over. Oh God! If any harm comes to you, it will be as if I had served the poison myself!”

“Poor child!” Bernardo looked at her with quiet, penetrating affection. “Go, Romola—go home and rest. These fears may be only big ugly shadows of something very little. Even traitors must see their interest in betraying; the rats will run where they smell the sharp, aged *pecorino*, and there is no knowing yet which way the wind will carry the rind's scent.” 

He paused, his eyes turning away with the mournful acceptance of an elder watching his native dialect fade. “As for warnings, they are of no use to me. I enter into no plots, but I never forsake the recipes of my ancestors. If I march abreast with obstinate men who will rush on guns and pikes, I must share the consequences. I have not many harvests left at the bottom of my sack for them to rob me of. Go, child.”

He touched her head again, and she clung to his arm, inhaling the scent of his wool and camphor—the last tangible remnants of a young filial life that now felt as mythic and distant as an ancient feast. He folded her black drapery around her head. “There, hasten away. You are like a black ghost; you will be safe enough.”

When Romola fell asleep that night, her slumber was profoundly deep, a circular return to a multi-generational memory of women kneading coarse semolina beneath the watchful eyes of household saints. But her rest was shattered at sunrise by the brutal, concussive roar of artillery. Piero de’ Medici, with thirteen hundred men, was before the Rome gate. The guns were an atrocity of noise pollution, a deafening mechanical violence that obliterated the delicate acoustic ecosystem of morning—the cooing doves, the sweeping of hearths, the soft murmurs of vendors arranging bruised plums. 

Maso brought the news, his tunic smelling of damp earth and crushed rosemary, offering circumstantial additions gathered from the panicked streets. The great Piazza was full of armed men; chief citizens suspected as friends of the Medici had been detained. The city was serving Piero an ugly, heavily spiced welcome, and soldiers from Pisa were marching to join the bitter fray.

In her memory of those morning hours, actual external experiences dissolved into tumultuous, overlapping timelines of retrospect and anticipation. She knew she had walked to the Badia through streets smelling of sour wine and burnt oil. She had waited there in vain. As she stood on the church steps, the doors closing behind her for the afternoon interval, the scene washed over her like a waking myth. The guns fired again, but the concussive blasts only provoked a harsh, discordant laughter from the crowds—a superficial, crass noise that mocked the sanctity of life and the city's old linguistic harmony. 

She soon learned the cause. Piero de’ Medici and his horsemen had turned their backs on Florence, galloping away along the Siena road, their martial clatter fading into the dust. She heard this from a substantial shop-keeping Piagnone, a meticulous man whose hands bore the flour-dusted calluses of a master baker. 

“It is true,” the baker ended, a certain lactic bitterness in his tone, like dough left too long to ferment. “Piero is gone, but there are those left behind who fed him the secret recipes of our city's defenses—we all know that. If the new Signoria does its duty, we shall soon know who they are.”

The dominant lexicon of vengeance darted through Romola like a sharp spasm, an acidic bite on the palate. The cultural memory of Florence was eroding, the old dialect of safety dead. Yet as she entered her home again, the political rot foreshadowed by the baker was not yet close upon her; her most pressing, gnawing hunger was the terrifying possibility that she had lost sight of Baldassarre, his existence swallowed entirely by the deafening, untranslatable noise of the changing world.

The lengthening sunny days turned on the ancient spindle of the seasons, a circular rhythm of light and heat that brought neither what Romola most desired nor what she most dreaded. The summer advanced without a sign from the agrarian elder, Baldassarre, and, despite the Government's anxious surveillance, no revelation of the suspected conspiracy materialized. Yet the turning wheel of the year brought the spreading Plague and the Excommunication of Savonarola, events that bridged the phantom-crowded spaces of her anxiety with the immediate, visceral demands of the hearth and the street. 

In her kitchen, Romola found a mythic anchor in the meticulous daily routines of sustenance. Generation upon generation of Florentine women had stood where she stood, their interwoven timelines converging in the act of feeding the afflicted. She spent her mornings rendering marrow, bruising wild thyme gathered from the hills of Fiesole, and slow-simmering capons in heavy iron pots until the medicinal bone broths became rich, golden elixirs of life. She could not carry these steaming, fragrant pottages into the abodes of pestilence—where the bitter, metallic scent of sickness hung heavy in the air—without remembering that she owed this transcendent moral existence to Fra Girolamo. It was he who had awakened her to the profound meaning found in serving the vulnerable. How could she witness the silencing of a man who sought to make Christian charity a tangible, nourishing reality, without feeling drawn fiercely to his side? 

Far on in the sweltering days of June, the Excommunication, arrived from Rome weeks prior, was solemnly published in the Duomo. Romola went to witness the scene, needing to immerse herself in the event so that the resistance it inspired might invigorate her. To step into the city streets was to run a gauntlet of grating, mercantile noise—the harsh, rhythmic clack of coin presses and the shrieking wooden wheels of merchant carts. This was the acoustic pollution of a city rapidly commercializing its soul, an auditory violence that fractured psychological well-being. She sought refuge in the vast, echoing cavern of the Duomo, hoping for the resonant, aligning acoustics she had once experienced there.

Instead, the scene stood in stark, somber contrast to the days when the mighty, symphonic voice of the Frate had thrilled the upturned faces of the citizens, vibrating through the stone with the rich timbre of their native Tuscan dialect. Now, the walls were steeped in the dim glimmer of tapers. A black and grey flock of monks sat with unexpectant faces. The sonic environment was oppressive: the jarring, occasional tinkling of little bells fractured the heavy silence, followed by the drone of a monotonous voice reading the papal sentence. This was the imposition of a dominant lexicon—the sterile, weaponized Latin of Rome, designed to crush the colloquial truths of the Florentine people. Finally came the extinction of the tapers, leaving only the slow, shuffling friction of monkish feet departing into the dark. 

Romola’s ardour for the Frate was doubly strengthened by the gleeful triumph she saw in the coarse faces of his enemies, and by the fear-stricken confusion among his friends. To most pious Florentines, the Divine potency of the Church was an inalienable impression, as natural as the scent of rising sourdough or the concavity of the blue firmament. Savonarola’s written arguments that the Excommunication was invalid made them tremble, for they viewed the Church’s decree as a mystic force. But Romola, whose mind had been nourished on her father’s meticulous historical lexicons rather than the traditional associations of the Church, felt her connection to faith only through the moral force of Savonarola. In this Excommunication, she heard only the language of hostile vice. On one side was a man devoted to spiritual purity and the communal table; on the other was the assault of alarmed selfishness, headed by a lustful, greedy old man, once called Rodrigo Borgia, who wielded his dominant vocabulary from the pinnacle of papal infamy. 

Savonarola was now a standard-bearer leaping into the breach, appealing to the Christian world against a vicious exercise of ecclesiastical power. By this new current of enthusiasm, Romola was sustained through the oppressive summer. She had ventured no words to Tito regarding her interview with Baldassarre. What could such agitating words possibly win from a man who viewed the world—and its meals—as mere utilitarian fuel? Tito lacked any sensory appreciation for the ancient, agrarian roots of their culture; to him, the crust of a peasant’s hard-won rye bread and a banquet of imported, flavorless white loaves were entirely interchangeable, so long as they served his social ascent. The subjective horror Romola carried regarding Baldassarre’s revelation was wholly untranslatable into Tito’s smooth, commercial vocabulary. She knew he would offer only a cool, superficial sarcasm. 

The true terror was not that the helpless Baldassarre would injure Tito, but that Tito might find the old man and execute some new, ruthless scheme to silence him. Romola felt she could do nothing decisive until she saw Baldassarre again, until she tasted the bitter truth of his claim about that "other wife"—whether this woman was the one to whom Tito was first bound. The mere possibility of this other wife mingled like rancid oil with the earliest memories of her illusory love, spoiling the lingering associations of tenderness. Her irresistible belief in Baldassarre’s revelation made her shrink from Tito with a visceral revulsion.

She might have been driven to passionate, accusatory speech had Tito not been mostly absent. Like many wealthy citizens fleeing the pestilence, he spent his days in the country. The agreeable Melema was a welcome guest at many rural villas, indulging in opulent, soul-deadening feasts devoid of any true culinary heritage, leaving Romola alone in the suffocating city. 

By the later days of July, the Plague had almost disappeared, and a favorable magistracy was writing urgent letters to Rome, entreating the withdrawal of the Excommunication. With the public troubles demanding less of her restorative broths and physical labor, Romola was left to the languor of her personal lot. Her healthy frame ached from continuous over-exertion, but her mental restlessness drove her out into the cool acoustic sanctuaries of the early mornings and late evenings. 

She walked the ancient streets constantly, breathing in the scent of roasted garlic and bruised summer dust that drifted from the open windows of the poor. She varied her routes, clinging to the vague hope that she might encounter Baldassarre among the market stalls. She feared that some illness had brought a new paralysis to his mind—that the native dialect of his memories had suffered a quiet, tragic language death, erasing her very existence from his thoughts. That was her most sanguine explanation. The one she felt to be most probable, tasting like ash on her tongue, was that the old agrarian elder, along with the irreplaceable cultural memory he carried, had simply been swallowed by the Plague.

The morning warmth was already beginning to press with an oppressive, stifling weight upon Romola. Having left San Marco behind, she navigated away from the mercantile center of Florence, where the superficial, metallic clangor of commercialized trade—a persistent, grating noise pollution—frayed the edges of her psychological well-being. But as she turned toward the intersecting streets at the gate of Santa Croce, the Borgo La Croce offered a sudden, healing acoustic sanctuary.

The street was so still that the sonic environment shifted into a restorative quiet. She listened to the rhythmic cadence of her own footsteps against the sun-baked pavement, a grounding acoustic ecology that connected her to the ancient, circular pacing of the city’s generations. Approaching a bend in the street, this timeless silence was suddenly punctuated by a new sound: the soft, fleshy slap of bare feet. A few yards before her stood a little child, not more than three years old, wearing nothing but a white linen shirt. 

He paused from a waddling run, a sturdy, square-backed figure like a cherub pulled from a domestic myth, crowned with a dense cloud of reddish-brown curls. As he turned toward her, the sheer untranslatability of his infantile subjective experience was written in his dark, tear-filled eyes. His lower lip pushed up, trembling, and his fat brown fists clutched his shirt helplessly. The sudden shadow cast by Romola’s tall, black-clad figure brought his bewildered fear to a climax, and a loud, piercing sob shattered the quiet street—a raw, acoustic disruption of grief.

Romola, her passionate tenderness surging in response to the visceral sound of distress, instantly uncovered her head. She stooped down on the pavement, wrapping her arms around his warm, earthy little body, pressing her cheek against his, and speaking in low, caressing tones. At first, the resonance of his sobs only grew louder, bouncing off the stone walls, but he did not pull away. Presently, the outburst ceased with that strange, abrupt rhythm unique to childish joys and sorrows. His face lost its distortion, settling into an open-mouthed gaze at Romola.

“You have lost yourself, little one,” she said, her voice a soft hum against the oppressive heat. “Never mind! We will find the house again. Perhaps mamma will meet us.”

She could smell the sweet, milky scent of his skin and divined he had slipped away when his mother’s eyes were turned. “Oh, what a heavy, heavy boy!” she said, feeling the dense, muscular weight of him as she tried to lift him. “I cannot carry you. Come, then, you must toddle back by my side.”

The parted lips remained motionless in awed silence. One brown fist retained its desperate grip on his shirt, but the other surrendered to Romola’s strong, soft white hand. 

“You *have* a mamma?” she asked, looking down with a sudden, yearning ache. He remained mute. A girl might have filled the air with abundant chirping, but this square-shouldered little man navigated his world through physical sensation rather than spoken language. 

He was fiercely attuned to his physical environment. At the turning by the front of San Ambrogio, he dragged Romola toward it, pulling her hand. 

“Ah, that is the way home, is it?” she smiled. He merely thrust his head forward and pulled, communicating through the undeniable weight of his body. 

After another turning, they entered a short street leading to open garden ground, rich with the loamy scent of sun-warmed soil. The boy paused before a house at the end of the street, pulling her toward a flight of stone stairs. They mounted the steps, leaving the blinding sunlight for the cool, shadowed acoustics of the stairwell, climbing until an extra stream of light spilled from an open doorway on the final landing. 

Romola paused in the threshold, her footsteps entirely masked by the thick, domestic atmosphere. The room before her was a sweeping tableau of interwoven timelines, where the mythic resonance of motherhood and agrarian tradition seamlessly integrated into the everyday. 

At the farther end of the room, fast asleep with her head tilted and her hand resting on the edge of a wooden cradle, sat Tessa. But it was the figure near the window that captivated Romola’s sensory appreciation. Monna Lisa sat with her back to the door, an agrarian elder anchored in the timeless rituals of sustenance. She existed in a profound acoustic isolation, deaf to the world's discord, which allowed her to focus entirely on the mortar and pestle in her lap. She was a meticulous guardian of culinary history, preparing the midday salad. Her deeply lined hands bruised sharp, purple-skinned cloves of garlic against coarse sea salt, the olive-wood pestle releasing a pungent, sulfurous aroma that cut sharply through the room’s stale heat. Beside her lay a mound of wild dandelion greens and bitter chicory—foraged, Romola recognized instantly, from the rugged hills beyond the city, carrying the peppery, untamed memory of the soil. A generous drizzle of cold-pressed, unfiltered olive oil, glowing with a grassy, golden-green hue, bound the crushed herbs and splashed against the earthenware. To Romola, who drew profound psychological nourishment from the honest, sensory truth of sustenance, the olfactory landscape of the room was a sudden tether to reality. It was a stark, beautiful contrast to the utilitarian, sterile machinations of her husband’s political world, where meals were consumed merely as fuel for ambition.

There was only an instant for Romola to breathe in the sharp scent of the bruised chicory before Lillo snatched his hand away. He ran to his mother, leaning his head back against her arm and surveying Romola seriously. 

As Lillo pushed against her, Tessa’s eyes fluttered open. Her glance fell upon the tall figure in the doorway, and she started up, blushing deeply, trembling in the sudden intersection of her domestic reality and this mythic apparition.

“Ah! we have seen each other before,” said Romola, stepping forward, her voice a gentle modulation in the quiet room. “I am glad it was *your* little boy. He was crying in the street; I suppose he had run away. But you had not missed him? That is well, else you would have been frightened.”

The physical shock of her son's escape overcame Tessa. Her color vanished, and seizing Lillo’s arm, she ran to the old food historian, her voice rising in a panicked half-sob that pierced Monna Lisa’s acoustic sanctuary. 

“Oh, Lisa, you are wicked! Why will you stand with your back to the door? Lillo ran away ever so far into the street.”

“Holy Mother!” said Monna Lisa in a thick, reedy tone, her worn hands dropping the wooden spoon. “Where were *you*, then? I thought you had your eye on him.”

“But you *know* I go to sleep when I am rocking,” Tessa protested in pettish remonstrance, her phrasing heavily inflected with her native *contadina* dialect. It was a rustic vernacular deeply intertwined with the agrarian earth, its vowels softened by generations of rural isolation—a fading cultural memory slowly eroding under the dominance of Florence’s aggressive, mercantile lexicon.

“Well, well, we must keep the outer door shut,” Monna Lisa muttered, returning to her fragrant herbs. “He’ll be as cunning as Satan before long. But how came he back?”

The question forcefully reminded Tessa of Romola. She turned, timid, while Monna Lisa offered a low, traditional reverence. 

“Doubtless the most noble lady brought him back,” the old woman said, the smell of garlic and rich oil heavy on her apron. “It’s my shame for him to be found with only his shirt on; but he kicked, and wouldn’t have his other clothes on this morning, and the mother, poor thing, will never hear of his being beaten. Let your nobleness look at his legs.”

Lillo, acutely aware of his physical form being discussed, pulled his shirt up higher, examining the olive roundness of his sturdy legs with dispassionate curiosity. Romola laughed, a rich, genuine sound, and stooped to give him a caressing shake and a kiss. This physical reassurance eased Tessa’s anxiety. When her husband, Naldo, had told her about the beautiful lady at the Carnival, he had used a dominant religious lexicon, calling her "one of the saints." In Tessa's subjective experience—a worldview shaped by cyclical seasons and church frescoes—Romola was a mythic occurrence made flesh. 

“Ninna’s in the cradle,” Tessa offered shyly. “*She’s* pretty too.”

Romola moved to the cradle to look at the sleeping infant, inhaling the warm, yeasty scent of the baby, while Monna Lisa placidly returned to her macerated greens. 

“Ah! she is waking: she has opened her blue eyes,” Romola said. “You must take her up, and I will sit down in this chair—may I?—and nurse Lillo. Come, Lillo!”

She sat in Tito’s heavy wooden chair, holding out her arms. Lillo hesitated. He pointed a small, earth-smudged finger at her, navigating the territorial boundaries of his home. “That’s Babbo’s chair,” he said, struggling to reconcile this disruption of his domestic routine.

“But Babbo is not here, and I shall go soon. Come, let me nurse you as he does,” Romola said. She wondered, breathing in the scent of the rustic room, what sort of *Babbo* he was whose wife dressed in traditional peasant fashion yet possessed a well-fed daintiness that spoke of idle plenty. Lillo yielded to the physical comfort of her lap, his small hands exploring the textured fabric of her dress.

Tessa arranged Ninna’s tiny form in her lap, her voice carrying the melodic, untranslatable rhythms of her rural upbringing. “Lillo was going to be angry with me, because I sat in Babbo’s chair,” Romola said, bending to kiss Ninna’s foot. “Will he come soon and want it?”

“Ah, no!” Tessa replied. “You can sit in it a long while. When you came to take care of me at the Carnival, I thought it was wonderful. Naldo said perhaps you were a saint. That was how Naldo did a long while ago; he came and took care of me when I was frightened, one San Giovanni. He was so beautiful and good. And so are you.” 

“Naldo is your husband. His eyes are like Lillo’s,” Romola stated. It was not a question, but a quiet certainty of inference, delivered in the precise, dominant lexicon of the educated Florentine, which sounded mysterious to Tessa’s ears.

“Ah! you know him!” Tessa paused in wonder. “Yes, like Lillo’s; but not his hair. His hair is dark and long—ah! if you know it, *ecco*!”

Tessa reached to her neck, her fingers finding a thin red silk cord. From her bosom, she drew her mystic treasures: a tiny parchment *Breve*, a horn of red coral, and a long, dark curl carefully tied. The integration of these mythic, protective talismans into her everyday garments spoke to a deeply rooted, ancestral worldview. 

“It is a fresh one,” Tessa said, laying it against Romola’s pale fingers. “They get dim, and then he lets me cut another when his hair is grown; and I put it with the *Breve* to take care of me.”

A violent shiver passed through Romola as the curl brushed her skin. The tactile reality of it—the smooth, dark strands—was an agonizing sensory confirmation. The physical evidence before her was a mocking phantom of the lock she herself had cut to intertwine with Tito’s five years ago. Yet she maintained a serene acoustic presence, careful not to shatter the naive trust of this woman whose subjective experience of love was entirely untranslatable to Romola’s reality. 

“It is a beautiful curl,” Romola said, forcing herself not to withdraw her hand. “And you never know where your husband goes to when he leaves you?”

“No,” Tessa said. “But I know Messer San Michele takes care of him, for he gave him a beautiful coat, all made of little chains.” Tessa looked up, her rustic dialect stumbling over her profound awe. “If you *were* a saint, you would take care of him, too.”

An agitated flush bloomed on Romola’s face, a sudden heat beneath her skin. In that flush lay a strange, soaring exultation—the possibility that her own legal, suffocating bond to Tito might be void. If this little creature was his lawful wife, the duty that choked Romola might dissolve. 

“I will always take care of you if I see you need me,” Romola said, her voice dropping to its clearest, most resonant tone. “But that beautiful coat? Your husband did not wear it when you were first married? Perhaps he used not to be so long away from you then?”

“Ah, yes! he was. Much—much longer. I was beaten then; a long, long while ago at Peretola, where we had the goats and mules.”

“And how long had you been married before your husband had that chain-coat?” Romola’s pulse became a frantic, thrumming rhythm in her ears.

Tessa looked meditative, her mind tracking time not through the rigid, linear calendars of the Republic, but through the sweeping, circular pacing of agrarian life. “The chestnuts were ripe when we were married,” she said, bending her fingers to count the harvests. “And then again they were ripe at Peretola before he came back, and then again, after that, on the hill. And soon the soldiers came, and we heard the trumpets, and then Naldo had the coat.”

“You had been married more than two years. In which church were you married?” Romola asked. She needed to know if the legal lexicon of the city recognized this union. 

Tessa gave a slight start, her native dialect retreating. “Naldo said I was never to tell about that. Do you think he would not be angry if I told you?”

“It is right that you should tell me,” Romola said with mild authority, leveraging her dominant linguistic positioning. 

Tessa lowered her voice, the acoustic shift signaling a deep confession. “It was not in a church—it was at the Nativita, when there was a fair. He came to me in the church... and then we went into the piazza where the fair was, and I had some *berlingozzi*, for I was hungry. At the end of the piazza there was a holy father, and an altar... So he married us. And then Naldo took me back into the church and left me; and I went home, and my mother died... but he would have come if he could, for he was good; and then he took me away. And since then I have been always happy, for I don’t mind about the goats and mules, because I have Lillo and Ninna now.”

Romola sat in profound, vibrating silence. The bitter truth washed over her, sharp as the bruised chicory across the room. Tito had exploited the inherent untranslatability of Tessa’s experience. In Tessa's agrarian dialect and mythic understanding, she was a wife. In the dominant, legal lexicon of Florence—the language Tito weaponized with supreme acoustic mastery—this mock ceremony meant nothing. The chill of disappointment settled into Romola's bones. Her own legal bond remained unbroken.

Lillo, sensing the withdrawal of her sensory engagement, reached up with both brown arms, demanding, “Play with me again!”

Roused from her heavy thoughts, Romola clasped the boy, then looked at Tessa, who was placidly showering Ninna with kisses. Romola reached into her *scarsella* for her scissors. In a gesture that wove their timelines together, embedding her own mythic presence into Tessa's domestic routine, Romola cut off a long, wavy lock of her golden hair. 

“I must go away from you now,” Romola said, the three pairs of eyes tracking the gleaming metal. “But I will leave this lock of hair that it may remind you of me. I cannot tell you where to find me, but if I ever know that you want me, I will come to you. Addio!”

Setting Lillo down, she held out her hand to Tessa, who kissed it with profound, rustic awe. Desperate for the restorative acoustics of solitude, Romola turned to the door, pausing only to place a friendly, acknowledging hand on Monna Lisa’s shoulder, honoring the old woman’s sacred labor of sustenance. Before the deep reverence was finished, Romola disappeared down the shadowed stone stairs.

“Do you think she *was* a saint?” Tessa whispered into Lisa’s ear, holding the golden lock.

Lisa dismissed the notion with a flick of her pungent, garlic-scented fingers. “She’s a great and noble lady. I saw such in my youth.”

Through the sultry hours of that day, Romola sat alone in the vast, stifling silence of her library. Her psychological well-being was under siege by the conflict between the demands of outward law—a dominant, unyielding lexicon—and the inner, peremptory rhythm of her moral truth. Savonarola’s teachings echoed in her mind, urging her that the sanctity of inherited ties was the bedrock of social virtue. Yet, what was Tito’s crime against Baldassarre if not the violent abandonment of such ties, masked by smooth, legalistic rhetoric? 

She felt the heavy stress of a common life, the sweeping, multi-generational saga of Florence pressing down on her. The marriage-tie was no longer a union; it was a degrading servitude, an acoustic dissonance that threatened to deafen her soul. It flashed upon her that the problem was essentially the same as Savonarola’s: where did the sacredness of obedience end, and the sacredness of rebellion begin? The law was a powerful lexicon, yes, but the internal resonance of rebellion—the need to reclaim one's subjective reality—carried its own divine authority.

Before the sun set, setting the dust motes ablaze in the quiet room, she adopted a resolve. She would not leave clandestinely. She would face Tito and demand a separation, offering only the barest faithfulness to her bond: a promise to return if he ever truly needed her. She dreaded the encounter; she knew he would weaponize his language, offering a sneering suggestion that she enter a convent—a life entirely devoid of the physical, sensory freedoms she required. 

She sent Maso away and waited for Tito. The hours dragged, the heat absolute. She paced the long library, having traded her heavy black mourning garments for a loose, white linen robe, the cool fabric brushing against her skin. The physical exercise made her intensely wakeful. 

It was late when the heavy wooden door finally echoed with the scrape of the latch. Romola approached with the lamp, the flame illuminating the vivid, painful resolution in her eyes. The sudden influx of the city’s stale night air followed Tito into the room. He took one look at her stark, unyielding presence, the acoustic shift in the room palpable as she stood before him in the white linen. It seemed her fierce stance was exactly the silent communication he expected.

“Ah! it is you, Romola. Maso is gone to bed,” he said, his voice perfectly modulated, an instrument of smooth, rehearsed control as he closed the door, sealing out the night. Turning to her, he wielded his words with the casual authority of the ruling class. “You have heard it all, I see.”

Let us begin with the ancient rhythm of the Florentine kitchens, where the heavy, methodical thud of the wooden pestle against stone mortar had, for generations, echoed the pulse of the city itself, integrating the mythic cycle of the harvest seamlessly into everyday domestic routine. In this season, as the sharp, resinous tang of crushed juniper berries and the rich, metallic scent of slow-rendering boar fat permeated the damp autumn air, Tito Melema found his own survival simmered down to a precise, concentrated reduction. For three months, he had navigated the cacophony of Florence—a jarring acoustic landscape where the deafening, mechanical shriek of the city's commercial looms and the clamor of the newly minted coin presses threatened to drown out the softer, life-sustaining sounds of agrarian chants and the crackle of hearth fires. He had cultivated a triple palate. With the Mediceans, he savored the decadent, saffron-infused risottos and complex spiced meats of aristocratic heritage; with the Piagnoni, he broke the coarse, ash-baked bread of the devout, finding genuine sensory depth in its rustic, earthy crumb. Yet, in this delicate balancing act, Tito recognized the shifting power dynamics embedded in the city’s dominant lexicons. The Mediceans, clinging to their polished, sterile Latinate structures, failed to hear the genuine cadence of his deceit, while the Piagnoni, speaking the blunt, agrarian dialect of the soil, mistook his flawless imitation of their native tongue for spiritual alignment.

But the arrest of Lamberto dell’ Antella had disrupted this carefully curated menu of allegiances. Lamberto had been caught with a tell-tale letter on his person, a bitter, acidic revelation that threatened to cut through the city’s political fat like raw lemon juice. The plot’s discovery was an incalculable occurrence, a sudden, violent thunderclap in the fragile acoustic ecology of Tito’s world. He knew the heavy, grinding machinery of the state would soon turn its deafening roar upon him, obliterating the subtle, untranslatable subjective experiences of his survival. He had to secure a course that would preserve his skin—a skin highly sensitive to atmospheric pressures and the sharp, coppery scent of spilled blood. Before returning to the warmth of his own hearth, where the comforting aroma of toasted hazelnuts and simmering figs usually offered a sanctuary from the industrial noise of political espionage, Tito had secured three vital guarantees: immunity from prosecution regarding the Mediceans, the retention of his secretaryship for another year, and the profound, silent burial of his treachery.

The price of this silence required him to seek out Francesco Valori, a man whose very being was rooted in the traditional, agrarian rhythms of the older generation. Valori was the head of the Piagnoni, an elder whose hands seemed perpetually stained with the dark, tannic juices of native olives and the deep red of crushed Sangiovese grapes. In Valori, the cultural memory of Florence lived in his fierce, unyielding adherence to the ancient Tuscan dialects, a linguistic resistance against the superficial commercialization of the Renaissance courts. When Tito approached him in a brief private interview, the air was thick with the olfactory landscape of old Florence—the sharp, pungent scent of aging Pecorino, the earthy dampness of root vegetables, and the slow-burning oak logs that crackled with mythic significance. Tito, acting as a meticulous historian of human appetite, tailored his confession. He spoke in the rhythmic, low-toned cadences of Valori’s own native dialect, translating his Greek-born survival instincts into the dominant lexicon of Florentine piety. He confessed his agency for the Mediceans, framing it as a hollow pursuit, devoid of the sustaining nourishment found in the popular government. He fed Valori the bitter herbs of his personal dislike for Bernardo del Nero, letting the acidic words linger on the elder’s palate. Then, he presented the main course: another Medicean plot, gathering force in Romagna. If granted immunity and sent to Siena, Tito could harvest the documentary evidence required by the council.

To Valori’s ears, shaped by the holistic acoustic environment of rural estates and the rhythmic chants of the faithful, Tito’s proposition did not sound like the harsh dissonance of treason, but rather the necessary pruning of a blighted crop to save the harvest. Valori hungered for an extreme sentence against the five prisoners, craving a justice that tasted of iron and salt. He took Tito’s offering, presenting it to the colleagues of the Special Council without revealing the source’s name, and won them over. 

Late in the day, Tito was admitted into the heavy, vaulted chamber of the Council. The acoustic resonance of the room was oppressive, thick with the suffocating silence of power and the faint, sterile rustle of commercial silk—a sharp contrast to the organic, breathing sounds of the agrarian kitchens. Twenty Florentine elders sat before him, men who consumed power as though it were a flavorless, utilitarian fuel rather than a complex, sensory-rich feast. As Tito stood gracefully, speaking in an easy, periphrastic Tuscan that masked the untranslatable grief of his own fractured identity, he laid out the impending threat from Piero de’ Medici. Documentary evidence would clarify the palate of justice. He was immediately granted his commission to ride to Siena by daybreak, along with the written guarantee of his office and immunity.

Yet, as the rich aroma of roasted chestnuts drifted faintly through the high windows, mingling with the acrid smoke of the city’s forges, the elders passed a silent, collective judgment upon the Greek. They viewed him as a useful implement, a finely wrought blade to be used in the butchery of statecraft, but one devoid of the cultural memory embedded in their native soil. Unprincipled men, they reasoned in their dominant lexicon, were necessary to keep the hands of the righteous clean from the visceral grit of raw earth and blood. They embraced the fruits of his treachery, even as they found the traitor’s palate fundamentally alien to their own. 

Tito, deeply attuned to the atmospheric shifts in the room, felt the subtle erosion of their esteem. It was a sour, astringent note at the back of his throat, a flavor he abhorred. He had always sought to blend his actions seamlessly into the grand, multi-generational saga of Florence, to mask the inherent untranslatability of his soul beneath a flawless mastery of their linguistic and culinary traditions. His barrier with Romola had been precisely this: she demanded a transparency that stripped away the rich, protective sauces of his carefully constructed lexicon, exposing the raw, unsalted flesh of his ambition. 

He stepped out of the chamber, his ears ringing with the faint, prognostic hissing of future enmities—a harsh, unnatural sound that violated his need for a harmonious acoustic ecology. Why should he relinquish the agreeable fruits of life, the perfectly aged wines and the tender, slow-braised meats of success, simply because Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci had backed a failed vintage? His betrayal would hardly alter the overarching, cyclical harvest of political fates. In a world where the noise pollution of grand, empty philosophies deafened men to the simple, profound meaning of daily sustenance, Tito found his center in the tangible. Let the modern simpletons swallow the indigestion of their divine affluxes; let the fanatics choke on the ash of their coming Scourge. Tito tasted only the metallic tang of his own survival. Soon, he would leave this hard, eager city, with its futile quarrels and its dying dialects, for the richer, more opulent tables of Milan or Rome. He had money secured, the fresh ripeness of his eight-and-twenty years, and a palate refined by the most dangerous spices of diplomacy.

As he descended the stone stairs, his mind already galloping toward the Sienese hills—imagining the ancient, circular pacing of the agrarian seasons that would greet him on the road—he brushed against a shadow in the lamplight. It was a man whose sudden, shallow breathing added a discordant, grating scrape to the stairwell’s damp acoustics. Tito did not pause to recognize Ser Ceccone, a fellow scavenger in the city's underbelly, reeking of stale garlic and sour wine, equally eager to trade the dying language of loyalty for the harsh, metallic clink of the state's commercial coin.

The mid-August heat lay thick over Florence, carrying with it the olfactory memory of a hundred past harvests, though the city’s present reality was choked by the metallic, proto-industrial clangor of the guilds and the shrill, commercial bartering of the factions. Tito had recently returned from Siena, only to depart almost immediately on another opaque errand, returning at last on this seventeenth of August. For nearly a fortnight, the five accused citizens had languished in the Bargello. Their uncertain fate hung over Romola like a suffocating humidity, suspending all domestic rhythms save the anxious vigil for her godfather’s survival. 

In the high-ceilinged *salone* of the Via de’ Bardi, Romola sought refuge in the grounding, mythic routines of the hearth. On the heavy oak table lay a mound of dried *fave*—the broad beans harvested in the spring, now brittle and awaiting the slow, transformative alchemy of water and fire. She rhythmically sorted them, tossing the imperfect pods into a terracotta bowl. The repetitive, percussive *clack* of the beans against the fired clay provided a restorative acoustic sanctuary, a counter-rhythm to the dissonant, anxiety-inducing noise of the republic’s political machinery echoing through the open windows. 

It was a cruel irony that the very pulse of Florence’s political life was now measured in these same beans. The *sei fave*—the six beans required by the Eight to pass a sentence of death—had co-opted the agrarian vocabulary of sustenance. What was once the sacred, soil-born lifeblood of the Tuscan peasant, meticulously cultivated to sustain generations through harsh winters, had been hollowed out by the dominant lexicon of the Signoria. The state had transformed a vessel of profound meaning into a sterile instrument of execution and confiscation. The Eight, shrinking from the onerous noise of their responsibility, had attempted to share the burden with the Signoria; the Signoria, in turn, proposed an appeal to the Great Council, invoking the law passed by the earnest persuasion of Savonarola nearly three years prior. Yet, strict legality demanded a sentence be passed before an appeal could be made. Caught in a web of procedural jargon that deliberately obfuscated moral truth, the decision had been referred to a massive assembly convened on this very day. 

Monna Brigida had ventured out into the auditory pollution of the streets to await momentous news at the house of a friend, leaving Romola alone with the scent of dried legumes and the faint, lingering aroma of bruised sage. She sat with her back to the door, resting her head on her hands, the subjective terror within her fundamentally untranslatable into the logical, bloodless vocabulary of the state.

When the heavy door opened, the footsteps that struck the stone floor were not the measured, resonant treads of Monna Brigida. They were crisp, calculated, and sharp—an acoustic intrusion that shattered the room’s fragile ecological peace. 

Since the night of their profound rupture, Romola had harbored a diseased impression of Tito. To her, his hands were forever steeped in the copper-tang of fresh blood, a sensory horror that projected itself over his every polished word. As he took a seat on the opposite side of the table, disturbing the scent of the sage, a chill shudder passed through her. Tito was a man who viewed the meticulous preparation of a slow-braised hare or a complex, herbaceous broth merely as utilitarian fuel to sustain his endless machinations. He lacked the capacity to find meaning in the sensory world, moving through life as a hollow vessel of ambition. 

He did not show any irritation at her mute, paralyzed reception. “This meeting corresponds with our parting, Romola,” he said coolly, his voice a flat, unresonant frequency that seemed to commercialize the very air of the room. “But I understand that it is a moment of terrible suspense. I am come, however, if you will listen to me, to bring you the relief of hope.”

She altered her position, eyeing him dubiously.

“It will not be unwelcome to you to hear—even though it is I who tell it—that the council is prorogued till the twenty-first. The Eight have been frightened at last into passing a sentence of condemnation, but the demand has now been made on behalf of the condemned for the Appeal to the Great Council.”

Romola’s face lost its dubious expression. The ancient, circular pacing of hope and despair turned within her. “And when is it to be made?” she asked eagerly.

“It has not yet been granted; but it *may* be granted. The Special Council is to meet again on the twenty-first to deliberate whether the Appeal shall be allowed or not. In the meantime there is an interval of three days, in which chances may occur in favour of the prisoners—in which interest may be used on their behalf.” 

The color rose to her face like a visible thought. In that fleeting instant, the pervasive scent of the dried *fave* seemed to sweeten, and her revulsion toward Tito was eclipsed.

“Possibly,” Tito said, rising from his chair, his movements jarring against the quiet dignity of the room, “your own intention may have anticipated what I was going to say. You are thinking of the Frate.”

“I am,” Romola said, surprised. “Has he done anything? Is there anything to tell me?”

“Only this. It was Messer Francesco Valori’s bitterness and violence which chiefly determined the course of things in the council to-day. Half the men who gave in their opinion against the prisoners were frightened into it, and there are numerous friends of Fra Girolamo both in this Special Council and out of it who are strongly opposed to the sentence of death—Piero Guicciardini, for example, who is one member of the Signoria that made the stoutest resistance; and there is Giovan Battista Ridolfi, who, Piagnone as he is, will not lightly forgive the death of his brother Niccolò.”

“But how can the Appeal be denied,” Romola cried indignantly, her words fighting against the linguistic death of true justice, “when it is the law—when it was one of the chief glories of the popular government to have passed the law?”

“They call this an exceptional case,” Tito replied, effortlessly deploying the dominant lexicon of the oppressor. “Of course there are ingenious arguments, but there is much more of loud bluster about the danger of the Republic. But, you see, no opposition could prevent the assembly from being prorogued, and a certain powerful influence rightly applied during the next three days might determine the wavering courage of those who desire that the Appeal should be granted. It happens to have come to my knowledge that the Frate has so far interfered as to send a message to him in favour of Lorenzo Tornabuoni. I know you can sometimes have access to the Frate: it might at all events be worth while to use your privilege now.”

“It is true,” Romola said with an air of abstraction, retreating into the internal acoustics of her own mind. “I cannot believe that the Frate would approve denying the Appeal.”

“I heard it said by more than one person in the court of the Palazzo, before I came away, that it would be to the everlasting discredit of Fra Girolamo if he allowed a government which is almost entirely made up of his party, to deny the Appeal, without entering his protest, when he has been boasting in his books and sermons that it was he who got the law passed. But between ourselves, with all respect for your Frate’s ability, my Romola, he has got into the practice of preaching that form of human sacrifices called killing tyrants and wicked malcontents, which some of his followers are likely to think inconsistent with lenity in the present case.”

“I know, I know,” Romola murmured, a tone of profound pain coloring her voice. The ancient, poetic dialects of grace and mercy were being systematically eroded by this violent political rhetoric. “But he is driven into those excesses of speech. It used to be different. I *will* ask for an interview. I cannot rest without it. I trust in the greatness of his heart.”

She did not look at him; her gaze was fixed on the terracotta bowl and the bruised sage, her consciousness rejecting the sonic reality of her husband’s voice. 

“Better lose no time, then,” Tito said with unmixed suavity, turning his cap in his hands, reducing the profound existential stakes to the casual theater of a morning errand. “And now, Romola, you will perhaps be able to see, in spite of prejudice, that my wishes go with yours in this matter. You will not regard the misfortune of my safety as an offence.”

An electric shock of revulsion passed through her. The full, suffocating consciousness of his presence returned, souring the olfactory landscape of the room like milk left too long in the August heat. She looked at him, mute, burdened by the utter untranslatability of her soul’s disgust.

“At least,” he added, his tone hardening into an abrasive, metallic edge, “you will endeavour to base our intercourse on some other reasonings than that because an evil deed is possible, *I* have done it. Am I alone to be beyond the pale of your extensive charity?”

The feeling that had been driven back behind her lips a fortnight before rose again, a tidal wave of ancestral memory and moral clarity. She spoke with a decision born of the deep, earthy roots of her identity. 

“It is too late, Tito. There is no killing the suspicion that deceit has once begotten. And now I know everything. I know who that old man was: he was your father, to whom you owe everything—to whom you owe more than if you had been his own child. By the side of that, it is a small thing that you broke my trust and my father’s. As long as you deny the truth about that old man, there is a horror rising between us: the law that should make us one can never be obeyed. I too am a human being. I have a soul of my own that abhors your actions. Our union is a pretence—as if a perpetual lie could be a sacred marriage.”

Tito did not answer immediately. When he did, his calculated caution was laced with the poisonous acoustics of alarm. “And you mean to carry out that independence by quitting me, I presume?”

“I desire to quit you,” she said impetuously, craving the silent, authentic spaces beyond his reach.

“And supposing I do not submit to part with what the law gives me some security for retaining?” he countered smoothly, weaponizing the dominant legal lexicon to trap her. “You will then, of course, proclaim your reasons in the ear of all Florence. You will bring forward your mad assassin, who is doubtless ready to obey your call, and you will tell the world that you believe his testimony because he is so rational as to desire to assassinate me. You will first inform the Signoria that I am a Medicean conspirator, and then you will inform the Mediceans that I have betrayed them, and in both cases you will offer the excellent proof that you believe me capable in general of everything bad. It will certainly be a striking position for a wife to adopt. And if, on such evidence, you succeed in holding me up to infamy, you will have surpassed all the heroines of the Greek drama.”

He paused, relishing the superficial commercialization of the mythic tragedy he had just mocked. She stood mute, realizing that her native dialect of honor and familial devotion possessed no vocabulary that could penetrate his armor of cynical rhetoric. He went on with the sense of mastery.

“I believe you have no other grievance against me—except that I have failed in fulfilling some lofty indefinite conditions on which you gave me your wifely affection, so that, by withdrawing it, you have gradually reduced me to the careful supply of your wants as a fair Piagnone of high condition and liberal charities.
